# 📚 Pico 2500: Documentation (Projects 1201-1300)

## Batch 121: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1201: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Hidden Install". At the end of this project, you will be able to analyze the magnetic permeability of different materials by measuring the effective detection range of a reed switch through various barriers (wood, plastic, metal).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Magnetic Flux**: The movement of magnetic field lines through space or materials.
*   **Magnetic Permeability**: How easily a material allows magnetic flux to pass through.
*   **Ferrous vs. Non-Ferrous**: Ferrous materials (containing iron) can "shunt" or block magnetic fields.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**
*   **Material Samples** (Wood, Plastic, Aluminum, Steel sheets)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch (Side A)** | GP15 | Connect to internal Pull-Up |
| **Reed Switch (Side B)** | GND | Ground return |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_init`** (set GP15 as Input_PullUp)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (read GP15)
*   **from Text, drag `pico_print`** (print results)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_triggered**: Boolean value storing the current sensor state.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Configure Pin**: From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 to **Input** with **Pull-Up**.

**B. Control Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Read State**:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `is_triggered` = From **Smart IO**, **pico_gpio_read** GP15.
4.  **Reporting**:
    *   From **Logic**, if `is_triggered` == 0:
        *   From **Text**, **Print** "Contact Detected".
    *   From **Logic**, else:
        *   From **Text**, **Print** "No Contact".
5.  **Test**: Place wood, then steel, between the magnet and switch. Note if the "Contact Detected" range changes.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: The Pico sets GP15 as an input with an internal pull-up resistor.
2.  **Monitoring**: The code rapidly polls the pin state.
3.  **Trigger**: When a magnet gets close, the internal metal reeds touch, pulling GP15 to 0V.
4.  **Feedback**: The Terminal shows "Contact Detected" instantly.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# GP15 setup with internal Pull-Up
reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    # 0 = Sw closed (Magnet present), 1 = Sw open
    if reed.value() == 0:
        print("STATUS: Contact Made (Magnet Detected)")
    else:
        print("STATUS: Open Circuit")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ferrous Housing**: If you mount the switch inside a steel box, the flux might travel through the box instead of the switch, preventing a trigger.
*   **Floating Pin**: Forgetting the `PULL_UP` will cause the input to act like an antenna, picking up static noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Neodymium Upgrade**: Use a high-power magnet to see if you can trigger the switch through a thick tabletop.

## 1. Project 1202: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"State Duration Timer". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement non-blocking chronometer logic to track exactly how long a door remains open using a Reed Switch and an OLED display.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Accumulation**: Calculating time by comparing timestamps.
*   **Event Transitions**: Detecting the "Rising Edge" (the moment a door opens).
*   **String Formatting**: Converting raw milliseconds into MM:SS format.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **OLED Display (I2C)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL** | GP4/GP5 | I2C0 Bus |
| **Reed Switch** | GP15 | GND connected |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_ms`** (get current clock)
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_diff`** (calculate delta)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `oled_show_text`** (display timer)

### 7. Variables
*   **start_ms**: Timestamp when the door was first opened.
*   **is_counting**: Boolean flag tracking if the timer is active.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Setup OLED**: From **Smart Display**, **Init** OLED (SDA: GP4, SCL: GP5).
2.  **Setup Sensor**: From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 to **Input** with **Pull-Up**.

**B. Logic Loop**
3.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
4.  **Edge Detection**:
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` GP15 == 1 (Door Open):
        *   From **Logic**, if `is_counting` == false:
            *   From **Variables**, **Set** `start_ms` = From **Time**, **pico_ticks_ms**.
            *   From **Variables**, **Set** `is_counting` = true.
5.  **Calculations**:
    *   From **Logic**, if `is_counting` == true:
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `elapsed` = From **Time**, **pico_ticks_diff**.
        *   From **Smart Display**, **Show Text** "Time: " + `elapsed`.
6.  **Reset**:
    *   From **Logic**, else (Door Closed):
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `is_counting` = false.
        *   From **Smart Display**, **Clear Screen**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Closed**: Screen is blank; Pico waits for GP15 to go HIGH.
2.  **Open**: GP15 goes HIGH; Pico captures the start time and sets `is_counting` to TRUE.
3.  **Update**: Every Loop, the screen updates with a live counter: "Door Open: 00h 01m 02s".
4.  **Close**: Timer stops and screen clears.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

start_time = 0
counting = False

while True:
    if reed.value() == 1: # Open
        if not counting:
            start_time = utime.ticks_ms()
            counting = True
        
        diff = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start_time) // 1000
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text(f"OPEN: {diff} sec", 0, 30)
        oled.show()
    else:
        counting = False
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Overflow**: Don't use standard math for time; always use `ticks_diff` to handle counter rollover after 65 days.
*   **Screen Refresh**: Updating the OLED 1000 times a second will cause flicker. The 100ms sleep is essential.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Buzzer Alert**: Play an alarm tone if the door stays open for more than 30 seconds.

## 1. Project 1203: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Sequential Access Lock". At the end of this project, you will be able to design a sequential state machine where three distinct magnetic sensors must be triggered in a specific order (A -> B -> C) to activate a servo motor.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Machine Design**: Tracking progress through a multi-step sequence.
*   **Input Sequencing**: Differentiating between chronological order and simultaneous states.
*   **Latching Stages**: Using variables to "lock in" progress.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x Reed Switches**
*   **Servo Motor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed A** | GP13 | Switch 1 |
| **Reed B** | GP14 | Switch 2 |
| **Reed C** | GP15 | Switch 3 |
| **Servo** | GP16 | Digital Lock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if_elseif`** (multi-stage check)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (unlock)
*   **from Variables, drag `set_initial_value`** (initialize stage)

### 7. Variables
*   **lock_stage**: Integer tracking progress (0-3).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Configure Latch**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servo** GP16 to **0** (Locked).
2.  **Reset Logic**: From **Variables**, **Set** `lock_stage` to **0**.

**B. Sequence Logic**
3.  **Step 1**: From **Logic**, if `lock_stage` == 0 AND **Smart IO** Read GP13 == 0:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `lock_stage` to **1**.
4.  **Step 2**: From **Logic**, else if `lock_stage` == 1 AND **Smart IO** Read GP14 == 0:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `lock_stage` to **2**.
5.  **Step 3**: From **Logic**, else if `lock_stage` == 2 AND **Smart IO** Read GP15 == 0:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `lock_stage` to **3**.

**C. Unlock Action**
6.  **Activate**: From **Logic**, if `lock_stage` == 3:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servo** GP16 to **90** (Unlocked).
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 5s.
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servo** GP16 to **0**.
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `lock_stage` to **0**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Pico is in Stage 0. Swipe Magnet over B or C does nothing.
2.  **Start**: Swipe magnet over A. Stage becomes 1.
3.  **Progress**: Swipe over B then C in order.
4.  **Terminal State**: After C, the servo rotates 90 degrees to unlock the cabinet.
5.  **Timeout**: After 5s, the system re-locks and resets the sequence.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
swA = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
swB = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
swC = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)

stage = 0

while True:
    if stage == 0 and swA.value() == 0:
        stage = 1
        print("Stage 1 OK")
    elif stage == 1 and swB.value() == 0:
        stage = 2
        print("Stage 2 OK")
    elif stage == 2 and swC.value() == 0:
        print("UNLOCKED!")
        # Servo unlock code
        stage = 0 # Reset
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Magnet "Bleed"**: If the switches are too close, a single magnet might trigger both A and B at once. Use smaller magnets or space sensors 10cm apart.
*   **Sequence Skip**: Forgetting the `elif` will cause the stages to run all at once if conditions overlap.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Fail Delay**: If the wrong switch is triggered, block all inputs for 10 seconds as a penalty.

## 1. Project 1204: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dynamic Zone Dashboard". At the end of this project, you will be able to manage a list of active alerts and implement a scrolling user interface on an OLED that cycles through multiple "Open Door" zones without freezing the main sensing loop.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic Lists**: Building and clearing lists in every loop iteration.
*   **UI Scrolling Logic**: Cycling an index based on time.
*   **Zone Mapping**: Associating GPIO pins with human-readable names.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x Reed Switches** (Zones 1-4)
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Zones 1-4** | GP12, 13, 14, 15 | Switch Inputs |
| **OLED (I2C)** | GP4/GP5 | Standard Bus |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `create_empty_list`** (active_zones)
*   **from Lists, drag `add_item`** (append name)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `oled_show_text`** (scrolling view)

### 7. Variables
*   **active_zones**: List of string names for open pins.
*   **scroll_idx**: Pointer for the currently displayed zone.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Scanning Phase**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Clear List**: From **Variables**, **Set** `active_zones` to **Lists** `create_empty`.
3.  **Read Inputs**:
    *   From **Logic**, if GP12 is HIGH, From **Lists**, **Add** "FRONT DOOR" to `active_zones`.
    *   From **Logic**, if GP13 is HIGH, From **Lists**, **Add** "BACK DOOR" to `active_zones`.
    *   (Repeat for all 4 zones).

**B. Display Phase**
4.  **Timer Loop**: From **Logic**, if **Time** `ticks_diff` > 2000ms:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `scroll_idx` = (`scroll_idx` + 1) MOD **Lists** `length_of` `active_zones`.
5.  **Output**:
    *   From **Logic**, if `active_zones` is NOT empty:
        *   From **Smart Display**, **Show Text** `active_zones` at `scroll_idx`.
    *   From **Logic**, else:
        *   From **Smart Display**, **Show Text** "STATUS: SECURE".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: All doors closed. OLED shows "STATUS: SECURE".
2.  **Breach**: Front door and Window are opened. `active_zones` = ["Front", "Window"].
3.  **Scroll**: OLED shows "Front" for 2 seconds, then "Window" for 2 seconds.
4.  **Loop**: The scan continues in the background, ensuring if a third door opens, it is added to the scroll list immediately.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
zones = [(12, "FRONT"), (13, "BACK"), (14, "GARAGE"), (15, "GATE")]
pins = [machine.Pin(p, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP) for p, n in zones]

idx = 0
last_move = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    active = [zones[i][1] for i in range(4) if pins[i].value() == 1]
    
    if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_move) > 2000:
        if active: idx = (idx + 1) % len(active)
        last_move = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    oled.fill(0)
    if not active:
        oled.text("SECURE", 40, 30)
    else:
        oled.text("ALERT:", 0, 10)
        oled.text(active[idx], 20, 35)
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Index Error**: If you unplug a switch while the list is scrolling, `idx` might point to a value that no longer exists. The `% len(active)` math prevents this crash.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Web Server**: Post the list of names to a local webpage so you can check your house status from your phone.

## 1. Project 1205: Automated Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Pattern Recognition". At the end of this project, you will be able to distinguish between a single tap and a timed "Double Tap" within a 1-second window to create a secret magnetic unlock gesture.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Event Frequency**: Measuring occurrences per time unit.
*   **Gesture Timeout**: Using expiration timers to invalidate incomplete patterns.
*   **Software Debouncing**: Filtering mechanical noise in high-speed sensing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP15 | Hidden Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Success Beep |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_ms`** (event timing)
*   **from Variables, drag `increment_variable`** (tap count)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_expire`** (pattern reset)

### 7. Variables
*   **tap_count**: How many times the magnet swiped past.
*   **last_tap**: Timestamp of the previous swipe.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Sensing**
1.  **Monitor**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Detection**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP15 == 0 (Tap detected):
    *   From **Variables**, **Change** `tap_count` by **1**.
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `last_tap` = **Time** `pico_ticks_ms`.
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 0.3s (Debounce).

**B. Validation**
3.  **Pattern Check**: From **Logic**, if `tap_count` == 2:
    *   From **Text**, **Print** "ACCESS GRANTED".
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP16 HIGH (Buzzer).
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `tap_count` to **0**.
4.  **Timeout**: From **Logic**, if (`pico_ticks_ms` - `last_tap`) > 1000ms:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `tap_count` to **0** (Pattern expired).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Tap 1**: User swipes magnet. Pico records time and sets `tap_count` to 1.
2.  **Window**: The system starts an internal countdown (1 second).
3.  **Tap 2**: User swipes again quickly. Pico sees `tap_count` is now 2 and triggers the unlock.
4.  **Slow Tap**: If the user waits 2 seconds between swipes, the system resets to 0 before the second tap occurs.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

taps = 0
last_time = 0

while True:
    now = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    if reed.value() == 0:
        taps += 1
        last_time = now
        utime.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
        
    if taps == 2:
        print("UNLOCKED")
        buzzer.value(1); utime.sleep(1); buzzer.value(0)
        taps = 0
        
    if taps > 0 and utime.ticks_diff(now, last_time) > 1000:
        taps = 0
        print("Timeout Reset")
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Infinite Taps**: If you swipe too fast without a debounce delay, a single magnet pulse might look like 5 taps. 300ms is the "Goldilocks" value for human interaction.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Secret Rhythm**: Require a specific pattern like (Tap ... Tap-Tap) to unlock.

---

## 1. Project 1206: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Industrial Interlock". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial safety interlock where a dangerous component (Motor) is software-locked until a physical safety condition (Guard closed) is met and a manual reset button is pressed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fail-Safe Interlocking**: System design where failure of a sensor/state results in a safe condition.
*   **Software Latching**: Maintaining an "Alarm" state until a secondary manual event clears it.
*   **Safety Overrides**: Prioritizing safety sensor logic over user power commands.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch** (Guard Sensor)
*   **DC Motor / L9110 Driver**
*   **Push Button** (Manual Reset)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Guard Position |
| **Reset Button** | GP15 | Normal Open |
| **Motor (IA/IB)** | GP16/GP17 | Driver Inputs |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_motor_speed`** (control motor)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (check guard/reset)
*   **from Variables, drag `set_boolean`** (tripped flag)

### 7. Variables
*   **safety_tripped**: Boolean tracking if the system is currently locked for safety.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring Phase**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Sense Breach**:
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** `pico_gpio_read` GP14 == 1 (Guard Removed):
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `safety_tripped` = true.
        *   From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motor** GP16/17 speed to **0**.

**B. Recovery Phase**
3.  **Latch Logic**: From **Logic**, if `safety_tripped` == true:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motor** to **0**.
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP14 == 0 (Guard On) AND Read GP15 == 0 (Reset Pressed):
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `safety_tripped` = false.

**C. Drive Phase**
4.  **Operational**: From **Logic**, else if `safety_tripped` == false:
    *   From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motor** to **50** (Normal speed).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Running**: Guard is closed. Motor spins at 50% power.
2.  **Stop**: User opens the guard. Motor stops immediately; `safety_tripped` becomes TRUE.
3.  **Blocked**: User closes the guard. Motor stays at 0 (Latching safety).
4.  **Reset**: User presses the Manual Reset button. System validates the guard is closed and clears the alarm. Motor resumes.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
guard = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
reset = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
mot_a = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
mot_b = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

tripped = False

while True:
    if guard.value() == 1: # Guard open
        tripped = True
        
    if tripped:
        # Emergency STOP
        mot_a.low(); mot_b.low()
        if guard.value() == 0 and reset.value() == 0:
            tripped = False
    else:
        # Standard Drive (Forward)
        mot_a.high(); mot_b.low()
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Auto-Restart**: Engineering code must NEVER restart a motor automatically when a safety condition is cleared without a physical manual interaction.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Warning Siren**: Pulse a buzzer while the guard is missing to alert neighbors.

---

## 1. Project 1207: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Hourly Traffic Bucketing". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an event counter that sorts physical sensor triggers into 24 distinct "Hourly Buckets" using internal RTC logic for retail analytics.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Array Mapping**: Using time values as indices for data storage.
*   **Edge Detection**: Counting a state change only once (from open to closed).
*   **Simulated RTC**: Managing 24-hour cycles within MicroPython.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch** (Entry Sensor)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP15 | Connect to GND |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `set_list_item_at_index`** (increment bucket)
*   **from System & Storage, drag `pico_get_hour`** (index finder)
*   **from Variables, drag `pico_state_memory`** (prev_state)

### 7. Variables
*   **traffic_log**: A list of 24 numbers (Hourly counts).
*   **prev_val**: State memory for edge detection.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Setup Clock**: From **System & Storage**, **Init RTC** to current time.
2.  **Setup Log**: From **Variables**, **Set** `traffic_log` to **Lists** `create_list_length` 24 (all 0).

**B. Event Detection**
3.  **Detect Edge**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
4.  **Logic**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP15 == 0 AND `prev_val` == 1:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `h_idx` = From **System & Storage**, **Get Hour**.
    *   From **Lists**, **Change Item** at `h_idx` in `traffic_log` by **1**.
5.  **Memory**: From **Variables**, **Set** `prev_val` = Read GP15.

**C. Reporting**
6.  **Report**: Every hour, From **Text**, **Print** "Current Daily Log: " + `traffic_log`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **AM**: At 9:05 AM, a customer enters. `h_idx` = 9. `traffic_log[9]` increases from 0 to 1.
2.  **Noon**: 5 entries at 12 PM. `traffic_log[12]` becomes 5.
3.  **24h Cycle**: The system resets or cycles back to 0 at midnight, providing a daily footprint of door activity.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

rtc = machine.RTC()
# Set to 1:00 PM for test
rtc.datetime((2023, 1, 1, 0, 13, 0, 0, 0))

reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
counts = [0] * 24
last_val = 1

while True:
    now_val = reed.value()
    if now_val == 0 and last_val == 1:
        hour = rtc.datetime()[4]
        counts[hour] += 1
        print(f"Update: Hour {hour} has {counts[hour]} visitors.")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        
    last_val = now_val
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Double Counts**: Without the `last_val` comparison, a door left open for 2 seconds would count as 20 distinct entry events.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **SD Backup**: Save the list to a file every hour so data is not lost if the Pico is unplugged.

---

## 1. Project 1208: Smart Advanced Reed Switch Alarm System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Mailbox Alert (UART Signaling)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement remote signaling by transmitting structured metadata packets over a Serial (UART) bus whenever a physical flap sensor is triggered.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Asynchronous Communication**: Sending data without a shared clock line.
*   **Packet Construction**: Formatting raw text as meaningful packets (e.g., `[MAIL_IN]`).
*   **Transmission Efficiency**: Ensuring messages are sent only on meaningful state changes.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **UART Display or PC Interface**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP15 | Flap Sensor |
| **UART TX** | GP0 | Data Out |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Comm, drag `uart_setup`** (9600 baud)
*   **from Comm, drag `uart_send_text`** (transmit alert)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (state change check)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_open**: Current flap status.
*   **msg_sent**: Flag to prevent spamming the serial line.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Serial Setup**
1.  **Configure**: From **Comm**, **Init UART0** (TX: GP0, Speed: 9600).

**B. Transmission Logic**
2.  **Sense**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Alert Logic**:
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP15 == 1 (Flap Open):
        *   From **Logic**, if `msg_sent` == false:
            *   From **Comm**, **Send UART** "ID:MAILBOX_EVENT:STATUS:OPEN\n".
            *   From **Variables**, **Set** `msg_sent` = true.
4.  **Reset Logic**:
    *   From **Logic**, else:
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `msg_sent` = false.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Wait**: Mailbox is closed. Serial line is idle.
2.  **Drop**: Mail carrier opens the flap. Pico detects HIGH on GP15.
3.  **Blast**: "ID:MAILBOX_EVENT..." is sent once via GP0.
4.  **Reset**: Flap closes. The system is ready for the next delivery.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))
reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
sent = False

while True:
    if reed.value() == 1:
        if not sent:
            uart.write("EVENT:MAIL_IN\n")
            sent = True
    else:
        sent = False
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Baud Rate Gap**: Ensure your receiver (PC or second Pico) is set to 9600. Different speeds will result in "garbled text" or nothing at all.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Two-Node Mesh**: Build a second Pico receiver that lights up an LED when it receives the "MAIL_IN" string.

## 1. Project 1209: Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Hysteresis Plotting". At the end of this project, you will be able to quantify the "Magnetic Dead Zone" by mapping the precise millimeter gap between activation and deactivation points of a reed switch.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hysteresis Curve**: The difference between the point where a sensor turns ON and where it turns OFF.
*   **Sensitivity Mapping**: Understanding field strength vs. distance.
*   **Diagnostic Mode**: Designing code specifically for hardware debugging.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Neodymium Magnet**
*   **Plastic Ruler**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP15 | Test sensor |
| **Onboard LED** | GP25 | Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_led`** (match sensor)
*   **from Text, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (log state)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Direct real-time feedback is used for this tool.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Tool Setup**
1.  **Indicator Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Map LED**: From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP25 = **NOT** From **Smart IO**, Read GP15. (LED lights when magnet is present).

**B. Data Collection**
3.  **Actuation Measurement**:
    *   Start magnet far away. Move slowly toward switch.
    *   Stop exactly when LED turns ON. Record Ruler mm.
4.  **Release Measurement**:
    *   Move magnet away slowly from zero.
    *   Stop exactly when LED turns OFF. Record Ruler mm.
5.  **Calculate**: Hysteresis = Release mm - Actuation mm.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Test**: Magnet at 15mm (LED OFF).
2.  **Event**: Magnet at 10mm (LED ON!). Actuation = 10mm.
3.  **Test**: Magnet moving away. 11mm (ON), 12mm (ON), 13mm (OFF!). Release = 13mm.
4.  **Result**: Hysteresis is 3mm. This helps you design door gaps that don't "flicker".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

reed = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
led = machine.Pin(25, machine.Pin.OUT) # Onboard LED

while True:
    # Set LED to the inverse of the pin (Active Low)
    is_on = (reed.value() == 0)
    led.value(is_on)
    
    if is_on: print("IN RANGE")
    else: print("OUT OF RANGE")
    
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Magnetic Drag**: Do not use a metal ruler; it will distort the field and invalidate your measurements. Use plastic or wood only.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Side-Approach Test**: Repeat the test approaching from the side of the switch vs. the front. The results will be drastically different!

## 1. Project 1210: Advanced Advanced Reed Switch Alarm Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"USB HID Keyboard Emulation". At the end of this project, you will be able to transform a magnetic reed switch into a physical USB keystroke injector that sends "SPACE" or "ESC" to a computer when a magnet is swiped.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **USB Device Class (HID)**: How Microcontrollers "pretend" to be keyboards or mice.
*   **Keystroke Buffering**: Managing the difference between a "Key Down" and "Key Up" event.
*   **Active-High Emulation**: Mapping physical magnetic closure to logical key commands.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Reed Switch**
*   **Magnet**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Reed Switch** | GP14 | Key Switch |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from USB, drag `usb_keyboard_send`** (send key)
*   **from Logic, drag `wait_until_open`** (release check)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Real-time state management used.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. HID Setup**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Input Pulse**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP14 == 0 (Magnet present):
    *   From **USB**, **Press Key** "SPACE".
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 0.1s.
    *   From **USB**, **Release Keys**.

**B. Debounce & Repeat Prevention**
3.  **Safety Buffer**: From **Logic**, while **Smart IO** Read GP14 == 0:
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 0.01s. (Wait for user to move magnet away before allowing next key).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Usage**: You open a text document on your laptop.
2.  **Action**: You swipe a magnet past the reed switch on your Pico.
3.  **Result**: A space character appears on your screen as if you hit the keyboard.
4.  **Repeat**: If you hold the magnet there, the code blocks and does NOT send 1000 spaces. It waits for you to clear the sensor.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
# Requires CircuitPython or specialized HID MicroPython build
# import usb_hid

reed = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    if reed.value() == 0:
        print("[USB HID] SENDING: SPACE")
        # In HID firmware: k.send(Keycode.SPACE)
        
        # BLOCK until released (Prevents repetition)
        while reed.value() == 0:
            utime.sleep(0.01)
            
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **USB Mode**: Standard MicroPython doesn't support HID by default. You must flash the "Pico HID" firmware or use CircuitPython for the computer to recognize the Pico as a keyboard.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Secret ESC**: Mount the switch under your desk. Swipe a magnet to instantly "Alt-Tab" or "Minimize All" when someone enters the room!

## Batch 122: Advanced Tilt Sensor 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1211: Advanced Tilt Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Noisy Switch". At the end of this project, you will be able to analyze the mechanical properties of a ball-tilt switch by implementing high-speed polling to count contact bounces during a single orientation change.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Mechanical Bounce**: The phenomenon where metal contacts vibrate and produce multiple high/low signals before settling.
*   **Signal Noise Analysis**: Using code to quantify the "cleanliness" of a physical sensor.
*   **High-Speed Polling**: Investigating events that occur in the microsecond to millisecond range.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Ball-Tilt Switch** (Standard SW-520D or similar)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch (Lead A)** | GP16 | Signal Input |
| **Tilt Switch (Lead B)** | GND | Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`ticks_ms`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_digital`**
*   From **Comm**, drag **`print`**

### 7. Variables
*   `bounce_count`: Counter for the number of transitions detected.
*   `last_state`: Stores the previous reading for comparison.
*   `test_start`: Timestamp of when the 100ms window began.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Configure Input**:
    *   Initialize **GP16** as an input with an internal **PULL_UP** resistor.
2.  **Wait for Trigger**:
    *   Create a loop that does nothing until the **Tilt Switch** state changes from its idle value.
3.  **Start Analysis Window**:
    *   Once a change is detected, set `test_start` to **`ticks_ms`**.
    *   Set `bounce_count` to **0**.
4.  **High-Speed Count**:
    *   Run a `while` loop for exactly **100 milliseconds** (`ticks_ms` - `test_start < 100`).
    *   Inside this loop, read the pin value constantly.
    *   Each time the value is different from `last_state`, increment `bounce_count`.
5.  **Output Results**:
    *   Print the final `bounce_count` to the terminal. You will be surprised to see 10-50 "taps" for a single tilt!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Ready**: Pico waits for you to move the breadboard.
2.  **Tilt**: As the ball rolls, it rattles. The Pico captures every tiny vibration.
3.  **Report**: "Transition complete. Total Bounces: 38".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Setup tilt switch on GP16
tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("--- Tilt Switch Noise Analyzer ---")
print("Waiting for movement...")

while True:
    # 1. Wait for the initial transition
    start_val = tilt.value()
    while tilt.value() == start_val:
        pass # Wait for change
    
    # 2. Windowed analysis (100ms)
    count = 0
    last_state = tilt.value()
    start_time = utime.ticks_ms()
    
    while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start_time) < 100:
        current = tilt.value()
        if current != last_state:
            count += 1
            last_state = current
            
    # 3. Report
    print(f"Movement Detected! Switch bounced {count} times in 100ms.")
    utime.sleep(1) # Wait before next test
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Interrupt Overhead**: Using IRQs for this test might actually miss bounces if they happen faster than the Pico can handle the interrupt context switch. Polling is better for raw noise analysis.
*   **Slow Code**: Including `print()` statements inside the 100ms counting loop will significantly slow it down, causing you to miss 90% of the bounces.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hardware Filtering**: Add a 100nF capacitor across the switch leads (GP16 to GND). Run the code again. The bounce count should drop to nearly zero.

    *   Place Switch 1 on your breadboard leaning 45 degrees to the left.

## 1. Project 1211: Advanced Tilt Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Mechanical Noise Profiler". At the end of this project, you will be able to analyze the mechanical properties of a ball-tilt switch by implementing high-speed polling to count contact bounces during a single orientation change.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Mechanical Bounce**: The phenomenon where metal contacts vibrate and produce multiple high/low signals before settling.
*   **Data Resolution**: capturing events occurring in the microsecond range using low-latency loops.
*   **Noise Qualification**: Distinguishing between intentional state changes and mechanical interference.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Ball-Tilt Switch** (SW-520D)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch (A)** | GP16 | Polling Input |
| **Tilt Switch (B)** | GND | Common Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_ms`** (window timing)
*   **from Variables, drag `increment_variable`** (bounce count)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (high-speed poll)

### 7. Variables
*   **bounce_count**: Total number of fluctuations detected.
*   **start_time**: Reference for the 500ms analysis window.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Trigger Phase**
1.  **Wait for Change**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_wait_until` GP16 != `prev_state`.
2.  **Start Window**: From **Variables**, **Set** `start_time` = From **Time**, **pico_ticks_ms**.

**B. Analysis Phase**
3.  **Polling Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_repeat_while` (**Time** `ticks_diff` < 500):
    *   From **Logic**, if GP16 value != `last_poll`:
        *   From **Variables**, **Change** `bounce_count` by **1**.
4.  **Reporting**: From **Text**, **Print** "Bounces detected: " + `bounce_count`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Pico waits for the ball to move.
2.  **Event**: You tilt the sensor. The first contact triggers the recording window.
3.  **Capture**: For the next 500ms, every single vibration of the ball against the pins is counted.
4.  **Result**: The terminal displays the "Noise Profile" (e.g., 42 bounces).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
last_val = tilt.value()

print("Tilt Sensor Profiler Active. Move the sensor now...")

while True:
    current = tilt.value()
    if current != last_val:
        # Event detected! Start high-speed count
        bounces = 0
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 500:
            poll = tilt.value()
            if poll != current:
                bounces += 1
                current = poll
        
        print(f"TRIGGER DETECTED: {bounces} mechanical bounces in 500ms.")
        last_val = current
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Slow Polling**: Adding a `sleep(0.01)` inside the analysis loop will cause you to miss 90% of the bounces. The inner loop must be "tight".

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Vibration Grade**: Classify the tilt as "Smooth" or "Rough" based on the bounce count.

---

## 1. Project 1212: Advanced Tilt Sensor Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"4-Axis Orientation Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement multi-sensor coordinate mapping by combining two ball-tilt switches into a 4-state logic matrix (Up, Down, Left, Right).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Binary Combinatorics**: Using 2 digital inputs to represent 4 unique system states (00, 01, 10, 11).
*   **Dead-Reckoning Orientation**: Determining position without an expensive IMU/gyroscope.
*   **State Enums**: Mapping numeric combinations to human-readable text.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Tilt Switches** (Mounted at 90-degree offsets)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch X** | GP16 | X-Axis |
| **Tilt Switch Y** | GP17 | Y-Axis |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if_elseif`** (matrix decoding)
*   **from Variables, drag `get_multiple_pins`** (batch read)
*   **from Text, drag `pico_print`** (status output)

### 7. Variables
*   **x_val / y_val**: Digital states of the two axes.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Sensing**
1.  **Read Batch**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Store States**:
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `x_val` = Read GP16.
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `y_val` = Read GP17.

**B. Decoding Phase**
3.  **Check Up**: From **Logic**, if `x_val` == 0 AND `y_val` == 0, **Print** "FLAT".
4.  **Check Left**: From **Logic**, else if `x_val` == 0 AND `y_val` == 1, **Print** "TILT LEFT".
5.  **Check Right**: From **Logic**, else if `x_val` == 1 AND `y_val` == 0, **Print** "TILT RIGHT".
6.  **Check Down**: From **Logic**, else, **Print** "UPSIDE DOWN".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Horizontal**: Both balls rest on pins. Result: "FLAT".
2.  **Rotation**: You tilt the board left. Only one ball moves away from its pins.
3.  **Result**: The code identifies the unique "01" pattern and prints "TILT LEFT" every 100ms.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

swX = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
swY = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    x = swX.value()
    y = swY.value()
    
    # 2-Bit Logic Matrix
    if x == 0 and y == 0:
        print("ORIENTATION: LEVEL")
    elif x == 0 and y == 1:
        print("ORIENTATION: TILT LEFT")
    elif x == 1 and y == 0:
        print("ORIENTATION: TILT RIGHT")
    else:
        print("ORIENTATION: INVERTED")
        
    utime.sleep(0.2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mounting Offset**: If the switches aren't exactly 90 degrees apart, you'll get "ghost" states where the system thinks it's tilted when it's not.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Tilt Lock**: Create a secret code where you must tilt: Left -> Right -> Left to unlock a variable.

## 1. Project 1213: Advanced Tilt Sensor Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Fail-Safe Incline Guard". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a protective interlock that automatically cuts power to a system if a tilt angle threshold is exceeded for more than 2 seconds.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Threshold Persistence**: Ignoring momentary tilt (vibration) but responding to sustained tilt.
*   **Hardware Interlocks**: Using software to physically disconnect an actuator for safety.
*   **Time-Bounded Validation**: Using timers to verify that an error condition is real.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Relay / Motor**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP16 | Safety Sensor |
| **Relay Signal** | GP15 | Power Control |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Alarm Audio |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_ms`** (safety timer)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (cut power)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then_else`** (persistent check)

### 7. Variables
*   **tilt_start**: Timestamp of when the unsafe tilt began.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Check Tilt**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP16 == 1 (Unsafe):
    *   From **Logic**, if `tilt_start` == 0:
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `tilt_start` = **Time** `ticks_ms`.

**B. Alarm Validation**
3.  **Timer Comparison**: From **Logic**, if `tilt_start` > 0:
    *   Calculate `elapsed` = **Time** `ticks_diff`.
    *   From **Logic**, if `elapsed` > 2000 (Unsafe duration):
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 LOW (Cut power).
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP14 HIGH (Buzzer).

**C. Recovery**
4.  **Reset**: From **Logic**, else (Safe orientation):
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `tilt_start` = 0.
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 HIGH (Restore power).
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP14 LOW (Silence).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Running**: Device is level. Power relay is ON.
2.  **Bumping**: A sudden shake tips the switch for 0.5s. `elapsed` is only 500, so nothing happens.
3.  **Critical**: The device tips and stays at 45 degrees.
4.  **Shutdown**: After 2.0s of continuous tilt, the relay clicks OFF and the buzzer screams.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
relay = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT, value=1) # Normal ON
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

tilt_start = 0

while True:
    if tilt.value() == 1: # Unsafe orientation
        if tilt_start == 0:
            tilt_start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Check if duration exceeds 2s limit
        if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), tilt_start) > 2000:
            relay.low() # KILL POWER
            buzzer.high() # ALERT
            print("ALERT: CRITICAL TILT DETECTED. EMERGENCY STOP.")
    else:
        # Safe
        relay.high()
        buzzer.low()
        tilt_start = 0
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Sleep**: If you use `time.sleep(2)` to wait for the tilt, you won't be able to turn the buzzer off or see if the device leveled out halfway through.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Visual Warning**: Make the power LED blink yellow during the 2-second "grace period" before shutdown.

---

## 1. Project 1214: Advanced Tilt Sensor Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Electronic Level (LED Gauge)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an interactive visual gauge that indicates the magnitude of tilt using a series of 5 LEDs, providing real-time UX feedback for orientation leveling.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Array Mapping (LED Banks)**: Activating specific members of an array based on an input value.
*   **Linear Feedback**: Translating a digital sensor reading into a proportional visual representation.
*   **UX Design**: Creating a clear "Center" vs "Out of Range" visual indicator.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Tilt Switches** (Opposite directions)
*   **5x LEDs**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Left/Right** | GP14, 15 | Directional inputs |
| **LED Bar (1-5)** | GP16-20 | Visual Gauge |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `for_each_item`** (clear all LEDs)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (highlight center)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_elseif_else`** (gauge logic)

### 7. Variables
*   **led_pins**: List of the 5 GPIO numbers used.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Setup Bank**: From **Loops**, drag `for_each` through [16,17,18,19,20]:
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** pin to **Output**.

**B. Gauge Logic**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Clear Bar**: Turn off all 5 LEDs.
4.  **Directional Check**:
    *   From **Logic**, if GP14 (Left) is 0:
        *   From **Smart IO**, Turn ON GP16 (Far Left).
    *   From **Logic**, else if GP15 (Right) is 0:
        *   From **Smart IO**, Turn ON GP20 (Far Right).
    *   From **Logic**, else (Steady):
        *   From **Smart IO**, Turn ON GP18 (Center Green).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Balanced**: Device is flat. Only the center LED (GP18) is lit.
2.  **Lean**: You tilt it slightly left. GP14 closes.
3.  **Feedback**: Center LED turns off; GP16 (Red) turns on, telling the user to "Move Right".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

leds = [machine.Pin(p, machine.Pin.OUT) for p in range(16, 21)]
tilt_l = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
tilt_r = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

def clear():
    for l in leds: l.value(0)

while True:
    clear()
    
    # Simple 3-Zone logic
    if tilt_l.value() == 0:
        leds[0].value(1) # LEFT ALERT
    elif tilt_r.value() == 0:
        leds[4].value(1) # RIGHT ALERT
    else:
        leds[2].value(1) # LEVEL STABLE
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Over-Initialization**: Don't reset the `Pin` objects inside the loop! Only update their `.value(0)` or `.value(1)`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Persistence**: If the tilt is extreme, make the LEDs blink instead of staying solid.

---

## 1. Project 1215: Automated Advanced Tilt Sensor Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Active Ledge Prevention". At the end of this project, you will be able to prototype an autonomous rover safety system that uses a forward-facing tilt sensor to detect "Cliff Edges" and execute an emergency reverse maneuver before the vehicle falls.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Emergency Overrides**: Logic that ignores user input to execute a hard-coded safety routine.
*   **Blind Reversing**: Moving backwards for a fixed duration to exit a danger zone.
*   **Reactive Navigation**: Using edge-triggered events to change movement direction.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch** (Mounted pointing slightly forward/down)
*   **2x DC Motors**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Edge Sensor** | GP15 | "Cliff" detector |
| **Motor L/R** | GP16/17, 18/19 | Drive pins |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_motor_speed`** (drive)
*   **from Time, drag `pico_wait`** (maneuver duration)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_triggered`** (edge event)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Direct sequential logic is used.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Cruising**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Drive Forward**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motors** to **40%** PWM.

**B. Evasion**
3.  **Detect Cliff**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP15 == 1 (Ball falls forward):
    *   **STOP**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motors** to **0**.
    *   **Text**: From **Text**, **Print** "CLIFF DETECTED! REVERSING...".
    *   **Reverse**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motors** to **-50%**.
    *   **Wait**: From **Time**, **Wait** 1.5 seconds.
    *   **Panic Turn**: Set Left Motor -50%, Right Motor +50% for 1 second.
4.  **Resume**: Loop restarts and robot tries a new direction.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Explore**: The robot drives toward the end of a table.
2.  **Trigger**: The front wheel goes over the edge; the tilt switch trips as the robot nose-dives slightly.
3.  **Save**: The code catches the trip, slams the motors into reverse for 1.5s, spins the robot 90 degrees, and keeps exploring safely.

###  Ten. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Motors
m1_a = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
m1_b = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)
# ... Motor 2 setup ...

cliff = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

def move(speed):
    # speed logic for forward/reverse
    pass

while True:
    # 1. Normal Scan
    # move(40)
    
    # 2. Safety Intercept
    if cliff.value() == 1: # BALL MOVED FORWARD (OVER EDGE)
        print("EDGE! EVADING...")
        # STOP
        # move(0)
        utime.sleep(0.2)
        
        # REVERSE
        # move(-50)
        utime.sleep(1.5)
        
        # TURN
        # set_motors(-50, 50)
        utime.sleep(1.0)
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Center of Gravity**: If your robot is too fast, it will fall off the table before the software can react. Keep `move` speeds low during testing!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Speed Ramp**: As the cliff sensor triggers, gradually increase reverse power to avoid wheel spin on the edge.

---

## 1. Project 1216: Advanced Tilt Sensor Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Man Down Alert". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a personnel safety device that monitors orientation and triggers an emergency alarm only after a sustained period of horizontal inactivity.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sustained Verification**: Distinguishing between accidental tilts vs supported emergencies.
*   **Deadman Timer**: A timer that resets when activity is detected but counts up during danger.
*   **Warning vs Alarm States**: Providing an audible grace period before full alarm activation.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Piezo Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP16 | Vertical sensor |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Safety Siren |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `pico_ticks_ms`** (timer)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `buzzer_beep`** (warning tone)
*   **from Variables, drag `set_boolean`** (is_horizontal)

### 7. Variables
*   **fall_time**: Timestamp when the device first tilted.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Sense Tilt**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP16 == 0 (Fallen):
    *   From **Logic**, if `fall_time` == 0:
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `fall_time` = **Time** `ticks_ms`.

**B. Alarm Validation**
3.  **Check Duration**: From **Logic**, if `fall_time` > 0:
    *   Calculate `elapsed` = **Time** `ticks_diff`.
    *   **Pre-Alarm**: From **Logic**, if `elapsed` > 5000:
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Beep** GP15 (Short pulses).
    *   **Full Alarm**: From **Logic**, if `elapsed` > 10000:
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 HIGH (Continuous).

**C. Reset Condition**
4.  **Stand Up**: From **Logic**, else (Upright):
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `fall_time` = 0.
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 LOW.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: Device is upright. Buzzer is silent.
2.  **Fall**: User lies flat. For 5 seconds, nothing happens.
3.  **Warning**: After 5s, the device chirps, allowing the user to reset it by standing up.
4.  **Emergency**: After 10s of no movement, the buzzer screams continuously.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
fall_start = 0

while True:
    if tilt.value() == 0: # Fallen
        if fall_start == 0:
            fall_start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        diff = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), fall_start) // 1000
        if diff >= 10:
            buzzer.value(1) # CRITICAL ALARM
        elif diff >= 5:
            # Pulsing warning
            buzzer.value(not buzzer.value())
            utime.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        fall_start = 0
        buzzer.value(0)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **No Reset**: Ensure `fall_time` is set to 0 immediately when the device returns to vertical, or the alarm will trigger the next time the person sits down briefly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **LED Heartbeat**: Add a green blinking LED to show the system is active and monitoring.

---

## 1. Project 1217: Advanced Tilt Sensor Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Mishandling Seal". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement non-reversible status logging (latch logic) to detect if fragile cargo has been flipped during transit, even if it is returned to vertical.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Latching Flags**: Variables that "lock" into a state and cannot be cleared without an admin command.
*   **Tamper Evidence**: Using digital states to represent physical property breach.
*   **Fault Persistence**: Maintaining error codes across power cycles (simulated).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Red LED** (Tamper Light)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP16 | Orientation Sensor |
| **Tamper LED** | GP15 | Incident Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Variables, drag `set_boolean`** (is_ruined)
*   **from Logic, drag `controls_if`** (one-way trip)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_write`** (latch LED)

### 7. Variables
*   **seal_broken**: Boolean tracking if a flip occurred.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Detection**: From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP16 == 1 (Box Flipped):
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `seal_broken` = true.

**B. Feedback**
3.  **Latch Light**: From **Logic**, if `seal_broken` == true:
    *   From **Smart IO**, **Set** GP15 HIGH.
    *   From **Text**, **Print** "ERROR: PACKAGE MISHANDLED".

**C. Verification**
4.  **No Reset**: Observe that even if the box is tilted back to 0, the LED on GP15 stays bright RED until the Pico is hard-rebooted.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Pack the breadboard (cargo) into a box.
2.  **Flip**: The box is dropped or rolls over.
3.  **Arrival**: The box looks fine on the outside, but as soon as the inspector looks at the internal Pico, the glowing Red LED confirms the cargo was inverted.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

sensor = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
flipped = False

print("Shipping Monitor Active. Secure cargo...")

while True:
    # Check for violation
    if sensor.value() == 1:
        flipped = True
        print("!!! SECURITY ALERT: CARGO INVERTED !!!")
    
    # Latch behavior
    if flipped:
        led.value(1) # LED STAY ON FOREVER
        
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Volatile Memory**: Real-world seals save this state to flash memory so the "flipped" state survives if the battery dies.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Counter**: Count how many times the box was flipped during the trip.

## 1. Project 1218: Smart Advanced Tilt Sensor System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Gesture Remote Control". At the end of this project, you will be able to transform simple tilt motions into complex communication signals by mapping directional movements to specific UART command strings.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Gesture Mapping**: Translating physical motion into digital commands.
*   **Command Protocols**: Sending structured strings (e.g., `CMD:VOL_UP`) over serial.
*   **Interaction Debouncing**: Ensuring one tilt = one command, not 100 repeats.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Tilt Switches** (Forward/Backward)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Forward** | GP16 | Action 1 |
| **Tilt Backward** | GP17 | Action 2 |
| **UART TX** | GP0 | Command Out |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Comm, drag `uart_send_text`** (send trigger)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_triggered`** (edge detection)
*   **from Time, drag `pico_wait`** (cooldown)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Edge triggering handles the logic.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **UART Setup**: From **Comm**, **Init UART0** at 9600 baud.

**B. Gesture Logic**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Forward Gesture**: From **Logic**, if GP16 is 0 (Tilted Forward):
    *   From **Comm**, **Send UART** "ID:PICO1:CMD:PLAY\n".
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 1.0s (Gesture Cooldown).
4.  **Backward Gesture**: From **Logic**, else if GP17 is 0 (Tilted Back):
    *   From **Comm**, **Send UART** "ID:PICO1:CMD:STOP\n".
    *   From **Time**, **Wait** 1.0s.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Usage**: You hold the Pico like a remote.
2.  **Action**: You tilt it forward. The serial monitor shows "CMD:PLAY".
3.  **Logic**: The 1.0s wait ensures you don't send "PLAY" 50 times in the split second the ball is rolling.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

uart = machine.UART(0, 9600)
t_fwd = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
t_rev = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

while True:
    if t_fwd.value() == 0:
        uart.write("CMD:VOLUME_UP\n")
        print("Sent: VOL UP")
        utime.sleep(1.0)
    elif t_rev.value() == 0:
        uart.write("CMD:VOLUME_DN\n")
        print("Sent: VOL DN")
        utime.sleep(1.0)
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Floating Pins**: If pins GP16/17 aren't pulled HIGH, the remote will send "VOLUME UP" randomly due to air static!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Combo Gestures**: Only send a command if you tilt Forward then Backward within 2 seconds.

---

## 1. Project 1219: Advanced Tilt Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
## 1. Project 1219: Advanced Tilt Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Mechanical Trigger Characterization". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform physical hardware characterization by measuring the exact actuation angle of a ball-tilt switch using a manual calibration process.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Actuation Angle**: The exact degree of tilt from horizontal where the ball makes contact.
*   **Manual Benchmarking**: Using software to assist a human in finding physical limits.
*   **Repeatability**: Testing multiple times to see if the trigger varies.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Buzzer**
*   **Protractor** (Paper or Plastic)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP16 | Sensor under test |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Audio feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (poll switch)
*   **from Text, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (log state)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_buzzer_freq`** (tone feedback)

### 7. Variables
*   **None**: Direct feedback used.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Calibration Mode**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Audio Assist**:
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP16 == 0:
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Set Buzzer** GP15 to **1000** Hz.
    *   From **Logic**, else:
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Set Buzzer** to **0**.

**B. Measurement**
3.  **Bench Setup**: Tape the Pico to a protractor.
4.  **Test**: Rotate the device starting from 0 degrees.
5.  **Record**: The MOMENT you hear the beep, note the number on the protractor. Repeat 5 times and find the average "Trigger Point".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Flat**: 0 degrees. System is quiet.
2.  **Move**: 5 deg... 10 deg... 15 deg...
3.  **Beep**: At 18 degrees, the buzzer sounds. You now know your specific switch triggers at exactly 18 degrees.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

sw = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzz = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))

while True:
    if sw.value() == 0:
        buzz.freq(1000); buzz.duty_u16(32768)
        print(">>> TRIGGERED <<<")
    else:
        buzz.duty_u16(0)
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Breadboard Flex**: If the switch is loose, it might lean 5 degrees on its own, ruining your measurement. Ensure it is flush against the board.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Side Characterization**: Test if the trigger angle is different if you tilt to the side vs forward.

---

## 1. Project 1220: Advanced Advanced Tilt Sensor Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Low-Latency Digital Maracas". At the end of this project, you will be able to prototype a responsive electronic instrument that translates rhythmic percussion (shaking) into instantaneous auditory feedback.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Low-Latency Processing**: Minimizing the "lag" between a physical hit and a sound.
*   **Envelope Simulation**: Creating short bursts of noise to mimic acoustic textures.
*   **Interrupt-Style Polling**: checking sensors as fast as possible for musical timing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Tilt Switch**
*   **Piezo Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tilt Switch** | GP16 | Rhythm Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Sound Engine |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `buzzer_beep`** (30ms burst)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (high speed poll)
*   **from Variables, drag `pico_state_memory`** (last_raw)

### 7. Variables
*   **last_val**: The previous raw sensor reading.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Setup Audio**: From **Smart IO**, **Init Buzzer** GP15 at **5000** Hz (White noise hiss).

**B. Performance Loop**
2.  **State Check**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Trigger logic**:
    *   From **Logic**, if **Smart IO** Read GP16 != `last_val`: (Ball moved)
        *   From **Smart IO**, **Beep** GP15 for **30** ms.
        *   From **Variables**, **Set** `last_val` = Read GP16.

**C. Tuning**
4.  **No Sleep**: Do NOT add any `utime.sleep` blocks in the main loop or the instrument will feel "laggy". Use the 30ms beep as your only delay.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Shake**: You rattle the device back and forth.
2.  **Sound**: Every time the ball hits a pin, you hear a sharp "chik" sound.
3.  **Result**: The audio perfectly tracks your hand speed, allowing you to play complex rhythms.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzz = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
buzz.freq(5000) # High pitch hiss

last = tilt.value()

while True:
    curr = tilt.value()
    if curr != last:
        # Trigger short envelope
        buzz.duty_u16(5000)
        utime.sleep_ms(30)
        buzz.duty_u16(0)
        last = curr
    # No extra sleep - keep latency low!
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Frequency Too Low**: If you use 440Hz, it will sound like a "beep". 5000Hz+ sounds like the click of a real percussion instrument.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Double Shaker**: Add a second switch to trigger a different pitch for "High" and "Low" maraca sounds.

    *   If changed:
        *   Instantly set PWM Duty to **50%**.
        *   Wait exactly **20 milliseconds**.
        *   Set PWM Duty to **0%**.
4.  **Debounce Logic**:
    *   Wait another **20 milliseconds** before allowing the next "chik" to prevent double-hits from 
    residual ball rattle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Action**: You shake the device like a maraca.
2.  **Sound**: *Chik-chik... chik-chik*.
3.  **Result**: The audio perfectly matches your hand's motion.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

tilt = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
# Use high freq to sound like static/sand
shaker = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
shaker.freq(5000) 
shaker.duty_u16(0)

last = tilt.value()

print("Digital Maraca Active. Let's make some music!")

while True:
    curr = tilt.value()
    
    # On every movement of the ball
    if curr != last:
        # Start "Chik" sound
        shaker.duty_u16(5000) # Subtle hiss
        utime.sleep_ms(30)
        shaker.duty_u16(0)
        
        # Lockout to prevent rattle-noise (Tempo limit)
        utime.sleep_ms(30)
        last = curr
        
    # No sleep(0.1) here - we need maximum speed!
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Standard Sleep**: If you use `utime.sleep(0.1)`, the maraca will feel "laggy" and unresponsive to fast rhythms. Use `utime.sleep_ms()` for precision.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Hand**: Use two Picos, one in each hand, set to different frequencies (Sand vs. Lead beads).

## Batch 123: Advanced Laser Tripwire 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1221: Advanced Laser Tripwire Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Optical Response Analysis". At the end of this project, you will be able to characterize the optoelectronic response of an LDR by modulating a Laser via PWM and identifying the saturation points of the sensor.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **PWM Light Modulation**: Controlling average power to a laser diode to vary its intensity.
*   **Sensor Saturation**: The point where further light increase does not significantly change LDR resistance.
*   **Linearity Testing**: Determining how closely a sensor's analog output follows its digital control.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **5V Laser Diode Module** (Pico compatible)
*   **LDR (Light Dependent Resistor)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | PWM Control |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | ADC0 Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_motor_speed`** (PWM duty)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_analog_pin`** (LDR read)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_for_range`** (stepping)

### 7. Variables
*   **duty**: The power level sent to the laser.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Characterization Loop**
1.  **Iterate**: From **Loops**, create a `for` loop from 0 to 65535 in steps of 5000.
2.  **Modulate**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Motor (Laser)** GP14 speed to `duty`.
3.  **Stabilize**: From **Time**, **Wait** 100ms.

**B. Data Collection**
4.  **Sampling**: From **Smart Sensors**, **Read Analog** GP26.
5.  **Logging**: From **Text**, **Print** "Duty: [duty] Value: [read_val]".

**C. Completion**
6.  **Cleanup**: After the loop, **Set GP14** to 0.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Startup**: Laser is off. LDR reads ambient room light.
2.  **Ramp**: Laser intensity increases iteratively.
3.  **Analysis**: Observe the printed values to see where the LDR reading stops decreasing (reaches saturation).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

laser = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
laser.freq(1000)
ldr = machine.ADC(machine.Pin(26))

print("--- Laser/LDR Linearity Test ---")

for d in range(0, 65536, 5000):
    laser.duty_u16(d)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    print(f"Duty: {d}, LDR: {val}")

laser.duty_u16(0)
print("Test Complete.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Speed**: LDRs are chemical sensors and update slowly. If the loop runs too fast, the readings will "smear" and lag behind the actual laser power.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Graphing**: Copy the terminal data into Excel to plot the response curve for your specific room conditions.

---

## 1. Project 1222: Advanced Laser Tripwire Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Physical Alignment Gauge". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a visual strength indicator by mapping laser signal intensity to the position of a servo-driven pointer.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Analog Mapping**: Scaling a high-resolution input (0-65535) to a mechanical output (0-180 degrees).
*   **Mechanical Feedback**: Using physical motion as a user interface for alignment.
*   **Normalization**: Correcting for ambient light to ensure the gauge starts at 0% when the laser is off.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser & LDR**
*   **Servo Motor (SG90)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | ADC Input |
| **Servo PWM** | GP15 | Gauge Arm Control |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (pointer)
*   **from Math, drag `map_range_to_angle`** (scaling)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_analog`** (intensity)

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_light**: The current LDR reading.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Servo Sweep**: Perform a test sweep from 0 to 180 and back to center.

**B. Monitoring**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Sampling**: From **Smart Sensors**, **Read Analog** GP26.

**C. Mapping Logic**
4.  **Scaling**: From **Math**, **Map** the input (50000 -> 2000) to (0 -> 180).
5.  **Output**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servo** GP15 angle to the mapped result.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Laser is missing the sensor. Needle points to "Empty".
2.  **Calibration**: Adjust the laser direction. The needle moves in real-time.
3.  **Locked**: At perfect alignment, the needle hits the 180-degree mark.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
servo.freq(50)

def set_angle(angle):
    duty = int(1638 + (angle * (8192 - 1638) / 180))
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Scale: Bright=2000 (180 deg), Dark=50000 (0 deg)
    # Clip values to range
    val = max(min(raw, 50000), 2000)
    
    # Linear interpolation
    perc = 1 - (val - 2000) / (50000 - 2000)
    angle = perc * 180
    
    set_angle(angle)
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Jitter**: Small fluctuations in room light can cause the servo to "buzz" or vibrate. Use `utime.sleep(0.1)` to slow down the updates slightly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Audio Lock**: Connect a buzzer that increases in pitch as the servo approaches 180 degrees.

---

## 1. Project 1223: Advanced Laser Tripwire Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Intelligent Adaptation". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an adaptive threshold system that ignores slow changes in ambient light (like sunset) while triggering instantly on rapid signal drops.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Software High-Pass Filter**: Blocking slow "DC" signals while allowing fast "AC" events.
*   **Exponential Moving Average (EMA)**: A statistical method to track a shifting environment baseline.
*   **Dynamic Calibration**: Adjusting the trigger point in real-time based on current light conditions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser & LDR**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | Baseline Sensor |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Breach Alarm |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_calculate_ema`** (averaging)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_compare`** (anomaly)
*   **from Variables, drag `set_number`** (baseline)

### 7. Variables
*   **average**: The drifting "normal" level of the room.
*   **threshold**: Sensitivity (Gap between average and trigger).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Warm Up**: Read the LDR once at boot and set `average` to that value.

**B. Adaptive Loop**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Update Baseline**: Every 10ms, update `average` = `(average * 0.99) + (current * 0.01)`.
4.  **Detect Drop**: From **Logic**, if `(average - current) > 8000`:
    *   **ALARM**: From **Smart IO**, **Set GP14** HIGH for 1 second.
    *   **Debug**: Print "Intruder Detected!".

**C. Verification**
5.  **Test 1**: Dim the house lights slowly. The alarm should NOT trigger (baseline adapts).
6.  **Test 2**: Snap your finger through the beam. Alarm should trigger (baseline cannot follow fast events).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Morning**: Room gets brighter. Baseline follows slowly.
2.  **Evening**: Room gets darker. Baseline follows slowly.
3.  **Event**: A person breaks the laser. The signal drops instantly. The difference between the slow baseline and fast signal exceeds 8000. Alarm sounds.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
avg = ldr.read_u16()
threshold = 8000

print("Intelligent Laser Guard Armed.")

while True:
    curr = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Slow Adaptation (EMA)
    avg = (avg * 0.995) + (curr * 0.005)
    
    # Compare current value to its own average
    if avg - curr > threshold:
        print("!!! BREACH DETECTED !!!")
        buzzer.on()
        utime.sleep(1)
        buzzer.off()
        avg = ldr.read_u16() # Re-sync after event
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Alpha Gain**: If the filter factor (0.005) is too large, the baseline will follow a person moving slowly, and the alarm will never trip.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Maintenance Alert**: If the `average` stays very low for a long time, print "Sensor Obstructed - Please Clean".

---

## 1. Project 1224: Advanced Laser Tripwire Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Security Audit Log". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an event-driven display using the internal RTC (Real Time Clock) to timestamp breaches on an OLED screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Onboard RTC**: Using hardware timers to track literal hours, minutes, and seconds.
*   **Event Persistence**: Keeping an alarm status visible until manually cleared.
*   **Formatted Timestamps**: Converting raw system time into human-readable text.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser & LDR**
*   **OLED Display (SSD1306)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL** | GP4/GP5 | I2C Interface |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | Digital Probe |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System & Storage, drag `set_rtc_time`** (init)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_text_row`** (UI)
*   **from Text, drag `text_join`** (timestamp)

### 7. Variables
*   **hit_time**: The string representing the last occurrence.
*   **hit_count**: Total number of violations.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Clock Setup**
1.  **Initialize**: From **System**, set RTC to the current year/date/hour.

**B. Monitoring**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Detect**: If GP26 == 0 (Beam Broken):
    *   **Increment**: `hit_count` = `hit_count + 1`.
    *   **Timestamp**: Fetch time and format to "HH:MM:SS".

**C. Visual Update**
4.  **Display**: From **Smart Display**, **Draw Text** on OLED:
    *   Row 1: "GUARD ARMED"
    *   Row 2: "Last Hit: [hit_time]"
    *   Row 3: "Total: [hit_count]"

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: OLED shows "Armed" and "Last Hit: None".
2.  **Intrusion**: Someone walks through at 3:15 PM.
3.  **Persistence**: The OLED immediately updates to "Last Hit: 15:15:00". It stays visible so the owner sees it later.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

rtc = machine.RTC()
rtc.datetime((2023, 1, 1, 0, 9, 0, 0, 0))

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
sensor = machine.Pin(26, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

count = 0
last_time = "None"

def refresh_ui(status):
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("LASER GUARD", 20, 0)
    oled.text(f"STATUS: {status}", 0, 25)
    oled.text(f"LAST: {last_time}", 0, 40)
    oled.text(f"TOTAL: {count}", 0, 55)
    oled.show()

refresh_ui("SECURE")

while True:
    if sensor.value() == 0:
        count += 1
        t = rtc.datetime()
        last_time = "{:02d}:{:02d}:{:02d}".format(t[4], t[5], t[6])
        refresh_ui("!! BREACH !!")
        utime.sleep(2)
        refresh_ui("SECURE")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Zero Padding**: Without `{:02d}`, a time like 12:05 will look like `12:5`, which is confusing. Always pad with leading zeros.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Log to File**: Save the timestamps to `alerts.log` on the Pico's internal flash memory for forensic review.
