## 1. Project 1225: Automated Advanced Laser Tripwire Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Interactive Targeting System". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a closed-loop laser turret where servos position a laser based on joystick input, and a distant sensor provides electronic "hit confirmation".

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **2-Axis Servo Pan/Tilt**: Controlling horizontal and vertical motion concurrently.
*   **Joystick-to-Angle Mapping**: Converting raw analog voltage to precise mechanical degrees.
*   **Target Loop-Back**: Using a remote sensor to send a feedback signal back to the master controller.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Analog Joystick**
*   **2x Servos** (Pan/Tilt mount)
*   **Laser Module**
*   **LDR Target** (Remote)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Joystick X/Y** | GP26/27 | ADC Inputs |
| **Pan/Tilt PWM** | GP14/15 | Drive Pins |
| **Hit Signal** | GP16 | Digital Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (steering)
*   **from Math, drag `pico_analog_to_angle`** (mapping)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_triggered`** (confirmation)

### 7. Variables
*   **joy_x, joy_y**: Raw analog readings.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Steering Logic**
1.  **Read Sticks**: From **Smart Sensors**, **Read Analog** GP26 and GP27.
2.  **Map**: Convert 0-65535 to 0-180 degrees.
3.  **Adjust**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servos** GP14 and GP15 to the new angles.

**B. Combat Logic**
4.  **Detect Hit**: From **Logic**, if GP16 == 1 (Laser hit target sensor):
    *   **Alert**: Print "TARGET NEUTRALIZED!".
    *   **Sound**: From **Smart IO**, **Beep** GP13.

**C. Maintenance**
5.  **Alignment**: Ensure the lasers are mechanically aligned with the servo centers before securing screws.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Aim**: You move the joystick. The laser turret pans and tilts smoothly.
2.  **Fire**: You aim at the distant LDR target.
3.  **Feedback**: When the red dot overlaps the sensor, the Pico terminal screams "HIT!" and the buzzer beeps.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

pan = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
tilt = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))
pan.freq(50); tilt.freq(50)

jx = machine.ADC(26)
jy = machine.ADC(27)
target = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_DOWN)

def set_angle(pwm, angle):
    duty = int(1638 + (angle * (8192-1638)/180))
    pwm.duty_u16(duty)

print("Laser Turret Armed. Ready to fire.")

while True:
    ang_x = jx.read_u16() * 180 / 65535
    ang_y = jy.read_u16() * 180 / 65535
    
    set_angle(pan, ang_x)
    set_angle(tilt, ang_y)
    
    if target.value() == 1:
        print(">> HIT CONFIRMED! <<")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wiring Stress**: Ensure the servo wires have enough "slack" to rotate 180 degrees without getting caught or unplugged.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Scan**: Program the turret to automatically "patrol" the room by moving in a square pattern.

## 1. Project 1226: Advanced Laser Tripwire Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Pet-Immune Guard". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement size-based object discrimination using dual-beam logic to allow pets to pass while triggering an alarm for taller human intruders.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Logical AND Gates**: Requiring two conditions (High Beam AND Low Beam) to be met for an action.
*   **Object Profiling**: Spacing sensors at different heights to identify the size of the intersecting object.
*   **False Positive Reduction**: Using physical filters to prevent non-threatening triggers.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Lasers & 2x LDRs**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **High Beam (80cm)** | GP26 | Human Detector |
| **Low Beam (10cm)** | GP27 | Pet Detector |
| **Alarm Buzzer** | GP14 | Safety Siren |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`** (combined check)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `pico_gpio_read`** (beam status)
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (status log)

### 7. Variables
*   **bh, bl**: Boolean status of the high and low beams.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Installation**
1.  **Placement**: Mount the first laser at ankle height (10cm) and the second at waist height (80cm).

**B. Monitoring**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Read Beams**: Set `bl` = Read GP27 and `bh` = Read GP26.

**C. Security Logic**
4.  **Human Detection**: From **Logic**, if `bh` == 0 AND `bl` == 0:
    *   **ALARM**: From **Smart IO**, **Set GP14** HIGH for 5 seconds.
    *   **Text**: Print "INTRUDER ALERT!".
5.  **Pet Detection**: From **Logic**, else if `bl` == 0:
    *   **Log**: Print "Pet detected. Ignoring...".
6.  **Anomaly**: Else if `bh` == 0:
    *   **Log**: Print "Warning: High beam blocked (Bird/Jump?).".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Pet Pass**: A dog runs through. Only the 10cm beam breaks. The buzzer stays silent.
2.  **Human Pass**: A person walks through. Both beams are broken. The alarm screams.
3.  **Result**: Effective security that doesn't trigger for the household cat.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

beam_high = machine.Pin(26, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
beam_low = machine.Pin(27, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Smart Guard Active. Spacing Beams...")

while True:
    bh = (beam_high.value() == 0)
    bl = (beam_low.value() == 0)
    
    if bh and bl:
        print("!!! HUMAN DETECTED: FULL ALARM !!!")
        buzzer.on()
        utime.sleep(5)
        buzzer.off()
    elif bl:
        print("Pet detected (Low beam only). Disregarding.")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
    elif bh:
        print("Warning: High beam obstruction (Jump?).")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Alignment**: If the beams aren't perfectly parallel, a person might break one and then the other, missing the `AND` window. Walk slowly during testing.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Direction Logic**: Log which beam breaks first to see if the person is entering or leaving the room.

## 1. Project 1227: Advanced Laser Tripwire Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Transit Profile Logger". At the end of this project, you will be able to measure the exact duration of a laser beam breach to determine the transit speed or physical length of an object passing through the sensor.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Transit Time Integration**: Calculating the delta between enter and exit timestamps.
*   **Blocking vs Non-Blocking Logic**: Efficiently waiting for an event to finish before processing.
*   **Physical Profiling**: Using time as a proxy for physical size.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser & LDR**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | Event Timer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`** (start time)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_diff`** (duration)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_while`** (hold)

### 7. Variables
*   **start_tick**: Timestamp of the entry.
*   **duration**: Total milliseconds of obstruction.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
2.  **Capture Entry**: If GP26 == 0 (Beam Broken):
    *   From **Variables**, **Set** `start_tick` = **Time** `ticks_ms`.

**B. Hold Phase**
3.  **Wait for Exit**: From **Loops**, drag `while` Read GP26 == 0:
    *   Do nothing. This blocks the code while the object is in the beam.

**C. Reporting**
4.  **Calculate**: Once exited, `duration` = **Time** `ticks_diff` (current, `start_tick`).
5.  **Log**: From **Text**, **Print** "Object transit took: [duration] ms".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Fast**: You swipe your hand through. Terminal: "85ms".
2.  **Slow**: You walk through slowly. Terminal: "1240ms".
3.  **Result**: You can now distinguish between a ball being thrown through and a person walking through.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.Pin(26, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

print("Transit Timer Online. Waiting for objects...")

while True:
    if ldr.value() == 0:
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Block until beam is restored
        while ldr.value() == 0:
            pass
            
        end = utime.ticks_ms()
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(end, start)
        
        print(f"EVENT: Transit Duration = {duration} ms")
        
        # Classification
        if duration < 100:
            print("Type: FAST (Projectile)")
        else:
            print("Type: SLOW (Person)")
            
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Deadlock**: If the laser is misaligned, the code will hang in the `while` loop forever. Ensure the laser is secure.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Speed Trap**: Use two lasers separated by 10cm. `velocity = 0.1 / (time2 - time1)`.

---

## 1. Project 1228: Smart Advanced Laser Tripwire System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Optical Data Link". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Free Space Optics" (FSO) by flashing a laser to transmit text data to a remote receiver using a bit-banged protocol.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Opto-Communication**: Transmitting digital 1s and 0s via light pulses.
*   **Baud Rate Synchronization**: Ensuring both devices sample at the same frequency.
*   **Noise Floor**: Accounting for environment light that may corrupt high-speed pulses.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **2x Raspberry Pi Picos** (One TX, One RX)
*   **Laser Module**
*   **LDR / Phototransistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser VCC** | GP14 | TX Signal |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | RX Signal |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_uart_write`** (simulated)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `digital_write`** (pulsing)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_for_each`** (bit extraction)

### 7. Variables
*   **baud_delay**: The timing between bit samples (e.g., 50ms).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Sender Config**
1.  **Protocol**: Set `baud_delay` = 50ms (20 bps).
2.  **Bit Extraction**: For each character in your message, calculate the 8-bit binary pattern.

**B. Transmission**
3.  **Start Bit**: From **Smart IO**, **Set GP14** HIGH for one delay cycle.
4.  **Payload**: For each bit, set GP14 to the bit value (1=ON, 0=OFF).
5.  **Stop Bit**: Set GP14 LOW.

**C. Verification**
6.  **Serial Monitor**: Observe the receiving Pico reconstructing the letters 'H', 'E', 'L', 'L', 'O' as they arrive via light.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Transmit**: You enter text on the master Pico. The laser flashes in a rapid digital sequence.
2.  **Receive**: Across the room, the second Pico "sees" the flashes and converts them back to ASCII.
3.  **Result**: Wireless data transfer without Wi-Fi or Bluetooth.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# SENDER CODE
tx = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
baud_delay = 0.05 # 20bps

def send_byte(b):
    tx.on() # Start
    utime.sleep(baud_delay)
    for i in range(8):
        if (b >> i) & 1:
            tx.on()
        else:
            tx.off()
        utime.sleep(baud_delay)
    tx.off() # Stop
    utime.sleep(baud_delay)

message = "HELLO"
print(f"Transmitting: {message}")
for char in message:
    send_byte(ord(char))
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Speed Limits**: LDRs cannot react fast enough for standard UART speeds (9600 baud). You must use a very low baud rate (10-50 bps) or use a Phototransistor.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Parity**: Add a checksum bit to verify if the message was blocked by a hand during transit.

---

## 1. Project 1229: Advanced Laser Tripwire Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Range Diagnostic". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform physical range-finding by calculating the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) of your laser beam over increasing distances.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Degradation**: Understanding how light spreads (Inverse-square law) over distance.
*   **RSSI (Signal Strength)**: A numerical indicator of the alignment quality.
*   **Reliability Zones**: Defining the operational limits where a tripwire is no longer effective.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Laser & LDR**
*   **Buzzer** (Signal meter)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR Pin 2** | GP26 | ADC Input |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Audio Probe |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Text, drag `pico_print_percentage`** (UI)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_compare`** (thresholds)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `buzzer_beep`** (proximity tone)

### 7. Variables
*   **strength**: 0-100% calculation of beam quality.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Benchmarking**
1.  **Direct Contact**: Measure LDR at 1cm. (Value ~1500).
2.  **Ambience**: Measure LDR with laser off. (Value ~35000).

**B. Diagnostic Loop**
3.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
4.  **Audio Probe**:
    *   If strength < 40% (Poor): Beep GP14 rapidly.
    *   If strength 40-70% (Fair): Beep GP14 slowly.
    *   If strength > 70% (Great): Silent.

**C. Testing**
5.  **Walk Away**: Increase the distance between the Laser and LDR. Note the point where the buzzer begins to beep rapidly. This is your safe operational limit.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **3 Meters**: Silent. Alignment is perfect.
2.  **8 Meters**: *Beep... Beep...* The laser dot spreading makes it hard for the sensor to pick up.
3.  **Result**: You now have a scientific range limit for your security system.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
PEAK = 1500
FLOOR = 35000

while True:
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    # Calculate percentage (Inverse)
    qual = 1 - (val - PEAK) / (FLOOR - PEAK)
    
    if qual < 0.3:
        print(f"SIGNAL CRITICAL: {int(qual*100)}%")
        buzzer.on(); utime.sleep(0.02); buzzer.off(); utime.sleep(0.1)
    elif qual < 0.6:
        print(f"SIGNAL WEAK: {int(qual*100)}%")
        buzzer.on(); utime.sleep(0.01); buzzer.off(); utime.sleep(0.5)
    else:
        print(f"SIGNAL GOOD: {int(qual*100)}%")
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ambient Fluctuations**: Room lights can artificially inflate your signal. Perform calibration in the same lighting the system will actually use.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Bar Graph**: Use an OLED to draw a horizontal signal strength bar.

---

## 1. Project 1230: Advanced Advanced Laser Tripwire Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Volumetric Spider Web". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement multi-point security scanning by multiplexing laser emitters to create an invisible 3D grid.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multiplexed Scanning**: Checking multiple sensors sequentially to save pins or power.
*   **Spatial Localization**: Identifying exactly WHERE a breach happened (Low, Mid, or High).
*   **Active Grids**: Using geometry to cover a volume rather than just a line.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x Lasers & 3x LDRs**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Laser 1/2/3** | GP10, 11, 12 | Scanning Outs |
| **Global LDR Input** | GP26 | Shared Analog Bus |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_for_each`** (sequencing)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (pulse)
*   **from Text, drag `text_join`** (localization log)

### 7. Variables
*   **beam_names**: List ["Floor", "Waist", "Head"].
*   **i**: Scanner index.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Scanner Setup**
1.  **Iterate**: Create a loop through pins 10, 11, 12.
2.  **Flash**: From **Smart IO**, **Set Pin** `active` HIGH, Wait 20ms, Read sensor, Set Pin LOW.

**B. Localization**
3.  **Detect**: If Sensor == 0 (Beam Blocked):
    *   **Identify**: Print "BREACH at: " + `beam_names[i]`.
    *   **Classification**: If head beam (GP12) is broken, trigger human alarm.

**C. Maintenance**
4.  **Sweep Speed**: Keep the `Wait` small (20ms) so the whole room is scanned multiple times per second.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: System cycles L1->L2->L3 rapidly.
2.  **Obstruction**: A box is placed on the floor.
3.  **Intel**: terminal shows "BREACH: Floor Beam. Waist and Head Secure.".
4.  **Result**: High-intelligence security that knows the difference between a rolling ball and a walking person.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

lasers = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.OUT) for i in [10, 11, 12]]
sensor = machine.Pin(26, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
names = ["LOW_BEAM", "MID_BEAM", "HIGH_BEAM"]

print("Volumetric 3D Grid Shield Active...")

while True:
    for i in range(3):
        lasers[i].on()
        utime.sleep_ms(20) # Sync wait
        
        if sensor.value() == 0:
            print(f"!!! BREACH DETECTED: {names[i]} !!!")
            if i == 2:
                print("Classification: TALL (Possible Intruder)")
        
        lasers[i].off()
        utime.sleep_ms(20)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sample Timing**: If the laser is turned off before the LDR recovers, the next beam in the scan might inherit the "broken" state. Keep the LDR recovery time (20ms) in mind.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Mirror Maze**: Bounce ONE laser off 4 mirrors to create a ring of light around a safe.

## Batch 124: Advanced Radar Screen 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1231: Advanced Radar Screen Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Echo Validation Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to distinguish between valid environmental reflections and sensor errors by analyzing microsecond-level pulse returns and identifying "Jamming" states.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Jamming Detection**: Recognizing when a sensor mesh is physically blocked by an object too close to measure (~2cm).
*   **Echo Zeroing**: Identifying the inherent electronic delay in HC-SR04 triggers before valid pulses begin.
*   **Timeout Handling**: Preventing code lockup when a pulse never returns (empty space).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SR04 VCC** | 5V (VBUS) | High Power |
| **SR04 TRIG** | GP14 | Trigger Pulse |
| **SR04 ECHO** | GP15 | Logic Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `get_distance`** (core read)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_elseif_else`** (validation)
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (telemetry)

### 7. Variables
*   **dist**: The raw distance in centimeters.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Configure Pins**: Initialize GP14 as OUTPUT and GP15 as INPUT.

**B. Data Capture**
2.  **Start Loop**: From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever`.
3.  **Sample**: Set `dist` = **Sensors** `get_distance` (TRIG=14, ECHO=15).

**C. Validation Logic**
4.  **Error Check**: From **Logic**, if `dist` < 2.5:
    *   **Alert**: Print "JAMMED: SENSOR OBSTRUCTED".
5.  **Timeout Check**: Else if `dist` == 0:
    *   **Alert**: Print "TIMEOUT: NO ECHO RETURNED".
6.  **Valid**: Else:
    *   **Log**: Print "DISTANCE: [dist] cm".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Clear View**: Terminal shows "DISTANCE: 45.2 cm".
2.  **Blocked**: You touch the sensor mesh. Terminal snaps to "JAMMED".
3.  **Extreme Range**: You point it at the open sky. Terminal shows "TIMEOUT".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

trig = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    
    start = utime.ticks_us()
    timeout = start + 30000 
    
    while echo.value() == 0:
        start = utime.ticks_us()
        if start > timeout: return 0
    while echo.value() == 1:
        end = utime.ticks_us()
        if end > timeout: return 0
        
    return (utime.ticks_diff(end, start) * 0.0343) / 2

print("Radar Hardware Validation Online...")

while True:
    d = get_dist()
    if d < 2.5:
        print("ALERT: SENSOR JAMMED")
    elif d == 0:
        print("ERROR: NO ECHO")
    else:
        print(f"VAL: {d:.1f} cm")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Timeout Speed**: If the timeout is too short, you will never see distances beyond 1 meter. 30ms is standard for ~5 meters of travel.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Average Filter**: Sum 10 readings and divide by 10 to smooth out small flickering values in the terminal.

---

## 1. Project 1232: Advanced Radar Screen Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Trigonometric Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to convert polar sensor coordinates (Angle + Range) into Cartesian display coordinates (X, Y) to render a live radar sweep on an OLED screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polar-to-Cartesian Math**: Using `sin()` and `cos()` to translate circular motion into a square screen grid.
*   **Offset Origin**: Shifting the (0,0) coordinate to the bottom-center of the display.
*   **Spatial Sampling**: Determining the "Radar Resolution" (degrees per step).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Ultrasonic Sensor & Mini Servo**
*   **OLED Display (SSD1306)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL** | GP4/GP5 | I2C Bus |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Scanning Motor |
| **Ultrasonic** | GP14/15 | Range Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `sine` / `cosine`** (conversion)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (sweep)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_pixel_at`** (plotting)

### 7. Variables
*   **angle**: Current degree (0-180).
*   **dist**: Distance (cm).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Movement**
1.  **Iterate**: From **Loops**, create a `for` loop from 0 to 180.
2.  **Move**: From **Motion & Motors**, **Set Servo** GP16 to `angle`.
3.  **Sample**: Fetch current `dist`.

**B. Trigonometry**
4.  **Radians**: Convert `rad = angle * (3.14 / 180)`.
5.  **Plotting**:
    *   X = 64 + (dist * cos(rad))
    *   Y = 60 - (dist * sin(rad))

**C. Visual Update**
6.  **Refresh**: From **Smart Display**, **Draw Pixel** at (X, Y).
7.  **Auto-Clear**: Every 180 degrees, fill the screen with black to start a new scan.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Sweep**: The servo begins a slow arc from left to right.
2.  **Visualization**: Points appear on the OLED, drawing a real-time "map" of your environment.
3.  **Result**: A professional-looking radar interface with physical world correlation.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, math
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)

def set_angle(a):
    duty = int(1638 + (a * (8192-1638)/180))
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    oled.fill(0)
    for a in range(0, 181, 4):
        set_angle(a)
        utime.sleep_ms(50)
        # Mock distance for demo (replace with echo sensor)
        d = 20 + 10 * math.sin(a/10) 
        
        rad = math.radians(a)
        x = int(64 + d * math.cos(rad))
        y = int(60 - d * math.sin(rad))
        
        oled.pixel(x, y, 1)
        oled.show()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flipped Y**: Remember that Y=0 is the TOP of the screen. To draw UP, you must subtract your calculation from the bottom coordinate.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Range Rings**: Draw semi-circles at 10cm, 20cm, and 30cm to help estimate object distance visually.
    if d == 0:
        print("ERROR: Field Timeout (Sensor Air Gap?)")
    elif d < 2.5:
        print("ALERT: Sensor Blinded/Jammed!")
    else:
        print(f"RAD_LINK: {d:.2f} cm")
        
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Voltage Logic**: HC-SR04 output (Echo) is 5V. The Pico is 3.3V. Using a resistive voltage divider (1k and 2k resistors) on the Echo pin is critical to prevent damage.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Averaging**: Take 5 pings and discard the highest/lowest to remove atmospheric glitches.

## 1. Project 1232: Advanced Radar Screen Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Trigonometric Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to convert polar sensor coordinates (Angle + Range) into Cartesian display coordinates (X, Y) to render a live radar sweep on an OLED screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polar to Cartesian Conversion**: Using Sine and Cosine to translate circular movement into a square grid.
*   **Coordinate Offsetting**: Shifting the "Origin" (0,0) to the bottom-center of the screen.
*   **Spatial Sampling**: Determining the resolution (number of degrees per scan step) for a smooth UI.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **OLED Display (SSD1306)**
*   **Mini Servo**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL** | GP4/GP5 | I2C Comm |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Scan Motor |
| **SR04 Trig/Echo**| GP14/15 | Distance input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`sine`**, **`cosine`**
*   From **Smart Display**, drag **`set_pixel`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`set_servo_angle`**

### 7. Variables
*   `rad_angle`: The angle converted from degrees to radians for Python's math library.
*   `plot_x`, `plot_y`: The calculated pixel positions.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Movement Setup**:
    *   Sweep the servo from **0** to **180** in increments of **2 degrees**.
2.  **Distance Capture**:
    *   At each step, take an ultrasonic reading.
3.  **Coordinate Math**:
    *   Convert degrees to radians: `rad = deg * 3.14159 / 180`.
    *   Calculate X: `x = 64 + (distance * cos(rad))`.
    *   Calculate Y: `y = 63 - (distance * sin(rad))`.
4.  **Draw Frame**:
    *   Keep old pixels on screen (don't clear every pixel) to show the "field". Only clear the "sweep line".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Sweep**: The servo turns left to right.
2.  **Plotting**: Green (or white) dots appear on the OLED forming the shapes of the objects in front of the sensor.
3.  **Result**: The screen looks like a professional submarine radar.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

# Setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)

def set_servo(deg):
    duty = int(1638 + (deg * (8192-1638)/180))
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

def plot_radar(deg, dist):
    # Scale distance (max 40cm for display)
    if dist > 40: dist = 40
    r = (dist / 40) * 30 # Max 30 pixels radius
    
    rad = math.radians(deg)
    # Center origin at (64, 60)
    x = int(64 + r * math.cos(rad))
    y = int(60 - r * math.sin(rad))
    
    oled.pixel(x, y, 1)

oled.fill(0)
oled.text("RADAR ACTIVE", 25, 30)
oled.show()
utime.sleep(1)

while True:
    oled.fill(0)
    # Draw base arc
    oled.hline(10, 60, 108, 1)
    
    # Forward Sweep
    for d in range(0, 181, 5):
        set_servo(d)
        # In a real setup, call get_distance() here
        # Simulating distance for demo:
        dist = 20 + 10 * math.sin(d/10) 
        plot_radar(d, dist)
        oled.show()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Conversion**: `oled.pixel` requires whole numbers. If you forget to wrap your trig results in `int()`, the code will throw an error.
*   **Origin Flipping**: In graphics, Y increases *downwards*. To make the radar look correct, you must subtract the Y calculation from the bottom coordinate (e.g., `63 - Y`).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ghost Line**: Draw a line from the center to the current scan point that fades away.

## 1. Project 1233: Advanced Radar Screen Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Consistency Filtering". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Ghost Rejection" by only displaying objects that appear in the same position across consecutive sweeps.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistence Filtering**: Storing previous data frames to verify current readings.
*   **Multipath Interference**: Understanding how sound can "bounce" off walls and create phantom objects.
*   **List Buffering**: Using arrays to store a 180-degree spatial map in memory.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Scanning Radar Assembly**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Motor Control |
| **Ultrasonic** | GP14/15 | Range Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `create_empty_list`** (memory)
*   **from Lists, drag `set_list_item`** (save)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_abs_diff`** (comparison)

### 7. Variables
*   **history**: A list of last-known distances.
*   **current**: The new reading from the sensor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Allocate**: Create `history` with 36 items (all zeros).

**B. Audit Phase**
2.  **Sample**: As the radar spins, get the `current` distance for angle `i`.
3.  **Consistency**: From **Logic**, check if `abs(current - history[i]) < 5`:
    *   **Valid**: If yes, draw the pixel (Object is stable).
    *   **Reject**: If no, object might be a "Ghost" or noise. Don't draw yet.

**C. Update**
4.  **Save**: Set `history[i]` = `current` to prepare for the next sweep.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Flickering**: A small fly passes through the beam once. The radar ignores it because it won't be there on the next pass.
2.  **Stable**: A box sits on the floor. It passes the check every time and appears solid on the screen.
3.  **Result**: A jitter-free UI that only shows real obstacles.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

history = [0] * 37 # 5-deg steps
trig = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def get_dist():
    # standard echo code...
    return 10.0 # Mock

while True:
    for i in range(37):
        ang = i * 5
        curr = get_dist()
        
        # Only print stable data (Ghost Rejection)
        if abs(curr - history[i]) < 3:
            print(f"STABLE_OBJ: {ang} deg, {curr} cm")
            
        history[i] = curr
        utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Resolution Drift**: If the servo isn't precise, `history[i]` will compare the 90-degree spot in Sweep 1 to the 92-degree spot in Sweep 2, causing valid objects to be rejected. Use a high-quality servo.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Heat Mapping**: If an object appears 5 times in a row, change its color or brightness on the screen.

## 1. Project 1234: Advanced Radar Screen Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Critical Sector Alert". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement sector-based status monitoring where the center of the display changes color or displays text depending on which "Slice" of the radar field is obstructed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Field Segmentation**: Dividing a 180-degree fov into "Left", "Center", and "Right" sectors.
*   **Stateful UI**: Changing the interface layout dynamically based on external sensor events.
*   **Proximity Priority**: Learning how to prioritize the closest object in the field.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Scanning Radar**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED** | GP4/GP5 | Status Feed |
| **Radar** | GP14/15 | Proximity Data |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_text_row`** (Alerts)
*   **from Logic, drag `switch_case`** (Sector Logic)
*   **from Math, drag `get_min_value`** (Closest)

### 7. Variables
*   **sector**: Current slice (0=Left, 1=Center, 2=Right).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Logic Setup**
1.  **Divide**: 0-60 (Left), 61-120 (Center), 121-180 (Right).

**B. Event Monitoring**
2.  **Detect**: If any distance in a sector is < 20cm:
    *   **Alert**: From **Variables**, set `sector_flag` to the active zone.

**C. UI Rendering**
3.  **Process Alerts**: From **Logic**, check `sector_flag`:
    *   **Case 0**: **Draw Text** "OBSTRUCTION LEFT".
    *   **Case 1**: **Draw Text** "!!! WARNING CENTER !!!".
    *   **Case 2**: **Draw Text** "OBSTRUCTION RIGHT".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: OLED shows "SWEEPING... ALL CLEAR".
2.  **Event**: You wave your hand in the center of the arc.
3.  **Alert**: The OLED instantly flashes "!!! WARNING CENTER !!!" in big letters.
4.  **Result**: Faster human reaction time to specific spatial threats.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)

def alert_ui(msg):
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("SEC_ALERT", 30, 0)
    oled.text(msg, 0, 30)
    oled.show()

while True:
    for a in range(0, 181, 10):
        d = 40 # get_dist()
        if a == 90 and d < 20: 
            alert_ui("BLOCKAGE: CTR")
        elif a < 60 and d < 20:
            alert_ui("BLOCKAGE: LEFT")
        elif a > 120 and d < 20:
            alert_ui("BLOCKAGE: RIGHT")
        utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **UI Lag**: Calling `oled.show()` inside every degree will make the radar extremely slow. Only update the UI every 10-20 degrees.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Direction Arrow**: Draw an arrow on the screen pointing to where the last breach occurred.

## 1. Project 1235: Automated Advanced Radar Screen Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Autonomous Radar Patrol". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a closed-loop control system where the radar "Locks" onto a moving target, automatically slowing its sweep to track an object that enters its field.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Interest-Based Scanning**: Prioritizing data collection for active zones while ignoring empty ones.
*   **PID-Light Tracking**: Adapting move speed based on the "delta" (change) in sensor readings.
*   **State Machine Management**: Switching between "Search" (Fast Sweep) and "Track" (Slow Point) modes.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Scanning Radar Assembly**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo** | GP16 | Targeting Motor |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Lock-On Tone |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_speed`** (dynamic)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_triggered`** (lock)
*   **from Variables, drag `toggle_boolean`** (mode switch)

### 7. Variables
*   **tracking_mode**: True if an object is near.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Search Mode**
1.  **Fast Sweep**: Sweep the servo at 200ms per 180 degrees.

**B. Lock-On Logic**
2.  **Trigger**: If `dist` < 30cm:
    *   **Switch Mode**: Set `tracking_mode` = True.
    *   **Sound**: Beep GP14.

**C. Tracking Mode**
3.  **Sustained Observation**: When locked, reduce sweep speed to 5ms per degree.
4.  **Auto-Release**: If the object disappears for 2 seconds, return to **Search Mode**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Patrol**: The radar head zips back and forth searching.
2.  **Intercept**: You put a soda can in front of it.
3.  **Lock**: The radar immediately slows down, "staring" at the can and chirping the buzzer.
4.  **Result**: Intelligent behavior that focuses computer power where it matters most.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)
tracking = False

def set_a(a):
    servo.duty_u16(int(1638 + (a * 6554 / 180)))

while True:
    step = 10 if not tracking else 2
    delay = 0.02 if not tracking else 0.1
    
    for a in range(0, 181, step):
        set_a(a)
        d = 50 # replace with dist read
        
        if d < 30:
            tracking = True
            print("TARGET LOCKED")
            # Focus on this area...
        else:
            tracking = False
            
        utime.sleep(delay)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Over-Focus**: If your tracking mode is too slow, a fast object will escape the field before the radar can see it again. Always keep some "patrol" movement.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Double Lock**: If two objects appear, program the radar to toggle between them every 2 seconds.
## 1. Project 1236: Advanced Radar Screen Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Software Geo-Fencing". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an invisible safety perimeter where an alarm triggers only if an object enters a specific coordinate-defined "Danger Zone" while ignoring activity in "Safe Zones".

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Geo-Fencing Logic**: Creating virtual boundaries based on distance thresholds and angular sectors.
*   **Sector Exclusion**: Monitoring specific angles (e.g., a doorway) while ignoring static obstacles (like a nearby wall).
*   **Safety Interlocking**: Using a radar scan to trigger a hardware-level emergency stop or alarm.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Scanning Radar Assembly**
*   **Buzzer & Alarm LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Radar Trig/Echo** | GP14/15 | Input |
| **Alarm LED** | GP13 | Visual Status |
| **Buzzer** | GP12 | Audio Warning |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`** (zone check)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `stop_motors`** (interlock)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (alert)

### 7. Variables
*   **danger_dist**: The threshold for the fence (e.g., 50cm).
*   **is_breached**: Boolean state flag.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Zone Definition**
1.  **Define Sector**: Initialize a sweep from 30 to 150 degrees (ignoring the area directly behind the sensor).

**B. Monitoring**
2.  **Continuous Comparison**: As the radar spins, check every individual ping against `danger_dist`.
3.  **Filtered Trigger**: From **Logic**, if `dist` < 50 AND `angle` > 45 AND `angle` < 135:
    *   **Action**: Set `is_breached` = True.

**C. Response Flow**
4.  **Alarm**: From **Smart IO**, **Set GP13** and **GP12** HIGH.
5.  **Auto-Reset**: After 5 seconds of "All Clear" readings, turn the alarm off.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Patrol**: The radar head spins. LED is GREEN.
2.  **Incursion**: You step within 40cm.
3.  **Emergency**: The system identifies the breach in the "Center Sector", stops any motors, and sounds the siren.
4.  **Result**: High-intelligence security that only guards where instructed.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

led = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.OUT)
buzz = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.OUT)
LIMIT = 50

def alert(state):
    led.value(state); buzz.value(state)

print("Geo-Fence Perimeter ARMING...")

while True:
    for a in range(30, 151, 10):
        # Move servo to 'a'
        d = 70 # replace with get_dist()
        
        # Simulated intruder
        if a == 90: d = 25 
        
        if d < LIMIT:
            print(f"!!! BREACH AT {a} DEGREE !!!")
            alert(True)
            utime.sleep(1)
            alert(False)
        else:
            alert(False)
            
        utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blind Spots**: If your sweep is too slow, a person can run through the "Danger Zone" between pulses. Increase scanning resolution or speed for high-security areas.

## 1. Project 1237: Advanced Radar Screen Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Spatial Buffer Archiving". At the end of this project, you will be able to collect and store high-frequency radar datasets in the Pico's RAM to capture a "Time-Slice" of environmental movement for forensic analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Data Buffering**: Temporarily holding high-speed data in lists before processing.
*   **Sampling Intervals**: Determining how many "Snapshots" (full sweeps) per minute are needed to capture a scene.
*   **RAM Limits**: Calculating the memory footprint of a multi-dimensional radar dataset.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Scanning Radar**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Radar Assembly** | GP14/15/16 | Data Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `create_2d_list`** (archive)
*   **from Lists, drag `append_to_list`** (snapshot)
*   **from Loops, drag `repeat_x_times`** (loop duration)

### 7. Variables
*   **archive**: A 2D list storing [[Sweep1], [Sweep2]...].

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Buffer Allocation**
1.  **Initialize**: Create an empty list `archive`.

**B. Data Capture**
2.  **Perform Sweep**: Run a loop that collects 18 distance points (0-180 degrees).
3.  **Store Frame**: Set `snapshot` = [d1, d2, d3...].
4.  **Archive**: From **Lists**, **Append** `snapshot` to `archive`.

**C. Maintenance**
5.  **Dumping**: After 10 sweeps, loop through `archive` and **Print** each frame as a CSV row for your computer to read.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Record**: You move an object across the floor for 30 seconds.
2.  **Dump**: The Pico terminal suddenly spits out a long list of numbers.
3.  **Result**: You can copy this data into Excel to "re-play" the movement on a graph later.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, gc

archive = []

def capture_scan():
    frame = []
    for a in range(0, 181, 20):
        # Move and read...
        frame.append(45) # Mock
    return frame

print("Recording Started. Capture 10 frames...")

for i in range(10):
    start = utime.ticks_ms()
    scan = capture_scan()
    archive.append(scan)
    print(f"Frame {i+1} saved.")
    
    while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 1000:
        pass

print("SCAN DUMP:")
for idx, f in enumerate(archive):
    print(f"F{idx}:{','.join(map(str, f))}")

print(f"Free Memory: {gc.mem_free()} bytes")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Overload**: If you try to store 10,000 frames, the Pico will crash. Always monitor `gc.mem_free()` during long recordings.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Triggered Recording**: Only start the archive when an object is detected within 10cm.

---

## 1. Project 1238: Smart Advanced Radar Screen System

### 2. Learning Objective
"USB Telemetry Bridge". At the end of this project, you will be able to synchronize the Pico with a desktop computer, streaming real-time spatial data over USB to be rendered by high-performance PC software.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Serial Data Streaming**: Converting hardware readings into a formatted CSV/JSON stream for external PCs.
*   **Baud Rate Optimization**: Ensuring the connection (115200 bps) can handle high-resolution radar sweeps.
*   **Client-Server Handshaking**: Coordinating the start and stop of scanning via PC commands.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico & USB Cable**
*   **Scanning Radar Assembly**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **USB Port** | Micro-USB | Data Path |
| **Radar** | GP14/15/16 | Input Hardware |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_serial_print`** (streaming)
*   **from Text, drag `text_join`** (packetizing)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (sweep)

### 7. Variables
*   **packet**: The "Angle,Distance" string sent to the PC.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Streaming Protocol**
1.  **Format**: For every 2 degrees of movement, set `packet` = "angle,distance".

**B. Transmission**
2.  **Blast Data**: From **Comm**, **Print** `packet` to the terminal.
3.  **Synchronization**: Ensure a small 20ms delay exists between packets to prevent USB buffer overflow.

**C. Visualization**
4.  **PC Viewer**: Use a Python script (on your PC) to read the COM port and plot the data using `matplotlib`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Hardware**: The Pico sweeps back and forth.
2.  **Streaming**: The terminal blurs with rapid coordinates.
3.  **Software**: On your PC, a high-resolution radar screen appears with glowing paths and distance markers!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

# PICO SIDE SENDER
# Standard print in MicroPython defaults to 115200

while True:
    for a in range(0, 181, 2):
        # Move servo to 'a'
        d = 25 # replace with get_dist()
        
        # Format for PC parsing: "angle,distance"
        print(f"{a},{d}")
        utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Port Blocking**: You cannot have Thonny and your PC script open at the same time. Close Thonny's serial monitor before running your PC side plotter.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Binary Pack**: Use `struct.pack` to send raw bytes instead of text for even higher speed transmission.

## 1. Project 1239: Advanced Radar Screen Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Map Accuracy Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to identify and correct "Angular Distortion" (Map Smear) caused by the latency between issuing a servo command and the ultrasonic sensor firing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Settling Time Math**: Calculating the delay required for a servo to physically reach a position before triggering a ping.
*   **Angular Lag**: Correcting for "trails" in the radar map that occur when scanning too fast.
*   **Map Alignment**: Ensuring 90 degrees on the screen exactly matches 90 degrees in the physical room.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Radar Assembly**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo Motor** | GP16 | Speed Subject |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_calculate_lag`** (Audit)
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (Report)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`** (Timer)

### 7. Variables
*   **test_delay**: The pause (ms) between mechanical steps.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Benchmark Setup**
1.  **Target**: Place a box exactly at the 90-degree mark.

**B. Speed Ramp Audit**
2.  **Iterate**: Run 5 full sweeps, starting with a 100ms step-delay and decreasing to 5ms.
3.  **Observe**: Note at what speed the box on the screen starts "shifting" away from the 90-degree line.

**C. Selection**
4.  **Optimal Lock**: Identify the fastest delay that maintains +/- 2 degree accuracy.
5.  **Save**: Use this `test_delay` as the standard for all future radar projects.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Test 1 (Slow)**: Map is perfect but sluggish.
2.  **Test 2 (Fast)**: The box looks like a "smear" across 15 degrees.
3.  **Result**: Your code prints "OPTIMAL STEP DELAY FOUND: 22ms".
4.  **Result**: Maximum scanning performance without sacrificing accuracy.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

# Speed Audit Sequence
speeds = [100, 50, 25, 10, 5]

print("--- Radar Speed vs Accuracy Audit ---")

for s in speeds:
    print(f"\nStep Delay: {s}ms")
    for a in range(0, 181, 10):
        # set_servo(a)
        utime.sleep_ms(s) # Settling
        # d = read_dist()
        if a == 90:
            print("Audit Spot Check (90 deg)...")
    utime.sleep(1)

print("Calibration Complete.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Inertia**: Cheaper servos have "Over-shoot" where they swing past the target angle before settling. If your audit is too fast, your map will always be biased to one side.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Software Bias**: If you know the lag is 5 degrees, subtract 5 from the `angle` in your display math to fix it without slowing the motor!

## 1. Project 1240: Advanced Advanced Radar Screen Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Lidar Pathfinding Simulation". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a "Virtual Robot Navigator" that uses stored radar map data to calculate the safest path through a maze without physical sensors.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Algorithm Prototyping**: Testing high-level logic (navigation) using "Digital Twins" of the real world.
*   **Spatial Averaging**: Smoothing multi-beam readings to identify the widest "Clear Opening".
*   **Closed-Loop Virtual Control**: Updating a simulated position based on simulated sensor data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL** | GP4/GP5 | Navigation Readout |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `set_list`** (Virtual Room)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else_compare`** (Decision)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_text`** (Current Path)

### 7. Variables
*   **virtual_map**: A list representing walls (20) and openings (100).
*   **heading**: The index the virtual bot is currently facing.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. World Building**
1.  **Define Maze**: Create `virtual_map` with 18 indices. Use 20 for "Walls" and 100 for "Empty Floor".

**B. Navigation Brain**
2.  **Sample Environment**: Check indices `heading-1`, `heading`, and `heading+1`.
3.  **Choose Path**: From **Logic**, if the center is blocked (20), check if the Left or Right has a higher number (more space).

**C. Visualization**
4.  **Display Status**: On the OLED, print "PATH: CLEAR" or "TURN INITIATED".
5.  **Move**: Iteratively update the `heading` based on the logic to find the "Doorway" in the virtual room.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The bot starts facing a "Virtual Wall".
2.  **Calculation**: It "scans" its memory and identifies an opening to the left.
3.  **Action**: Its heading updates.
4.  **Result**: You have successfully developed an autopilot brain before your hardware even arrives!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

# Simulated environment: 20=Wall, 50=Clear
world = [50, 50, 20, 20, 50, 50, 50, 50, 10, 50, 50, 50, 50, 20, 20, 50]
heading = 8 # Facing center

print("--- Autonomous Lidar Simulation Online ---")

while True:
    print(f"Angle: {heading*10} deg")
    
    # 3-Beam Snapshot
    l, c, r = world[heading-1], world[heading], world[heading+1]
    
    if c < 30: # Blocked center
        print("OBSTACLE DETECTED. Choosing side...")
        if l > r:
            print("Decision: TURN LEFT")
            heading += 1
        else:
            print("Decision: TURN RIGHT")
            heading -= 1
    else:
        print("Decision: ALL CLEAR - PROCEED")
        
    heading = max(1, min(14, heading)) # Bounds check
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Oscillation**: If the bot sees two identical openings, it might toggle between them forever. Add a "Biased toward Right" rule to break ties.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Obstacles**: Randomized one of the `world` indices every 5 seconds to simulate a person walking through your maze.

## Batch 125: Advanced Flame Sensor 3 (P1241-P1250)

## 1. Project 1241: Advanced Flame Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Analog Sensitivity Profiling". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a calibration routine that maps the physical distance of a flame to its analog intensity values, allowing for distance-aware fire detection.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Intensity Scaling**: Understanding that IR intensity follows the Inverse Square Law (doubling distance quarters the signal).
*   **ADC Noise Floor**: Identifying and ignoring the "jitter" in readings that occurs when no flame is present.
*   **Dynamic Calibration**: Setting a "Baseline" reading based on the current room lighting before monitoring for fire.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor (IR Photodiode)**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor AO** | GP26 | Analog Input (ADC0) |
| **Sensor DO** | GP14 | Digital Input (Threshold) |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Audio Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_analog_pin`** (Intensity)
*   **from Math, drag `map`** (Normalization)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater`** (Trigger)

### 7. Variables
*   **flame_val**: The raw 0-65535 reading.
*   **baseline**: The ambient light level of the room.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Foundation**
1.  **Initialize**: Set **GP26** as an ADC input.
2.  **Calibration**: On startup, read **GP26** 10 times and average the result to set the `baseline`.

**B. Detection**
3.  **Pulse Check**: From **Smart Sensors**, read the current `flame_val`.
4.  **Offset Math**: Calculate `intensity = flame_val - baseline`.

**C. Feedback**
5.  **Distance Scaling**: From **Math**, map the `intensity` so that a value of 0 is "Safe" and 50000 is "CRITICAL".
6.  **Alert**: If `intensity` is greater than 10000, beep the buzzer at a frequency proportional to the heat.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Prep**: The code runs and "learns" the room's brightness.
2.  **Ignite**: You light a lighter 30cm away.
3.  **Response**: The terminal prints "Intensity: 15% - Remote Detection".
4.  **Proximity**: You move the flame closer. The buzzer frequency increases as the intensity reaches 90%.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

sensor = machine.ADC(26)
buzz = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Calibrating Baseline IR... Keep lighting steady.")
sum_val = 0
for _ in range(10):
    sum_val += sensor.read_u16()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
baseline = sum_val / 10
print(f"Room Baseline: {baseline}")

while True:
    val = sensor.read_u16()
    # Flame sensors often pull LOW when they see fire
    # but we will calculate difference from baseline
    diff = abs(val - baseline)
    
    if diff > 5000:
        print(f"FLAME DETECTED! Delta: {diff}")
        buzz.toggle()
        utime.sleep(0.1)
    else:
        buzz.off()
        utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Inversion**: Many flame sensors output a *lower* voltage when fire is detected (Active LOW). If your code shows 0% with a flame and 100% in the dark, subtract your reading from 65535.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Baseline**: Update the baseline every hour to adjust for the sun moving through the room.

---

## 1. Project 1242: Advanced Flame Sensor Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dual-Stage Intensity Analysis". At the end of this project, you will be able to distinguish between low-level IR interference and a dangerous "Point-Source Flame" using a dual-threshold state machine.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Normalization**: Scaling non-linear analog inputs to a 0-100 percentage.
*   **Hysteresis Recovery**: Ensuring that once an alarm is triggered, it doesn't rapidly blink on and off at the threshold boundary.
*   **State Machines**: Transitioning from "Clear" to "Caution" to "EMERGENCY".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor**
*   **RGB LED (Common Cathode)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Flame AO** | GP26 | Intensity Source |
| **LED Green** | GP16 | Safe Indicator |
| **LED Red** | GP17 | Danger Indicator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else_if`** (state logic)
*   **from Math, drag `pico_map_range`** (normalization)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (status)

### 7. Variables
*   **threat_level**: 0 (None), 1 (Suspicious), 2 (Critical).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**B. Logic Construction**
1.  **Read Signal**: From **Smart Sensors**, get the analog value from GP26.
2.  **Level Check**: From **Logic**, if value > 20000 AND value < 40000, set `threat_level` = 1.
3.  **Emergency**: If value >= 40000, set `threat_level` = 2.

**C. Output Routing**
4.  **Green State**: If level = 0, turn on GP16 (Green LED).
5.  **Caution State**: If level = 1, flash Red/Green (Amber warning).
6.  **Critical State**: If level = 2, turn on GP17 (Solid Red) and stop all other processes.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Idle**: Sensor sees ambient light. Green LED is active.
2.  **Match**: You light a match. The signal hits 30,000. LED flashes Amber.
3.  **Torch**: You bring a torch closer. Signal hits 50,000. RED ALERT active.
4.  **Result**: Multi-tier security that doesn't panic over small sparks.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

sensor = machine.ADC(26)
g = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
r = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

while True:
    raw = sensor.read_u16()
    # Normalize (Assuming 0 is dark, 65535 is high intensity)
    intensity = (raw / 65535) * 100
    
    if intensity > 60:
        print("!!! CRITICAL FIRE !!!")
        r.on(); g.off()
    elif intensity > 20:
        print("CAUTION: Heat Source Detected")
        r.on(); g.on() # Pseudo-amber
    else:
        r.off(); g.on()
        
    utime.sleep(0.2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Daylight interference**: If you put your sensor near a window, the sun might push your intensity to 30% automatically. Always shield the sensor with a small hood to direct its "vision".

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Smoothing**: Instead of reacting to one reading, use the average of 5 readings to prevent "jitter" alerts.

---

## 1. Project 1243: Advanced Flame Sensor Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"False Alert Rejection". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement temporal verification logic that requires a flame signal to remain consistently active for 2 seconds before escalating to a "Confirmed Fire" state, effectively ignoring lighter flickers or reflections.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Verification Window**: Requiring a sustained event rather than a momentary peak.
*   **Successive Hits Counter**: Counting how many sequential samples pass the threshold.
*   **Debounce Analogy**: Applying high-level logic (similar to switch debouncing) to IR light signals.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor**
*   **High-Decibel Siren / Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor Digital** | GP14 | Trigger Edge |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Alarm Port |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Loops, drag `repeat_while`** (verification loop)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`** (condition)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`** (timer)

### 7. Variables
*   **conf_timer**: Stores the start time of a suspicious event.
*   **is_verified**: Flag for confirmed fire.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Event Detect**: Set up an interrupt or loop to monitor GP14.
2.  **Start Clock**: If GP14 goes HIGH, save the current time in `conf_timer`.

**B. Verification Phase**
3.  **Hold State**: Enter a loop that waits for 2000ms.
4.  **Audit**: During this wait, if GP14 ever goes LOW (flame disappears), set `conf_timer` to 0 and exit.

**C. Fire Escalate**
5.  **Trigger**: If 2000ms passes and `conf_timer` is still active:
    *   **Action**: Sound the HIGH intensity buzzer.
    *   **Lock**: Do not turn the buzzer off until a physical "Reset" button is pressed.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Flicker**: You flick a lighter on and off quickly. The Pico notices but does nothing.
2.  **Burn**: You hold the flame steady for 3 seconds.
3.  **Result**: At 2 seconds, the alarm screams.
4.  **Security**: This prevents your oven pilots or sunlight flashes from waking you up at night.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

flame = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
siren = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Fire Watchdog Armed. Waiting for 2s sustained signal...")

while True:
    if flame.value() == 1:
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        verified = True
        
        # 2 Second Verification Loop
        while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 2000:
            if flame.value() == 0:
                print("Intermittent signal ignored.")
                verified = False
                break
            utime.sleep(0.1)
            
        if verified:
            print("!!! CONFIRMED FIRE DETECTED !!!")
            while True:  # LOCK STATE
                siren.toggle()
                utime.sleep(0.05)
                
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Main**: Don't use `utime.sleep(2)` for the verification, as you can't check if the flame disappears during that sleep. Use the `ticks_diff` loop shown above.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Sensor Voting**: Only sound the alarm if *two* different flame sensors in the room verify the signal.

---

## 1. Project 1244: Advanced Flame Sensor Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Live Threat Intensity Display". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a real-time OLED bar graph that maps the proximity of a fire to a "Safety Meter," providing an instant visual readout of potential danger levels.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic UI Rendering**: Updating shapes and lines on an OLED screen based on live sensor variables.
*   **Visual Logic Mapping**: Associating a 16-bit analog value with a pixel width (0-128 pixels).
*   **Refresh Rate Sync**: Balancing display updates so they are smooth but don't slow down sensor polling.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor**
*   **OLED Display (128x64 I2C)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4/5 | Display Bus |
| **Flame AO** | GP26 | Data Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_rectangle`** (meter)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `clear_screen`**
*   **from Math, drag `pico_scale`** (logic)

### 7. Variables
*   **fire_intensity**: Measured value scaled 0-100.
*   **bar_width**: Calculated pixel length.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Screen Setup**: Clear the OLED and draw a "Static Frame" (a box representing the empty meter).

**B. Data Processing**
2.  **Sample Pulse**: From **Smart Sensors**, read the flame intensity.
3.  **Calculate Width**: Map the 0-65535 value to 0-110 pixels (leaving room for labels).

**C. Visual Render**
4.  **Draw Bar**: From **Smart Display**, draw a solid rectangle inside the frame using `bar_width`.
5.  **Text Overlay**: Print "SITUATION: SAFE" if width < 20, and "DANGER" if width > 90.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Startup**: The screen shows an empty bar.
2.  **Heat**: A candle is placed 1 meter away. The bar fills up roughly 20%.
3.  **Proximity**: As the candle moves closer, the bar grows in real-time.
4.  **Result**: An intuitive cockpit-style gauge for fire safety monitors.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, ssd1306

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
flame = machine.ADC(26)

while True:
    val = flame.read_u16()
    # Invert and scale (assuming 65k is max flame)
    # Most sensors: Low Volts = High Fire
    intensity = 100 - (val / 65535 * 100)
    
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("FLAME INTENSITY", 0, 0)
    
    # Draw Meter Frame
    oled.rect(5, 20, 118, 15, 1)
    
    # Draw Fill Bar
    fill = int(intensity * 1.1) # Scale to 110px
    oled.fill_rect(7, 22, fill, 11, 1)
    
    if intensity > 80:
        oled.text("!!! DANGER !!!", 10, 45)
    
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Screen Burn**: If the flame is static for hours, the bar might "ghost" on the OLED. Clear the screen fully every few minutes or implement a screensaver logic.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Axis**: Add a second bar showing the "History" or "Average" intensity next to the real-time one.

---

## 1. Project 1245: Automated Advanced Flame Sensor Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Active Suppression Orchestration". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a robotic fire-suppression rig that uses a servo to physically orient a "Fire Hose" toward the source of highest heat and activates a "Pump" relay when the flame is in range.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Closed-Loop Response**: Using real-time sensor feedback to control mechanical movement.
*   **Mapping Servo to Signal**: Using the analog intensity to determine how "close" the nozzle should pivot.
*   **Safety Interlocks**: Ensuring the "extinguishing agent" (relay/pump) only fires when the servo is correctly aligned.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor Array (or single sensor on a mount)**
*   **Standard Servo**
*   **5V Relay (Pump Simulation)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Aiming Mechanism |
| **Relay Signal** | GP17 | Suppression Activation |
| **Flame Input** | GP26 | Guidance Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`** (Nozzle Aim)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (Suppressor)
*   **from Math, drag `pico_abs_diff`** (Error Check)

### 7. Variables
*   **target_pos**: The angle the servo needs to reach.
*   **fire_confirmed**: Boolean flag for suppression trigger.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Aiming Phase**
1.  **Monitor Intensity**: Continuously read the flame sensor on GP26.
2.  **Logic**: From **Math**, map the flame intensity to a servo angle (e.g., 0-180 degrees). As the flame gets brighter/closer, pivot the servo to "cover the area".

**B. Suppression Phase**
3.  **Fire Trigger**: From **Logic**, if intensity > 90% AND servo angle is within 5 degrees of target:
    *   **Action**: Set GP17 to HIGH (Activate Pump).
4.  **Cool Down**: Once intensity drops below 20%, turn off the pump.

**C. Safety Check**
5.  **Timeout**: If the pump is active for more than 10 seconds, shut it down automatically to prevent flooding.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Scan**: The servo pivots slowly searching for heat.
2.  **Ignite**: A candle is lit. The servo "locks on" and points directly at it.
3.  **Extinguish**: The "Pump" relay clicks on, "spraying" the target.
4.  **Result**: An end-to-end automated robotic response to a fire threat.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)
pump = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)
flame = machine.ADC(26)

def set_angle(a):
    duty = int(1638 + (a * 6554 / 180))
    servo.duty_u16(duty)

while True:
    val = flame.read_u16()
    intensity = 100 - (val / 65535 * 100)
    
    # Simple logic: Pivot servo based on intensity
    # In a real 3-sensor setup, you'd pivot toward the strongest one
    target = intensity * 1.8 # Map 0-100% to 0-180 deg
    set_angle(target)
    
    if intensity > 85:
        print("AIM ACQUIRED. DISPENSING...")
        pump.on()
    else:
        pump.off()
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Servo Jitter**: If your flame reading is noisy, the servo will "shake". Add a `deadband` of 5 degrees so the servo only moves if the change is significant.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Sensor Aiming**: Use two flame sensors (Left and Right). If Left is stronger, turn servo Left. If Right is stronger, turn servo Right.

---

## 1. Project 1246: Advanced Flame Sensor Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Emergency Gas Interlock". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-priority hardware interlock that instantly disables a fuel valve (simulated by a relay) if a fire is detected near a burner or industrial machine.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fail-Safe Wiring**: Designing systems where the default state (power loss) is "Safe" (Valve Closed).
*   **Interrupt-Driven Shutdown**: Using GP14 (Digital Trigger) to cut power without waiting for the next CPU cycle.
*   **Latching Faults**: Requiring a technician to physically inspect and reset the system after a fire event.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor**
*   **5V Relay (Gas Valve Simulation)**
*   **Manual Reset Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay Signal** | GP15 | Valve Control (NC) |
| **Flame DO** | GP14 | Emergency Link |
| **Reset Button** | GP13 | System Clearance |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (Safety Cutoff)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`** (Fault Check)
*   **from Wait, drag `wait_until`** (Reset)

### 7. Variables
*   **system_lock**: Boolean blocking all fuel flow.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Open Valve**: Set GP15 to HIGH (Fuel Flowing).

**B. Detection**
2.  **Emergency Check**: From **Smart Sensors**, monitor GP14.
3.  **Trip Logic**: If `flame_detected` == True:
    *   **Action**: Set GP15 to LOW (Fuel CUT).
    *   **Action**: Set `system_lock` = True.

**C. Recovery**
4.  **Reset Phase**: From **Wait**, wait for the physical button on GP13 to be pressed.
5.  **Restart**: Only after the button is pressed AND the flame is gone, set `system_lock` = False and re-open the valve.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Run**: The machine operates. Fuel flows.
2.  **Accident**: A flare-up triggers the sensor.
3.  **Protect**: Within milliseconds, the relay clicks. Fuel stops instantly.
4.  **Result**: Industrial-grade safety logic that prevents minor fires from becoming disasters.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

valve = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
flame = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
reset = machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

is_tripped = False

def emergency_stop(p):
    global is_tripped
    valve.off()
    is_tripped = True
    print("!!! SAFETY INTERLOCK TRIPPED !!!")

# Hardware-level interrupt for zero-latency stop
flame.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_RISING, handler=emergency_stop)

while True:
    if not is_tripped:
        valve.on()
        print("SYSTEM_STATUS: FLOWING")
    else:
        print("SYSTEM_STATUS: LOCKED. Awaiting inspection...")
        if reset.value() == 0 and flame.value() == 0:
            print("Resetting safety relay...")
            is_tripped = False
            
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Soft Shutdown**: Never rely on a complex `if/else` loop for safety. Use interrupts (IRQs) to ensure the valve closes even if the main program is busy or lagging.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Remote Kill**: Add a second interrupt that allows a human supervisor to trip the system from 10 meters away using a secondary button.

---

## 1. Project 1247: Advanced Flame Sensor Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Fire Incident Forensic Logging". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated logging system that records the Peak Intensity, Duration, and Recovery Time of fire events into the Pico's memory for professional safety auditing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Event Partitioning**: Identifying when a "Fire Incident" starts and ends.
*   **Peak Hold Analysis**: Storing the single highest value recorded during a dynamic event.
*   **RAM Buffering vs Flash Persistence**: Understanding where to store critical event data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Flame Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Flame AO** | GP26 | Incident Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `append_to_list`** (Audit Trail)
*   **from Math, drag `max`** (Peak Hold)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`** (Duration)

### 7. Variables
*   **incident_log**: A 2D list: [[Peak, Time, Date]...].
*   **active_peak**: Temporary store for the current fire.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Trigger Check**
1.  **Monitor**: Watch GP26. If intensity > 50%, set `incident_start` = `ticks_ms()`.

**B. Data Capture (The Incident)**
2.  **Peak Detection**: As the fire burns, constantly update `active_peak` with the highest value seen.
3.  **Ending**: When intensity drops below 10% for 5 seconds, mark `incident_end`.

**C. Archiving**
4.  **Save Record**: Calculate `duration = (end - start) / 1000`.
5.  **Log**: From **Lists**, append [active_peak, duration] to `incident_log`.
6.  **Report**: Print "INCIDENT 01 RECORDED: Max Heat 92%, Duration 14s".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Watch**: Pico is in monitoring mode.
2.  **Event**: A small fire occurs. Pico logs data in the background.
3.  **Audit**: Later, you plug the Pico into a PC and read the `incident_log` to see exactly how intense the fire was and how long it lasted.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

sensor = machine.ADC(26)
log = []

while True:
    raw = sensor.read_u16()
    intensity = 100 - (raw / 65535 * 100)
    
    if intensity > 30:
        print("RECORDING INCIDENT...")
        peak = 0
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        while intensity > 15: # Loop while fire exists
            raw = sensor.read_u16()
            intensity = 100 - (raw / 65535 * 100)
            if intensity > peak: peak = intensity
            utime.sleep(0.1)
            
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) / 1000
        log.append([peak, duration])
        print(f"SAVED: Peak={peak:.1f}%, Time={duration:.1f}s")
        
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **False Endings**: Fire flickers. If you stop the log the moment the intensity drops to 14%, you might end up with 10 small logs for 1 fire. Add a "Hysteresis Delay" of 3-5 seconds before finalizing any incident.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Flash Storage**: Use `f = open('fire_logs.csv', 'a')` to write the data permanently to the Pico's disk so it survives a power outage.

---

## 1. Project 1248: Smart Advanced Flame Sensor System

### 2. Learning Objective
"IoT Cloud Fire Alert". At the end of this project, you will be able to connect the Pico W to a local WiFi network and push real-time emergency notifications to a web dashboard using the HTTP protocol.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Webhook Integration**: Sending a GET/POST request to a cloud service (like IFTTT or ThingSpeak) to trigger an SMS or Email.
*   **Network Time Synchronization**: Using NTP to ensure fire logs have accurate "Real World" timestamps.
*   **Async Network Polling**: Ensuring the flame sensor continues to operate while the WiFi is attempting to reconnect.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W**
*   **Flame Sensor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Flame DO** | GP14 | Trigger Source |
| **WiFi Antenna** | Internal | Built-in (CYW43) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Internet, drag `connect_wifi`**
*   **from Internet, drag `http_get_request`** (Alert)
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`**

### 7. Variables
*   **api_url**: The endpoint provided by your cloud service.
*   **is_notified**: Flag to prevent spamming the server.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Network Setup**
1.  **Link**: Import the `network` module and connect to your local SSID.
2.  **Verify**: Wait until `wlan.isconnected()` is True.

**B. Alert Logic**
3.  **Detect**: Monitor GP14. If a fire starts:
4.  **Construct URL**: Set `full_url` = `[API_URL] + "?status=FIRE_DETECTED"`.
5.  **Push**: From **Internet**, call **GET Request** using `full_url`.

**C. Feedback**
6.  **Status**: Print the server response (e.g., "200 OK") to confirm the alert reached the cloud.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Safety**: The Pico is quietly watching the workshop.
2.  **Incident**: A flame is detected.
3.  **Transmit**: The blue LED flashes. The Pico sends an internet packet.
4.  **Result**: Your phone vibrates with an alert: "!!! EMERGENCY: FIRE IN WORKSHOP !!!"

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network, urequests, utime, machine

WIFI = "SSID_HERE"
PASS = "PASS_HERE"
WEBHOOK = "http://maker.ifttt.com/trigger/fire/with/key/YOUR_KEY"

def connect():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect(WIFI, PASS)
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        print("Waiting for WiFi...")
        utime.sleep(1)
    print("Cloud Connection Established.")

flame = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
is_alert_sent = False

connect()

while True:
    if flame.value() == 1 and not is_alert_sent:
        print("PUSHING ALERT TO CLOUD...")
        try:
            r = urequests.get(WEBHOOK)
            print(f"Server Response: {r.status_code}")
            r.close()
            is_alert_sent = True
        except:
            print("Network Error.")
            
    if flame.value() == 0:
        is_alert_sent = False # Reset for next fire
        
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Alert**: If `urequests.get` hangs (bad WiFi), your local buzzer might not sound. Always wrap network calls in `try/except` and prioritize local hardware safety over internet alerts.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Heat Level Telemetry**: Instead of just "On/Off", send the actual analog intensity percentage to a graph on **ThingSpeak**.
