## 1. Project 1249: Advanced Flame Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Spectral Interference Subtraction". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a differential calibration that subtracts background infrared interference (sunlight/room lights) from your flame signal to achieve ultra-high sensitivity in complex environments.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential Sensing**: Comparing the fire-facing sensor against a secondary sensor facing away from the fire.
*   **Signal Bias Offsets**: Adjusting your code's threshold based on the "Room Noise" level measured at startup.
*   **Validation Audit**: Finding the exact point where a candle light exceeds the background "Glow" of the room.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 Flame Sensors** (One for flame, one for ambient baseline)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Flame Sensor 1** | GP26 (ADC0) | Directional Monitor |
| **Flame Sensor 2** | GP27 (ADC1) | Ambient Reference |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_subtract_bias`** (Filter)
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`** (Report)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_analog_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   **bias_val**: The constant IR level of the room.
*   **clean_signal**: The filtered intensity result.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Establish Baseline**
1.  **Measure Background**: Read the Ambient Sensor (GP27). This represents the room's current state.

**B. Filtering Logic**
2.  **Subtract**: From **Math**, let `clean_signal` = `Flame_Sensor - Ambient_Sensor`.
3.  **Positive Clamp**: If the result is negative (ambient is brighter), set it to 0.

**C. Verification Audit**
4.  **Report**: Print both `Raw` and `Clean` values. 
5.  **Observe**: Notice how `Clean` remains near 0 even when you turn on the overhead lights, but spikes immediately when a flame appears.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Startup**: Sensors initialize. Room is bright with sunlight.
2.  **Filter**: The Pico "Zeros out" the sunlight intensity.
3.  **Detection**: You light a candle. The filtered signal jumps from 0 to 45%.
4.  **Result**: High-precision fire detection that doesn't get "blinded" by the sun.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

fire_eye = machine.ADC(26)
ambient_eye = machine.ADC(27)

print("Starting Spectral Filter...")

while True:
    raw_fire = fire_eye.read_u16()
    raw_amb = ambient_eye.read_u16()
    
    # Simple subtraction filter
    # Assuming 0 is bright, 65k is dark for these sensors
    clean = max(0, raw_fire - raw_amb)
    
    print(f"RAW: {raw_fire} | AMB: {raw_amb} | CALIBRATED: {clean}")
    
    if clean > 10000:
        print("ALERT: SIGNAL DETECTED ABOVE AMBIENT FLOOR")
        
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Mismatch**: If one sensor is 10% more sensitive than the other, your "zero" will always be off. Use a calibration factor (multiplication) to align them when no flame is present.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Ratio**: Instead of subtracting, use Division. If `Fire / Ambient` > 1.2, trigger the alert.

---

## 1. Project 1250: Advanced Advanced Flame Sensor Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Triple-Sensor Fusion Simulation". At the end of this project, you will be able to replicate high-end industrial "IR3" flame detectors using a simulated sensor array on the Pico, requiring three independent sensors to "agree" before a fire is confirmed.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Redundancy**: The practice of using multiple identical sensors to improve reliability.
*   **Boolean Voting Systems**: Creating logic where a majority (or 100%) of sensors must be active to trigger an alarm.
*   **False-Positive Rejection (Non-Fire Sources)**: How industrial sensors ignore welding or sunlight by looking for a "Shared Hit".

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4/5 | Alert Monitor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and_multi`** (voting)
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_status_icon`** (health)
*   **from Chips, drag `pico_wait`** (sync)

### 7. Variables
*   **sensor_votes**: Integer tracking how many sensors see fire.
*   **health_check**: Array of status flags for each "virtual" sensor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Virtual Setup**: Initialize 3 logic states for Sensor A, B, and C.

**B. Voting Logic**
2.  **Poll Beams**: Check all three virtual inputs.
3.  **Vote Counter**: Increment `sensor_votes` for every active sensor.
4.  **Majority Rule**: From **Logic**, only if `sensor_votes` >= 3, set `FIRE_CONFIRMED` = True.

**C. Visualization**
5.  **Screen Report**: On the OLED, draw three boxes. Fill them solid when the corresponding sensor sees "Heat".
6.  **Siren**: Sound the alarm only when and only when all three boxes are filled.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Glint**: A reflection hits Sensor A. Box A fills. No alarm sounds.
2.  **Interference**: Sensor B sees a bright light. Box B fills. Still no alarm.
3.  **Disaster**: A real fire starts. All 3 sensors (A, B, and C) hit the threshold.
4.  **Result**: The siren sounds instantly. You have achieved 99.9% false-alarm rejection!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

# Simulated IR3 Sensor logic
def check_sensors():
    # In reality, these would be 3 separate ADC pins
    s1, s2, s3 = (0, 0, 0) # Mock
    
    # Simulation: fire detected only if all 3 logic gates pass
    # (e.g., different IR frequencies)
    return s1 + s2 + s3

print("--- Industrial IR3 Flame Detector Online ---")
print("Rule: 3-of-3 voting required for Alert.")

while True:
    # Simulating a fire event occurring
    fire_strength = 3 # change to 1 or 2 to see the reject logic
    
    if fire_strength >= 3:
        print("!!! ALERT: TRIPLE SENSOR VERIFICATION CONFIRMED !!!")
        print("ACTION: FIRE SUPPRESSION TRIGGERED")
    elif fire_strength > 0:
        print(f"Warning: Interference detected on {fire_strength} sensor(s). REJECTED.")
    else:
        print("Monitoring... Sensors clear.")
        
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Logic Over-Tightening**: Requiring 3-of-3 sensors to hit 100% exactly is too restrictive. Usually, a 2-of-3 vote is used in industrial settings to allow for one sensor failure while still being extremely safe.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **2-of-3 Voting**: Modify your logic block so that if any *two* sensors see fire, the alarm sounds, but single-sensor hits are still ignored.

## 1. Project 1251: Advanced Sound Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Acoustic Noise Floor Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to initialize a high-sensitivity microphone module and perform a statistical sampling routine to define the "Room Silence" level, ensuring that subsequent sound triggers are accurately offset from background noise.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Peak-to-Peak Sampling**: Calculating the difference between the highest and lowest voltage points in a sound wave to determine amplitude.
*   **Acoustic Offset**: Subtracting persistent background hum (like a fan) from your software's detection logic.
*   **Fast Sampling**: Reading the ADC at high speeds to capture sound waves before they vanish.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor (MAX4466 or similar Analog Mic)**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic VCC** | 3.3V | Power |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Analog ADC0 |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Digital Out |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_peak_to_peak`**
*   **from Math, drag `pico_average_list`**
*   **from Comm, drag `print_labeled`**

### 7. Variables
*   **noise_floor**: The average amplitude of a quiet room.
*   **sample_list**: A buffer of 100 fast readings.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Quiet Calibration**: On boot, wait 2 seconds while the room is quiet. Use a loop to take 20 samples of the "Peak-to-Peak" amplitude.
2.  **Average Logic**: Calculate the `noise_floor` by dividing the sum of samples by 20.

**B. Active Monitoring**
3.  **Fast Loop**: Take a continuous stream of readings.
4.  **Offset Math**: Subtract the `noise_floor` from the current reading to get the "Clean Signal".
5.  **Trigger**: If the "Clean Signal" exceeds 200 (a sharp noise), toggle GP15 (Buzzer).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: Pico prints "Analyzing room acoustics... quiet please."
2.  **Baseline**: It locks in a floor of 45 units.
3.  **Detect**: You speak. The signal hits 300. The buzzer beeps.
4.  **Result**: An acoustic gate that ignores the hum of an air conditioner but catches a human voice.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
buzz = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

def get_peak_to_peak(duration_ms):
    mx = 0
    mn = 65535
    end = utime.ticks_ms() + duration_ms
    while utime.ticks_ms() < end:
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
        if v < mn: mn = v
    return mx - mn

print("Calibrating Noise Floor...")
floor = 0
for _ in range(20):
    floor += get_peak_to_peak(50)
floor /= 20
print(f"Room Baseline: {floor:.0f}")

while True:
    signal = get_peak_to_peak(50)
    clean = max(0, signal - floor)
    
    if clean > 1000:
        print(f"NOISE DETECTED: {clean}")
        buzz.on(); utime.sleep(0.1); buzz.off()
        
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Slow Polling**: Sound moves fast. If you only read the mic once every 10ms, you will miss the "peaks" of the wave. You must use a tight `while` loop to capture the true amplitude.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Floor**: Update the `noise_floor` every 60 seconds to adapt to changing background conditions.

---

## 1. Project 1252: Advanced Sound Sensor Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Decibel Heatmap Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to map analog sound intensity to an RGB LED gradient, creating a "Visual VU Meter" that shifts from cool green for silence to angry red for loud noises.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Logarithmic Scaling**: Understanding that sound is perceived logarithmically (Decibels), and mapping linear ADC values to a non-linear color scale.
*   **Persistence of Vision (Color Mixing)**: Rapidly cycling RGB intensities to create "Caution" Amber at specific sound levels.
*   **Visual Ergonomics**: Using color to provide immediate environmental awareness without a screen.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **RGB LED (Common Cathode)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Analog Data |
| **LED RED** | GP16 | PWM Alarm |
| **LED GREEN** | GP17 | PWM Safe |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_scale`** (Normalization)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_pwm_duty`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else_if`**

### 7. Variables
*   **vol_percent**: Normalized 0-100 amplitude.
*   **red_pwm**, **green_pwm**: Calculated duty cycles.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Foundation**
1.  **Read Mic**: Get the peak-to-peak amplitude from GP26.
2.  **Scale**: Convert the raw signal (e.g., 0-4000) to a percentage (0-100%).

**B. Color Gradient**
3.  **Green State**: If percentage < 30, set Green to 255 and Red to 0.
4.  **Caution State**: If percentage 30-70, mix Red (150) and Green (100) to create Amber.
5.  **Danger State**: If percentage > 70, set Red to 255 and Green to 0.

**C. Feedback Loop**
6.  **Refresh**: Run the loop every 20ms to allow for the LED to dance to the rhythm of the sound.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Ambience**: The sensor glows Green in a quiet room.
2.  **Conversation**: As people talk, the LED bounces between Green and Amber.
3.  **Shout**: A loud clap turns the LED instantly Red.
4.  **Result**: A functional room volume monitor useful for libraries or bedrooms.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
r = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); r.freq(1000)
g = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17)); g.freq(1000)

def get_amp():
    mx, mn = 0, 65535
    for _ in range(100):
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
        if v < mn: mn = v
    return mx - mn

while True:
    amp = get_amp()
    # Assuming 20000 is a "Loud" noise on this specific sensor
    percent = min(100, (amp / 20000) * 100)
    
    if percent < 30:
        r.duty_u16(0); g.duty_u16(65535)
    elif percent < 70:
        r.duty_u16(65535); g.duty_u16(20000) # Amber
    else:
        r.duty_u16(65535); g.duty_u16(0)
        
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Linear Perception**: Mapping 0-65535 directly to 0-100% Red/Green often feels "wrong" because double the voltage doesn't mean double the perceived loudness. You may need to "squash" the low-end values to make the meter feel more natural.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Slow Decay**: Make the Red LED stay on for 1 second after a loud noise, even if the sound stops, to act as a "Peak Indicator."

---

## 1. Project 1253: Advanced Sound Sensor Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Clap-Pattern Recognition". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement temporal filtering that distinguishes a "Double Clap" from a single random noise, using a state machine to track the timing between audio peaks.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time Windowing**: Requiring a second event to occur within 500ms of the first.
*   **State Machine Transitions**: Moving through states like `Wait_1`, `Heard_1`, and `Heard_2`.
*   **Timeout Logic**: Resetting the system if the second event doesn't arrive in time.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Trigger Edge |
| **LED** | GP16 | Status Target |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`** (Double Check)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_diff`** (Timer)
*   **from Loops, drag `repeat_while`** (Polling)

### 7. Variables
*   **last_pulse**: Timestamp of the first noise.
*   **clap_count**: Counter for sequential pulses.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Trigger Edge**
1.  **Monitor**: Watch for a sound peak > 30000.
2.  **Latch**: When heard, save `last_pulse = ticks_ms()`.

**B. Temporal Wait**
3.  **Cooldown**: Wait 100ms (preventing one clap being counted twice due to echo).
4.  **Window**: Wait up to 600ms for a second peak.

**C. Pattern Confirm**
5.  **Success**: If a second peak occurs in the window:
    *   **Action**: Toggle GP16 (LED).
    *   **Action**: Reset `clap_count` to 0.
6.  **Fail**: If time runs out, reset and start over.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Noise**: You drop a book. Pico ignores it after 600ms.
2.  **Pattern**: You clap-clap.
3.  **Response**: The LED toggles on the second clap.
4.  **Result**: An intelligent "The Clapper" style switch for home automation.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Smart Switch: Listening for Double-Clap...")

while True:
    # 1. Wait for first noise
    val = mic.read_u16()
    if val > 40000:
        print("Clap 1! Waiting...")
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        utime.sleep(0.15) # Anti-echo delay
        
        # 2. 600ms window for Clap 2
        success = False
        while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 600:
            if mic.read_u16() > 40000:
                success = True
                break
            utime.sleep(0.01)
            
        if success:
            print("DOUBLE CLAP CONFIRMED")
            led.toggle()
            utime.sleep(0.5) # Prevent re-trigger
            
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Echo Sensitivity**: A single clap in a tiled room will reflect off the walls and look like 3 or 4 claps to the Pico. The `utime.sleep(0.15)` is critical to block these "shadow" peaks.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Triple Clap**: Extend the logic to require three claps for a master "Power Off" command.

## 1. Project 1254: Advanced Sound Sensor Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"OLED Oscilloscope Waveform Rendering". At the end of this project, you will be able to visualize real-time audio waveforms on an OLED screen, plotting a scrolling graph of sound samples to see the "shape" of noises (e.g., sharp pulses vs. sustained tones).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Series Plotting**: Drawing a series of connected pixels where the X-axis is Time and the Y-axis is Amplitude.
*   **Circular Buffer**: managing a list where new data pushes the old data out, creating a "scrolling" effect.
*   **Pixel Drawing Optimization**: Understanding how to draw 128 lines on an OLED without causing significant lag.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **OLED Display (SSD1306)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Wave Source |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | Bus Link |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_pixel`** (Wave plot)
*   **from Lists, drag `shift_left`** (Scroll)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_raw_adc`**

### 7. Variables
*   **wave_buffer**: A list of 128 integers representing the Y-height of the wave.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Buffer Setup**
1.  **Initialize**: Create a list of 128 zeros.

**B. Data Ingest**
2.  **Sample**: Read GP26 ADC.
3.  **Scale for OLED**: Map the 0-65535 value to 0-40 pixels (centered vertically at Y=32).
4.  **Update Buffer**: Add the new value to the end and remove the first item.

**C. Render Logic**
5.  **Draw Loop**: Iterate from i = 0 to 127.
6.  **Line Plot**: Draw a pixel at (i, wave_buffer[i]).
7.  **Refresh**: Clear the screen and show the new frame.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Silence**: A flat line rolls across the center of the OLED.
2.  **Sound**: You whistle. The line turns into a smooth sine wave.
3.  **Snap**: You snap your fingers. A sharp vertical spike appears and travels across the screen.
4.  **Result**: A professional-looking diagnostic tool for audio analysis.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, ssd1306

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
mic = machine.ADC(26)

buffer = [32] * 128 # Start with middle line

while True:
    # 1. Read and Scale
    v = mic.read_u16()
    y = int(v / 65535 * 64) # Simple 0-64 map
    
    # 2. Shift Buffer
    buffer.append(y)
    buffer.pop(0)
    
    # 3. Draw Frame
    oled.fill(0)
    for x in range(127):
        oled.line(x, buffer[x], x+1, buffer[x+1], 1)
    
    oled.show()
    # No sleep for max refresh rate
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **I2C Bandwidth**: Plotting 128 lines every loop is heavy. If the wave looks "choppy," try drawing every 2nd or 3rd point, or increase the I2C frequency to 1MHz if your screen supports it.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hold Peak**: Draw a secondary line that stays at the maximum height seen in the last 2 seconds.

## 1. Project 1255: Automated Advanced Sound Sensor Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Voice-Activated Mood Lighting". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a voice-gated lighting system that uses "Soft Sounds" (low volume) to trigger dim blue light and "Sharp Sounds" (loud) to trigger bright white light, using PWM for intensity control.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multi-Threshold Gating**: Implementing different actions for different amplitude ranges.
*   **PWM Duty Interpolation**: Smoothly fading light intensity based on sound volume.
*   **Interlock Timers**: Keeping the lights on for a set duration (e.g., 5 seconds) after the last sound is heard to prevent flickering.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **5V Relay or High-Power LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED/Relay** | GP17 | PWM Load |
| **Mic AO** | GP26 | Control Hub |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else_if`** (Multi-gate)
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_pwm_fade`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`**

### 7. Variables
*   **light_timer**: Keeps track of when to turn the lights off.
*   **target_brightness**: The PWM level based on volume.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Poll Mic**: Read the GP26 peak-to-peak amplitude.

**B. Logic Branching**
2.  **Whisper**: If amplitude is 500-2000:
    *   **Action**: Set LED brightness to 20% (Night Light mode).
    *   **Action**: Set `light_timer` to now + 5000ms.
3.  **Shout**: If amplitude > 5000:
    *   **Action**: Set LED brightness to 100% (Safety mode).
    *   **Action**: Set `light_timer` to now + 10000ms.

**C. Timeout Phase**
4.  **Auto-Off**: If current time > `light_timer`, fade the LED to 0.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Wait**: Room is dark.
2.  **Voice**: You mutter "hello". A dim, cozy light appears for 5 seconds.
3.  **Sharp**: You clap. The room floods with bright light for 10 seconds.
4.  **Result**: An interactive, hands-free lighting controller that responds to the urgency of your voice.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17))
led.freq(1000)

timer = 0

while True:
    # Get Amplitude
    mx, mn = 0, 65535
    for _ in range(50):
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
        if v < mn: mn = v
    amp = mx - mn
    
    # Check Thresholds
    if amp > 10000: # SHOUT
        led.duty_u16(65535)
        timer = utime.ticks_ms() + 10000
    elif amp > 2000: # WHISPER
        led.duty_u16(10000)
        timer = utime.ticks_ms() + 5000
        
    # Timeout check
    if utime.ticks_ms() > timer:
        led.duty_u16(0)
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Rapid Re-triggering**: If you don't use a timer and instead just turn the LED on when `amp > X`, the light will blink like a strobe light to your voice. The "Linger Timer" is essential for a professional experience.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Soft Fade-Out**: Instead of turning the LED off instantly, use a loop to decrease the PWM duty from 100% to 0% over 2 seconds.
3.  **Calculate Counter-Movement**:
    *   `Servo_P = 90 - Pitch_Angle`.
    *   `Servo_R = 90 + Roll_Angle`.
4.  **Drive Actuators**:
    *   Update the PWM duty cycle for both servos.
5.  **Iteration Speed**:
    *   Run the loop every 20ms (50Hz), which is the standard signal frequency for servos.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Stability**: You rock the Pico base left and right.
2.  **Response**: The Roll servo twitches back and forth instantly.
3.  **Observe**: A pen taped to the camera tray remains pointing straight up, ignoring the base's motion.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Configure Servos (50Hz)
servo_p = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); servo_p.freq(50)
servo_r = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17)); servo_r.freq(50)

def set_angle(pwm, angle):
    # Standard MG90S mapping
    duty = int(1638 + (angle * (8192-1638)/180))
    pwm.duty_u16(duty)

print("Steady-Cam Gimbal: Initializing...")

while True:
    # 1. Capture orientation (Mock data)
    current_pitch = -12.0 # Nose down 12 deg
    current_roll = 5.0    # Banked right 5 deg
    
    # 2. Apply Compensation
    # If board is -12, servo must be +12 relative to 90 (102 deg)
    p_cmd = 90 - current_pitch
    r_cmd = 90 + current_roll
    
    # 3. Constrain to physical servo limits (0..180)
    p_cmd = max(0, min(180, p_cmd))
    r_cmd = max(0, min(180, r_cmd))
    
    set_angle(servo_p, p_cmd)
    set_angle(servo_r, r_cmd)
    
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Phase Shift**: If the servo responds too slowly, it can actually amplify the vibration (oscillation). Ensure your `utime.sleep` is as short as possible.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Sensors**: Use two Pico Ws—one on the base and one on the camera—to compare the delta between them for ultra-precise leveling.

## 1. Project 1256: Advanced Sound Sensor Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Glass Break / High-Impact Detection". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an acoustic safety trigger that identifies the specific sharp, high-frequency "spike" of breaking glass or an impact, instantly activating a lock-down relay for home security.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Acoustic Profiling**: Differentiating between a slow "thud" (door closing) and a sharp "snap" (glass breaking) using rise-time analysis.
*   **Hardware Interlocks**: Using a fast analog trigger to bypass main code delays during an emergency.
*   **Fail-Safe Latency**: Ensuring the relay triggers within 50ms of the sound event.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **5V Relay (Lock Simulation)**
*   **Red Warning LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay Signal** | GP15 | Security Lock |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Pulse Monitor |
| **LED** | GP16 | Alarm Status |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater`** (Peak detection)
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`** (Unlock relay)
*   **from Wait, drag `wait_until`** (Reset)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_locked**: Boolean state of the security perimeter.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Sense**: Read the microphone on GP26 using the high-speed sampling method.
2.  **Logic**: From **Math**, calculate the instantaneous amplitude.

**B. Emergency Trigger**
3.  **Detect**: If amplitude > 55000 (Extreme spike):
    *   **Action**: Set GP15 to HIGH (Activate Lock).
    *   **Action**: Set GP16 to ON (Red LED).
    *   **Status**: Set `is_locked` = True.

**C. Forensic Review**
4.  **Hold**: Maintain the lock-down state even if the sound stops.
5.  **Manual Reset**: From **Wait**, require the user to press a button to return to "Safe" mode.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Watch**: The Pico is in "Silent Watch" mode.
2.  **Impact**: A window nearby is struck.
3.  **Immediate**: The relay clicks instantly, locking the security door before a human could even react.
4.  **Result**: An audio-based alarm system that complements traditional motion sensors.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
relay = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Audio Security Guard: ON. Watching for impact spikes...")

while True:
    # High-speed spike check
    mx = 0
    for _ in range(200):
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
    
    if mx > 58000:
        print("!!! EMERGENCY: GLASS BREAK DETECTED !!!")
        relay.on()
        led.on()
        # Panic state - requires manual reset (reboot or button)
        while True:
            utime.sleep(1)
            
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **False Alarms**: A loud clap near the mic might trigger the alarm. Professional systems use a dual-microphone approach or specific frequency filters to ensure only "High-Pitch" glass sounds trigger the lock.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Pattern Matching**: Only trigger if two spikes occur within 100ms (representing the initial impact and the subsequent glass shattering).

## 1. Project 1257: Advanced Sound Sensor Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Ambient Sound Level Graphing". At the end of this project, you will be able to store a continuous log of room volume levels (e.g., average amplitude per minute) to create a historical profile of a location's acoustic environment.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Interval Aggregation**: Collecting thousands of samples and distilling them into a single representative "Volume Level" for a time block.
*   **Memory Paging**: Storing small data points (integers) instead of raw waveforms to save RAM.
*   **Statistical Outlier Removal**: Ignoring one-off door slams to get a true picture of the "Ambient Hum."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **OLED Display (Optional)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Log Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_mean`** (Average)
*   **from Lists, drag `append_to_list`**
*   **from Comm, drag `stream_log`**

### 7. Variables
*   **daily_log**: A list of data points representing hourly volume.
*   **running_sum**: Temporary total for the current minute.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Buffer**: Create an empty list called `vol_history`.

**B. Data Aggregation**
2.  **Sample Loop**: Every 1 second, take a 50ms peak-to-peak reading.
3.  **Minute Total**: Add this to a `running_sum`.
4.  **Save**: After 60 samples (1 minute), calculate the average and append it to `vol_history`.

**C. Visual Review**
5.  **Dump**: When a button is pressed, print the entire list to the terminal in CSV format: `Minute, Volume`.
6.  **Analyze**: Look for patterns (e.g., the office gets loud between 9 AM and 5 PM).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Pico starts silent background monitoring.
2.  **Long-Term**: It runs for 4 hours.
3.  **Retrieve**: You download the log.
4.  **Result**: A clear graph showing exactly when the construction work started outside or when the party next door reached its peak.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)
log = []

def get_amp():
    mx, mn = 0, 65535
    for _ in range(50):
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
        if v < mn: mn = v
    return mx - mn

print("Acoustic Logger: Active. Recording 1-minute averages...")

while True:
    minute_sum = 0
    for _ in range(60): # 60 seconds
        minute_sum += get_amp()
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    avg_vol = minute_sum / 60
    log.append(avg_vol)
    print(f"Logged Minute {len(log)}: Avg={avg_vol:.0f}")
    
    # Optional: Stop after 60 minutes
    if len(log) >= 60: break
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Overflow**: If you use a standard 16-bit integer for `minute_sum`, it will overflow very quickly when adding up large ADC readings. Use a broad float or Python's auto-expanding integers for the sum.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Threshold Triggered Log**: Instead of a time loop, only save a log entry when the volume exceeds a "Silence" threshold, saving memory.

## 1. Project 1258: Smart Advanced Sound Sensor System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Networked Audio Intrusion Alert". At the end of this project, you will be able to connect the Pico W to a local server (via UDP/TCP) and stream sound intensity data for remote monitoring, transforming your sensor into an IoT "Ear" that can be monitored from 100 meters away.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Wireless Data Buffering**: Storing audio peaks in a tiny "packet" before sending it over the air.
*   **UDP Protocol**: Using a fast, "best-effort" networking method for real-time telemetry where individual packet loss is acceptable.
*   **Remote Dashboarding**: Sending sensor strings to a computer running a simple Python listener.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **Wireless Router**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic OUT** | GP26 | Analog Signal |
| **Pico W WiFi**| Internal | Antenna |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Internet, drag `connect_wifi`**
*   **from Internet, drag `udp_send`**
*   **from Comm, drag `pico_print_labeled`**

### 7. Variables
*   **server_ip**: The address of your listening PC.
*   **packet_str**: The message "VOL: 450" being transmitted.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Network Link**
1.  **Login**: Connect the Pico W to your network.
2.  **Verify**: Log the assigned IP address to the terminal.

**B. Data Prep**
3.  **Calculate**: Read the amplitude from GP26.
4.  **Format**: Create a text string: `[DEVICE_ID]:[VOLUME]`.

**C. Broadcast**
5.  **Punch Out**: From **Internet**, use `udp_send` to blast the string to your PC on port 5005.
6.  **Loop**: Repeat every 500ms for a real-time remote "VU Meter."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Pico connects to the WiFi in the "Baby Room."
2.  **Monitor**: It sends silent packets every half-second.
3.  **Alarm**: The child wakes up and cries.
4.  **Result**: Your computer in the living room immediately prints "BABY_DEVICE: 8500 - LOUD" and sounds an alert.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network, socket, utime, machine

WIFI = "YourSSID"
PASS = "YourPass"
SERVER = "192.168.1.50" # Your PC IP
PORT = 5005

mic = machine.ADC(26)

def connect():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect(WIFI, PASS)
    while not wlan.isconnected(): utime.sleep(1)
    return socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)

sock = connect()

while True:
    # Get Peak-to-Peak
    mx, mn = 0, 65535
    for _ in range(100):
        v = mic.read_u16()
        if v > mx: mx = v
        if v < mn: mn = v
    amp = mx - mn
    
    # Send over network
    msg = f"NURSERY:{amp}"
    try:
        sock.sendto(msg.encode(), (SERVER, PORT))
    except:
        pass
        
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Firewall Blocking**: Your Windows/Mac computer might block incoming UDP packets on port 5005. You must explicitly allow the Pico's IP or the specific port in your firewall settings.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Node**: Deploy 3 Picos in different rooms. Your PC listener can now see the "Sound Map" of your entire house on one screen.

## 1. Project 1259: Advanced Sound Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Frequency Response Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a "System Sweep" to identify which frequencies (Low Bass vs. High Pitch) your specific sensor is most sensitive to, allowing for custom tuning of security or music-reactive projects.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Period vs. Frequency**: Understanding that a 1kHz sound-wave has a 1ms period, and configuring your code to capture samples at that resolution.
*   **Harmonic Distortion**: Identifying if the Pico's ADC is "clipping" the sound-wave top, leading to inaccurate frequency readings.
*   **Normalization of Response**: Adjusting the software gain to be equal across different sound pitches.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Sound Sensor**
*   **Smartphone with a "Tone Generator" app**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic AO** | GP26 | Spectral Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_sin_wave`** (Ref)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_high_speed_adc`**
*   **from Comm, drag `plot_serial`**

### 7. Variables
*   **sample_rate**: How many times per second the mic is read.
*   **freq_map**: A list tracking sensitivity from 100Hz to 10kHz.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Prep**
1.  **Set Rate**: Configure the Pico to read the ADC 20,000 times per second (20kHz).

**B. The Audit**
2.  **Tone Sweep**: Play a 440Hz (A4) tone on your phone near the mic.
3.  **Measure**: Capture 100 samples and find the `peak_to_peak`.
4.  **Repeat**: Move the phone app to 1000Hz, then 5000Hz.

**C. Tuning**
5.  **Observe**: Notice how the `peak_to_peak` value drops as the frequency increases.
6.  **Apply Boost**: In your code, multiply high-frequency signals by a "Correction Factor" (e.g., 1.5) to make the sensor response feel flat.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Audit tool begins.
2.  **Sweep**: You play a rising whistle sound.
3.  **Terminal**: The Pico shows "Sensitivity: [Low: 90% | Mid: 65% | High: 20%]".
4.  **Result**: You now know how to calibrate your code to listen for high-pitched whistles versus low-pitched thunder.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic = machine.ADC(26)

def audit_frequency(tag):
    print(f"--- Freq Audit: {tag} ---")
    vals = []
    # Rapid burst capture (10ms)
    for _ in range(500):
        vals.append(mic.read_u16())
    
    amp = max(vals) - min(vals)
    print(f"Response Strength: {amp}")
    return amp

print("Play tone now...")
utime.sleep(3)
strength_440 = audit_frequency("440Hz")

print("Play higher tone now...")
utime.sleep(3)
strength_2k = audit_frequency("2000Hz")

print(f"Drop-off factor: {strength_2k / strength_440:.2f}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Aliasing**: If you sample at 1kHz, you cannot accurately measure a 2kHz sound wave (it will look like a much slower wave). This is the "Nyquist Theorem"—always sample at least twice as fast as the sound you want to hear.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Audio Peak-Latcher**: Use an external Capacitor/Diode circuit (Envelope Follower) to turn the AC sound wave into a steady DC voltage for much easier ADC reading.
*   `offsets`: A list of 6 values [X_pos, X_neg, Y_pos, Y_neg, Z_pos, Z_neg].
*   `bias_x`, `bias_y`, `bias_z`: The calculated center-points.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Wizard Step 1**:
    *   Print "Lay Pico Flat (Z-UP). Press Button." Wait for 100 samples and average them.
2.  **Wizard Step 2-6**:
    *   Repeat the process for: Upside Down, Front, Back, Left Side, Right Side.
3.  **Math calculation**:
    *   `BIAS_X = (X_pos + X_neg) / 2`. The result should be 0.
4.  **Scaling**:
    *   Calculated `SCALE_X = 1.0 / ( (X_pos - X_neg) / 2 )`.
5.  **Output**:
    *   Print the final `bias` and `scale` values. Users should copy these into their main code.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Wizard says "FLIP OVER".
2.  **Done**: Screen reads: "X_BIAS: 124, Y_BIAS: -50, Z_BIAS: 670".
3.  **Accuracy**: All future spirit level projects now show "0.0" exactly on your table.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

def perform_6_step_cal():
    print("--- 6-POINT CALIBRATION WIZARD ---")
    faces = ["Z-UP", "Z-DOWN", "X-FORWARD", "X-BACK", "Y-LEFT", "Y-RIGHT"]
    results = []
    
    for face in faces:
        print(f"ACTION: Place Pico {face}. Press Key...")
        input() # Wait for user
        
        # Take 50 samples and average
        sum_accel = 0
        for _ in range(50):
            sum_accel += 16384 # mock_read()
            utime.sleep(0.01)
        
        results.append(sum_accel / 50)
        print(f"Captured: {results[-1]}")
        
    # Calculate X Bias as example
    # bias_x = (results[2] + results[3]) / 2
    print("Calculations complete. Check Serial Console for values.")

# perform_6_step_cal()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sloppy Alignment**: If you hold the Pico in your hand instead of placing it flat on a table, the calibration will be worse than the factory default.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **JSON Storage**: Have the Pico save these values to a hidden `cal.json` file so all your other projects can auto-load them.

## 1. Project 1260: Advanced Advanced Sound Sensor Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Mic Beamforming Simulation". At the end of this project, you will be able to simulate a "Directional Array" that uses phase timing (Delays between two microphones) to identify the angle of a sound source, allowing for robotic head-tracking toward a speaker.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time Difference of Arrival (TDOA)**: Calculating why one ear hears a sound slightly before the other.
*   **Phase Shift Analysis**: Using the delay (in microseconds) to determine if a sound came from the Left or Right.
*   **Acoustic Triangulation**: The math of using 3 mics to pinpoint a sound's exact XY coordinate in a room.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2 Sound Sensors** (Mounted 10cm apart)
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Mic L** | GP26 | Left Ear |
| **Mic R** | GP27 | Right Ear |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_less_than_multi`** (Phase sync)
*   **from Math, drag `calculate_tdoa`**
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_arrow`**

### 7. Variables
*   **time_delta**: Microseconds between L and R detections.
*   **source_angle**: Degrees relative to the center of the array.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Trigger Sync**
1.  **Pulse Watch**: Wait for either GP26 or GP27 to exceed a threshold of 40000.
2.  **Clock Start**: Record `t1 = ticks_us()`.

**B. Detection Link**
3.  **Second Pulse**: Wait for the *other* pin to hit the threshold.
4.  **Clock End**: Record `t2 = ticks_us()`.
5.  **Delta**: Let `time_delta = t2 - t1`.

**C. Orientation Render**
6.  **Calculate**: From **Math**, use the formula `angle = arcsin( (speed_of_sound * time_delta) / distance_between_mics )`.
7.  **Draw**: On the OLED, draw an arrow pointing toward the calculated angle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Ready**: Array is "Listening."
2.  **Sound**: You snap your fingers at a 45-degree angle to the right.
3.  **Compute**: Pico notes that Mic R heard it 120 microseconds before Mic L.
4.  **Result**: The OLED arrow snaps to point Right.
5.  **Security**: This is the core logic used in sniper-detection systems and high-end video conferencing cameras.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

mic_l = machine.ADC(26)
mic_r = machine.ADC(27)

print("Acoustic Beamformer: Active.")

while True:
    # 1. Busy-wait for first pulse
    # In real world, use IRQ on Digital Out pins for microsecond precision
    l_val = mic_l.read_u16()
    r_val = mic_r.read_u16()
    
    if l_val > 50000:
        print("Sound Source: LEFT FOCUS")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
    elif r_val > 50000:
        print("Sound Source: RIGHT FOCUS")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        
    utime.sleep_ms(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Speed of Software**: MicroPython's `read_u16()` is not fast enough to catch 1-microsecond differences (sound travels 0.3mm per microsecond). For a real project, use external "Comparators" into Digital pins to trigger Hardware Interrupts for true TDOA.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Servo Tracker**: Connect a servo to the array. Every time a sound is heard, rotate the servo to point at the source, creating a "Robotic Eye" that follows noise.

## Batch 127: Advanced Touch Sensor 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1261: Advanced Touch Sensor Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Capacitive Auto-Zero Calibration". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an intelligent power-on routine that records the "resting" state of a touch sensor, allowing the code to automatically ignore small electrical variations or proximity effects caused by the environment.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Capacitive Sensing Theory**: Understanding how human body capacitance changes the electrical charge on a sensor pad.
*   **Baseline Subtraction**: Recording a "Silent" value and using it as a zero-reference.
*   **Active Power Gating**: Using a Pico pin to reset the sensor hardware if a latching error occurs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor (TTP223 or metal pad)**
*   **Relay (indicator)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch OUT** | GP16 | Digital/Analog In |
| **VCC Rail** | GP15 | Switched Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_touch_value`**
*   **from Math, drag `pico_mean`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   **touch_baseline**: The average value when no one is near.
*   **trigger_threshold**: The delta required to confirm a touch.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Hardware Wake**
1.  **Power Up**: Set GP15 to HIGH to provide VCC to the sensor.
2.  **Stabilize**: Wait 500ms for the internal chip to settle.

**B. Calibration Wizard**
3.  **Sample**: From **Loops**, repeat 50 times: read GP16 and add to `noise_sum`.
4.  **Math**: Divide `noise_sum` by 50 to get `touch_baseline`.

**C. Active Watch**
5.  **Compare**: In the main loop, if (Current_Read - `touch_baseline`) > 500:
    *   **Action**: Turn on the Relay.
    *   **Action**: Print "TOUCH DETECTED: Hardware Synced".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico boots. "Calibrating touch pad... do not touch."
2.  **Ready**: Calibration logs 2450 as the baseline.
3.  **Interaction**: You hover your finger. The value hits 3500.
4.  **Result**: The relay clicks. The system is immune to the static electricity on your carpet because it "Zeroed" itself at start.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

pwr = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
touch = machine.ADC(26) # Using analog for precise thresholding

def calibrate():
    pwr.on()
    print("Zeroing Sensor...")
    utime.sleep(1)
    sm = 0
    for _ in range(100):
        sm += touch.read_u16()
        utime.sleep(0.01)
    return sm / 100

base = calibrate()
print(f"Base: {base:.0f}")

while True:
    val = touch.read_u16()
    if val > (base + 5000):
        print("TOUCH DETECTED")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Touching during Boot**: If you hold the sensor while the Pico is calibrating, your finger becomes part of the "Zero" baseline. The sensor will then only trigger if you touch it *even harder*, or it might never trigger at all.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Watchdog Reset**: If the sensor stays "High" for more than 30 seconds, use GP15 to cut power and "re-zero" the chip automatically.

## 1. Project 1262: Advanced Touch Sensor Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Long-Press vs. Short-Press Recognition". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement temporal logic that differentiates between a quick "Tap" (to toggle a light) and a "Long-Hold" (to dim/brighten the light), using high-resolution millisecond timers.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Held-State Detection**: Using boolean flags to track a persistent contact.
*   **Temporal Branching**: Executing different code paths based on the duration of a single input event.
*   **UI Response**: Providing immediate visual feedback as soon as a "Hold" is acknowledged.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor**
*   **PWM LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch OUT** | GP14 | Event Source |
| **LED** | GP16 | Variable Intensity |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater`**
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_pwm_duty`**

### 7. Variables
*   **touch_start**: Timestamp of the moment contact was made.
*   **hold_duration**: Calculated time in ms.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Trigger Edge**
1.  **Detect**: Wait for GP14 to go HIGH.
2.  **Timestamp**: Record `touch_start = ticks_ms()`.

**B. Verification**
3.  **Wait for Release**: Stay in a loop until GP14 goes LOW.
4.  **Math**: Calculate `hold_duration = ticks_ms() - touch_start`.

**C. Decision Tree**
5.  **Case: Tap**: If `hold_duration` < 500ms, toggle LED (On/Off).
6.  **Case: Hold**: If `hold_duration` > 1000ms:
    *   **Action**: Print "ENTER SETUP MODE."
    *   **Action**: Flash LED 3 times.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Tap**: You tap the sensor. The light turns on.
2.  **Hold**: You press and hold for 2 seconds.
3.  **Result**: The light flashes to confirm you've entered the "Dimming Menu," effectively doubling the functionality of a single sensor pad.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        # Wait for release
        while touch.value() == 1:
            utime.sleep(0.01)
        
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start)
        
        if duration > 1000:
            print(f"LONG PRESS: {duration}ms (Enter Menu)")
            for _ in range(5):
                led.toggle(); utime.sleep(0.1)
        else:
            print(f"SHORT TAP: {duration}ms (Toggle Power)")
            led.toggle()
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking while holding**: If you use a simple `sleep` inside the hold loop, you won't be able to run other background tasks (like updating a clock or checking a second sensor). Use the `ticks_diff` method for "non-blocking" timing.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Incremental Dimmer**: While holding, slowly increase the PWM duty cycle of the LED (0% -> 100%) so the user can release at the exact brightness they want.

## 1. Project 1263: Advanced Touch Sensor Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Double-Tap Interaction Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an advanced state machine that only triggers an action if two distinct touch events occur within a narrow 500ms window, similar to a computer mouse double-click.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Machines**: Transitioning through `IDLE` -> `SINGLE_TAP` -> `DOUBLE_TAP`.
*   **Inter-Arrival Time**: Measuring the gap between two pulses.
*   **UX Confirmation**: Using a buzzer to acknowledge the first tap to "guide" the user to the second.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch IN** | GP14 | Pulse Source |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Click Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_and`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_diff`**
*   **from Loops, drag `repeat_while`**

### 7. Variables
*   **tap_count**: How many hits in the current window.
*   **window_timer**: The deadline for the second tap.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. First Contact**
1.  **Watch**: Wait for GP14 to go HIGH.
2.  **Sound**: From **Smart IO**, play a 10ms click on the buzzer.
3.  **Start Window**: Set `window_timer = ticks_ms()`.

**B. The Window**
4.  **Poll**: For the next 500ms, watch for GP14 to go HIGH *again*.
5.  **Anti-Hold**: Ensure the finger left the sensor before counting the second tap.

**C. Success / Fail**
6.  **Success**: If second tap arrives < 500ms:
    *   **Action**: Print "ACCESS GRANTED."
    *   **Action**: Play a "Victory" tone.
7.  **Fail**: If timer expires, reset `tap_count` to 0.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Slow Tap**: You tap... wait... tap. Pico resets. Nothing happens.
2.  **Rapid Tap**: You tap-tap.
3.  **Result**: The buzzer plays a high-pitched "Double-Click" jingle, confirming the command was understood.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
buzz = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("System Locked: Double-Tap to Unlock.")

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        # First Tap
        buzz.on(); utime.sleep(0.05); buzz.off()
        while touch.value() == 1: utime.sleep(0.01) # Wait for release
        
        start = utime.ticks_ms()
        success = False
        
        # 500ms window for Second Tap
        while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 500:
            if touch.value() == 1:
                success = True
                break
            utime.sleep(0.01)
            
        if success:
            print("--- UNLOCKED ---")
            buzz.on(); utime.sleep(0.2); buzz.off()
            utime.sleep(2) # Stay unlocked
        else:
            print("Resetting...")
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Single Pulse Blurring**: If your code is too fast, one long touch might look like two rapid taps. You must wait for the digital signal to return to LOW (finger removed) before starting the window for the second tap.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hidden Combo**: Require "Tap - Hold - Tap" to trigger a secret administrator mode.

"OLED Touch-Point Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a real-time visual interface that plots coordinates or status icons on an OLED screen based on touch input, transforming a simple sensor into a graphical "Soft-Button" display.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Icon Rendering**: Drawing bitmap shapes or complex icons (like a lightbulb or a lock) on an OLED.
*   **Toggle Graphics**: Swapping between two different images based on sensor state.
*   **UI Refresh Optimization**: Only updating the part of the screen that changed to prevent flickering.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor**
*   **OLED Display (128x64)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **OLED I2C** | GP4/GP5 | Graphic Bus |
| **Touch SIG** | GP14 | UI Trigger |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Display, drag `draw_icon`**
*   **from Smart Display, drag `clear_area`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else`**

### 7. Variables
*   **is_active**: Boolean tracking the virtual button state.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization**
1.  **Draw Menu**: On boot, draw a large circle on the left side of the OLED with the label "STANDBY."

**B. Interaction**
2.  **Monitor**: If GP14 is HIGH, set `is_active` = True.
3.  **Update UI**:
    *   **Active**: Draw a "Solid Circle" and change text to "ACTIVE."
    *   **Standby**: Draw a "Hollow Circle" and change text to "STANDBY."

**C. Animation**
4.  **Feedback**: When touched, flash the entire screen border for 100ms to provide "Visual Haptics."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Wait**: The OLED shows a greyed-out bulb icon.
2.  **Touch**: You place your finger on the sensor.
3.  **Visual**: The bulb "lights up" on screen instantly.
4.  **Result**: A premium-feeling user interface that provides clear confirmation of user intent without mechanical buttons.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, ssd1306

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = ssd1306.SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)

def draw_ui(pressed):
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.rect(10, 10, 108, 44, 1) # Border
    if pressed:
        oled.fill_rect(20, 20, 88, 24, 1) # Fill when touched
        oled.text("PRESSED", 40, 28, 0)
    else:
        oled.text("IDLE", 50, 28, 1)
    oled.show()

while True:
    draw_ui(touch.value() == 1)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Laggy Response**: If your `draw_ui` function takes 200ms to run, the touch will feel "squishy" and unresponsive. Keep your drawings simple or use partial screen updates.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1265: Automated Advanced Touch Sensor Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Capacitive Fluid-Level Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to use a touch sensor as a "Non-Contact Level Switch." You will mount the sensor on the *outside* of a plastic water tank and write logic to trigger a pump (relay) when the water level rises to meet the sensor's position.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dielectric Sensing**: Understanding how water (high dielectric) triggers capacitive sensors through plastic/glass walls.
*   **Hysteresis in Fluid Control**: preventing a pump from rapidly turning on/off due to water ripples/splashing.
*   **Industrial safety**: Keeping electronics completely isolated from liquid for maximum reliability.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor (TTP223 - Adjusted to high sensitivity)**
*   **5V Relay (Water Pump simulation)**
*   **Plastic cup or tank**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch SIG** | GP14 | Tank Monitor |
| **Relay** | GP15 | Safety Exhaust |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_less`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`**
*   **from Wait, drag `wait_seconds`**

### 7. Variables
*   **is_full**: Boolean state of the reservoir.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Mounting**
1.  **Position**: tape the touch sensor to the outside of a dry plastic bottle at the 75% mark.

**B. The Watchdog**
2.  **Sample**: Read GP14. If HIGH, it means water is at or above the sensor level.
3.  **Anti-Splash**: Only trigger if the signal is stable for 3 seconds (ignoring ripples).

**C. Automated Action**
4.  **Drain**: If `is_full` is True:
    *   **Action**: Turn on GP15 (Relay) to "Drain" the tank.
    *   **Action**: Wait 10 seconds to ensure the level drops significantly below the sensor.
5.  **Shutdown**: Stop the relay and return to monitoring.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Fill**: You slowly pour water into the bottle.
2.  **Trigger**: Water level hits the sensor. The Pico waits 3 seconds to confirm it's not a splash.
3.  **Result**: The relay clicks, "Emptying" the tank before it can overflow.
4.  **Safety**: No wires ever touch the water, making it safe for drinking water or corrosive chemicals.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
pump = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Tank Watchdog Active. Monitoring thru vessel wall...")

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        # Debounce the water surface (3s check)
        print("Potential Overflow... Verifying...")
        stable = True
        for _ in range(30):
            if touch.value() == 0:
                stable = False
                break
            utime.sleep(0.1)
            
        if stable:
            print("!!! TANK FULL - DRAINING !!!")
            pump.on()
            utime.sleep(10) # Run pump for safety margin
            pump.off()
            print("Drain Complete.")
            
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Wall Thickness**: If the plastic is too thick (>3mm), the sensor might not "see" the water. You may need to solder a larger copper foil pad to the sensor plate to increase its range.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Differential Sensing**: Add a second sensor at the 10% mark. Only turn the pump *off* when both sensors show "Empty" to ensure a full drain cycle.

---

## 1. Project 1266: Advanced Touch Sensor Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Vandal Security Latch". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Abuse Detection" algorithm that identifies if a sensor is being touched too aggressively or frequently, automatically locking the system and sounding an alarm to prevent vandalism.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Rate-of-Change Auditing**: Measuring how many events occur per minute.
*   **Temporary lockout**: Disabling inputs for a cool-down period.
*   **Security Latency**: Ensuring the alarm triggers *before* the attacker can perform a second attempt.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor**
*   **Piezo Buzzer**
*   **Lock Relay**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Buzzer** | GP16 | Alarm Status |
| **Sensor Digital** | GP14 | Event Feed |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_increment`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater`**
*   **from Wait, drag `wait_seconds`**

### 7. Variables
*   **hit_counter**: Number of touches in the last 10 seconds.
*   **is_blacklisted**: State of the security lockout.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Event Hit**: Every time the sensor is touched, add 1 to `hit_counter`.

**B. Abuse Analysis**
2.  **Audit**: If `hit_counter` > 5 (Five touches in rapid succession):
    *   **Action**: Set `is_blacklisted` = True.
    *   **Action**: Play a 5-second Continuous Siren.

**C. Recovery**
3.  **Cool Down**: Wait 30 seconds before resetting `hit_counter` and clearing the blacklist.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Normal**: You touch the sensor once. System acknowledges.
2.  **Attack**: Someone taps the sensor 10 times in 2 seconds.
3.  **Result**: The buzzer screams. The main system disables all relay outputs. The "Attacker" is locked out for 30 seconds.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
alarm = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

hits = 0
last_hit = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        hits += 1
        print(f"Touch Count: {hits}/5")
        while touch.value() == 1: utime.sleep(0.01) # Wait release
        
        if hits >= 5:
            print("!!! SECURITY LOCKOUT: ABUSE DETECTED !!!")
            alarm.on()
            utime.sleep(5)
            alarm.off()
            print("System cooling down (10s)...")
            utime.sleep(10)
            hits = 0 # Reset after cooldown
            
    # Decay hits over time (Auto-reset every 5 seconds of silence)
    if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_hit) > 5000:
        hits = 0
        last_hit = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sticky Counter**: If you forget to reset `hits` to zero during silence, the system will eventually lock itself even if the user is legitimate (5 touches over 5 hours shouldn't trigger an alarm!).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Steal-Proofing**: If the sensor signal disappears entirely (wires cut), trigger a fail-safe alarm.

---

## 1. Project 1267: Advanced Touch Sensor Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Social Distancing Counter / Traffic Auditor". At the end of this project, you will be able to log the frequency of "Human Interaction Events" (touches) throughout the day, storing the timestamp of every interaction to determine when a public kiosk is most frequently used.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dwell Time Analysis**: Measuring how long a person remains in contact with the sensor.
*   **Timestamp Stamping**: Using an RTC (Real Time Clock) to mark data points.
*   **Memory Efficiency**: Logging only the "delta" (time since last touch) to save space.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor**
*   **RTC Module (DS3231 or internal RTC)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **RTC SDA/SCL** | GP4/5 | Time Sync |
| **Touch OUT** | GP14 | Audit Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `append_to_list`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `read_rtc_time`**
*   **from Comm, drag `dump_list_to_serial`**

### 7. Variables
*   **audit_log**: A list of strings: ["12:01", "12:05", "12:15"].

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Clock Sync**
1.  **Init**: Initialize the RTC and print current time to verify.

**B. Logging Phase**
2.  **Detect Contact**: When GP14 is HIGH, fetch current time `H:M:S`.
3.  **Record**: Append the time string to `audit_log`.
4.  **Wait**: Ensure you don't log the same person twice (delay 5 seconds).

**C. Retrieval**
5.  **Data Dump**: When a hidden button is pressed, print the entire list to the terminal in CSV format.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Day Start**: Kiosk is clean. No logs.
2.  **Usage**: 10 people touch the interactive map during the lunch hour.
3.  **Result**: You download the log and see a clear spike in usage between 12:00 and 1:00 PM.
4.  **Optimization**: This data allows you to schedule cleaning or restocking precisely when needed.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
log = []

def get_time():
    t = utime.localtime()
    return f"{t[3]}:{t[4]}:{t[5]}"

print("Auditor Active. Press sensor to log visits.")

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        ts = get_time()
        log.append(ts)
        print(f"LOGGED VISIT AT {ts}")
        
        # Cooldown prevents multi-counting one person
        utime.sleep(5)
        
    # Optional Data Dump on GP13
    if machine.Pin(13, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP).value() == 0:
        print("--- DAILY AUDIT REPORT ---")
        for entry in log: print(entry)
        utime.sleep(1)
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **No Timezone**: If your Pico isn't connected to the web or an RTC, `utime.localtime()` will reset to Jan 1st, 2021 on every reboot. Always use an external battery-backed RTC for real logging.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Heatmap Export**: Use Python on your PC to read the log and create a bar chart of usage frequency.

---

## 1. Project 1268: Smart Advanced Touch Sensor System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Wireless Touch-to-Cloud Dashboard". At the end of this project, you will be able to connect a Pico W to a local WiFi network and send a "PING" message to a remote dashboard (Ubidots, Adafruit IO, or simple Webhook) every time the touch sensor is activated.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HTTP Request Methods**: Using `GET` or `POST` to transmit data to a web server.
*   **JSON Formatting**: Wrapping your sensor data into a structured format that websites understand `{"touch": 1}`.
*   **Wireless Latency**: Understanding the delay between a physical touch and the cloud notification (usually 200ms-1s).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W**

*   **Internet Access**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch SIG** | GP14 | Event Feed |
| **Pico W** | Internal | WiFi Antenna |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Internet, drag `connect_wifi`**
*   **from Internet, drag `http_post_request`**
*   **from Text, drag `create_json`**

### 7. Variables
*   **api_url**: The destination for your touch data.
*   **wifi_status**: Boolean showing if link is active.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Wireless Handshake**
1.  **Connect**: Connect the Pico W to your router.
2.  **Display Status**: Flash an LED slowly while connecting, and hold it steady once online.

**B. The Webhook**
3.  **Detect**: When GP14 is HIGH, create a JSON payload: `{"id": "DOOR_SENS", "val": 1}`.
4.  **Send**: Use `urequests` to POST the payload to your API endpoint.

**C. Confirmation**
5.  **Success check**: Print the HTTP response code (e.g., 200 OK) to the terminal.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Placement**: You hide the sensor under the mousepad of your home computer.
2.  **Trigger**: Someone touches your computer while you are at school.
3.  **Notification**: Your phone buzzes instantly with a notification: "Alert: Desk Sensor Activated!"
4.  **Result**: An IoT-connected security layer for your personal space.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network, urequests, utime, machine

WIFI_SSID = "HomeWiFi"
WIFI_PASS = "12345678"
WEBHOOK_URL = "http://yourserver.com/api/touch"

touch = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)

def connect():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect(WIFI_SSID, WIFI_PASS)
    while not wlan.isconnected(): utime.sleep(1)
    print("WiFi Online!")

connect()

while True:
    if touch.value() == 1:
        print("Sending Alert...")
        try:
            r = urequests.post(WEBHOOK_URL, json={"touch": 1})
            print(f"Reply: {r.status_code}")
            r.close()
        except:
            print("Web Fail")
        
        utime.sleep(2) # Prevent spamming
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Requests**: `urequests` will wait for the server to reply. If the internet is slow, the Pico will "freeze" for a second. If you need low-latency buzzer feedback, play the buzzer *before* sending the web request.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Daily Log Cloud**: Send a total count of daily touches once every midnight instead of every single event.

## 1. Project 1269: Advanced Touch Sensor Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Environmental Noise Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Diagnostic Wizard" that measures the raw electrical noise (interference) in your environment, helping you choose the perfect "Digital Sensitivity" for your touch sensor projects.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)**: Understanding the gap between "Fake" noise and a "Real" touch.
*   **ADC Jitter Plotting**: Viewing the small variations in voltage when the sensor is idle.
*   **Dynamic Threshold Selection**: Automatically calculating `Threshold = Noise_Peak + 20%` for reliable operation.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Touch Sensor (Analog mode)**
*   **Pico Serial Monitor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Touch OUT** | GP26 | Analog ADC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `max`**
*   **from Comm, drag `plot_serial`**

### 7. Variables
*   **peak_noise**: The singular highest "ghost" reading seen in 5 seconds.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Audit Mode**
1.  **Start Logic**: Run a loop for 5000ms.
2.  **Record**: Keep track of the highest value seen on GP26. DO NOT TOUCH the sensor.

**B. Calculation**
3.  **Find Peak**: Print "Max Background Interference: [Value]".
4.  **Buffer**: Add a 10% safety margin to this peak.

**C. Testing**
5.  **Validation**: Now touch the sensor. Compare your touch value to the `Noise_Peak`. If the touch is only twice as high as the noise, your environment is "Dirty" (Electro-magnetic interference).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Tool starts. "Scanning room... hands off."
2.  **Analysis**: Peak noise is 500 units.
3.  **Diagnosis**: Recommendation: "Set Threshold to 650 for best results."
4.  **Result**: Rock-solid touch detection that never triggers when a microwave or vacuum turns on nearby.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

adc = machine.ADC(26)

print("--- Touch Sensitivity Audit ---")
print("STEP 1: Hands off. Scanning noise for 5 seconds...")

max_noise = 0
start = utime.ticks_ms()
while utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start) < 5000:
    v = adc.read_u16()
    if v > max_noise: max_noise = v
    utime.sleep_ms(1)

print(f"PEAK NOISE: {max_noise}")
safe_threshold = int(max_noise * 1.2)
print(f"RECOMMENDED THRESHOLD: {safe_threshold}")

print("\nSTEP 2: Touch sensor now to verify signal strength...")
while True:
    v = adc.read_u16()
    if v > safe_threshold:
        print(f"VERIFIED TOUCH: {v} (SNR: {v/max_noise:.1f}x)")
        utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **USB Noise**: Some cheap USB chargers produce massive electrical noise. If your "Peak Noise" is very high, try switching to a battery pack or a better computer port to see if the audit improves.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Gain Control**: Have your main project run this audit on every boot-up to adjust sensitivity to the specific place it's plugged in.

---

## 1. Project 1270: Advanced Advanced Touch Sensor Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Virtual Gesture Simulation (Haptic-Free Slider)". At the end of this project, you will be able to simulate a "Touch-Free Gesture Control" system that uses an analog touch sensor's proximity field to fade an LED or move a servo based on how *close* your hand is, even if you aren't actually touching the pad.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fringe Field Sensing**: Utilizing the very small changes in capacitance that occur as a hand approaches within 1-2cm of a wire.
*   **Proportional Fade (Linear)**: Mapping the inverse-square proximity signal to a linear PWM duty cycle.
*   **Signal Damping**: Using a "Rolling Average" to prevent the LED from flickering due to hand jitters.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Copper Tape or Metal Wire (Naked Sensor)**
*   **High-Brightness LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Naked Pad** | GP26 | Proximity Antenna |
| **LED** | GP16 | Visual Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_rolling_avg`**
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_pwm_duty`**
*   **from Logic, drag `map_to_inverted`**

### 7. Variables
*   **prox_val**: The immediate analog reading.
*   **smooth_prox**: The filtered value used for lighting.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Antenna Setup**
1.  **DIY**: tape a 3-inch piece of copper foil to the table. Connect it to GP26.

**B. Prox Logic**
2.  **Ingest**: Read GP26 ADC.
3.  **Invert**: Since proximity often *reduces* resistance/voltage in certain configurations, use the **Math** block to map the reading so "Close = High Value."

**C. Render**
4.  **Fade**: Pipe the `smooth_prox` directly to the PWM duty cycle of GP16.
5.  **Watch**: as your hand moves closer, the LED gets brighter. As you pull away, it dims into nothingness.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: LED is off.
2.  **Approach**: You move your hand within 1 inch of the copper tape. The LED starts to glow faintly.
3.  **Touch**: You touch the tape. The LED hits 100% brightness.
4.  **Result**: A futuristic, "Theremin-inspired" light controller that feels magical to the user.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

antenna = machine.ADC(26)
led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
led.freq(1000)

avg_noise = 2000 # Typical baseline
print("Gesture System: Online. Hover hand over pad.")

while True:
    raw = antenna.read_u16()
    # Find delta from baseline
    delta = max(0, raw - avg_noise)
    
    # Map delta to PWM (Scaling 0-15000 delta to 0-65535 duty)
    # This requires tuning based on your specific pad size
    duty = min(65535, delta * 4) 
    
    led.duty_u16(int(duty))
    
    if duty > 1000:
        print(f"Strength: {duty/655:.0f}%")
        
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Unshielded Wires**: The long wire connecting the Pico to the copper tape will act as an antenna too! Keep your detection wires as short as possible (~2 inches) to ensure only the pad is sensitive.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Gesture Swipe**: Use two pads. If signal peaks on Pad A then Pad B, it's a "Swipe Right" gesture—use this to change a track or flip a page on an OLED.
