## 1. Project 1271: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Sensor Horizontal FOV Mapping". At the end of this project, you will be able to initialize a dual-sensor array (Ultrasonic + IR) and perform a geometric scan to define the robot's "Safe Corridor," ensuring that obstacles are detected across a full 120-degree field of view.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Redundancy**: Using both light-based (IR) and sound-based (Ultrasonic) sensors to detect different types of materials.
*   **Field of View (FOV)**: Understanding the cone of detection for different sensors.
*   **Digital Data Cleaning**: Synchronizing two different sensor trigger rates to prevent interference.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **IR Obstacle Sensor (Digital)**
*   **Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Ultrasonic Trig/Echo** | GP16/17 | Long Range (Center) |
| **IR Sensor OUT** | GP14 | Short Range (Side) |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Warning Port |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_ultrasonic`**
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `read_ir_obstacle`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_or`**

### 7. Variables
*   **center_dist**: Distance in cm from the ultrasonic.
*   **side_blocked**: Boolean state from the IR sensor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Array Sync**
1.  **Poll Ultrasonic**: Fire the center sensor and store the distance.
2.  **Poll IR**: Check the digital status of the side sensor.

**B. Logic Fusion**
3.  **Threat Check**:
    *   **Case 1**: If `center_dist` < 20cm OR `side_blocked` == True:
        *   **Action**: Turn on the Buzzer.
        *   **Action**: Print "CORRIDOR BLOCKED."

**C. Feedback Loop**
4.  **Refresh Rate**: Run the loop every 50ms for high-speed navigation readiness.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Clear**: Robot sees a clear path. Buzzer is silent.
2.  **Object**: You place a transparent glass bottle in front. The Ultrasonic misses it, but the IR sensor (detecting proximity) catches it.
3.  **Result**: The buzzer sounds immediately. This dual-sensing approach prevents the robot from crashing into daily household objects.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
ir_side = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
buzz = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    duration = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return (duration * 0.0343) / 2

while True:
    d = get_dist()
    side = ir_side.value() == 0 # IR pulled low on detect
    
    if d < 25 or side:
        print(f"!!! OBSTACLE !!! Center: {d:.1f}cm, Side: {side}")
        buzz.on()
    else:
        buzz.off()
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Cross-Talk**: If you have two ultrasonic sensors too close together, one's "ping" might be heard by the other's "ear." Always fire them sequentially with at least 20ms of silence between pulses.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sector Labeling**: Print which side the obstacle is on (Left, Center, or Right) to help the steering logic.

---

## 1. Project 1272: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Polar Coordinate Sweep Visualization". At the end of this project, you will be able to mount a distance sensor on a Servo and create a "Radar Sweep" that maps the distance at every 10 degrees, storing the results in an array for spatial analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polar to Cartesian Mapping**: Understanding how a (Degree, Distance) pair represents a point in space.
*   **Servo-Sensor Synchronization**: Moving the servo to a specific angle and waiting for the "Echo" before moving to the next.
*   **Array Management**: Filling a 18-element list with real-time environment data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Servo Motor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Sweep Mechanism |
| **Ultrasonic** | GP17/18 | Data Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_servo_angle`**
*   **from Lists, drag `set_item_at_index`**
*   **from Loops, drag `count_with`**

### 7. Variables
*   **scan_data**: A list of 18 distances (0 to 180 degrees).
*   **current_angle**: The servo's current position.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. The Sweep**
1.  **Loop**: Count from `angle` = 0 to 180 by steps of 10.
2.  **Actuate**: Set the servo to `angle`.
3.  **Wait**: Delay 100ms to allow the servo to physically reach the position.

**B. Data Capture**
4.  **Sample**: Take a distance reading.
5.  **Log**: Save the distance into the `scan_data` list.

**C. Analysis**
6.  **Find Path**: After a full 180-degree sweep, print the index of the highest distance (the clearest direction).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Scan**: The "Radar" head swivels from left to right.
2.  **Observe**: It notices a wall at 0-90 degrees and an open doorway at 120-150 degrees.
3.  **Result**: The Pico prints: "RECOMMENDED HEADING: 135 DEGREES."
4.  **Intelligence**: The robot is no longer "blindly" reacting; it is building a map of its surroundings.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); servo.freq(50)
trig = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.IN)

scan_results = [0] * 19 # 0, 10, 20... 180

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

for i in range(19):
    angle = i * 10
    # Map 0-180 to servo duty
    duty = int(1638 + (angle * (8192-1638)/180))
    servo.duty_u16(duty)
    utime.sleep(0.2)
    
    dist = get_dist()
    scan_results[i] = dist
    print(f"Angle {angle}: {dist:.1f}cm")

# Simple logic: Find index of largest distance
best_dir = scan_results.index(max(scan_results)) * 10
print(f"CLEAREST PATH IS AT: {best_dir} DEGREES")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mechanical Slop**: If the sensor mount is loose, the readings will "jitter" as the servo moves. Ensure the mount is tight and use a `utime.sleep` after the servo move to let the vibration stop.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Continual Scanning**: Use a non-blocking sweep that scans left-to-right, then right-to-left continuously while the robot drives.

---

## 1. Project 1273: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dynamic Braking Distances". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Variable Thresholds" that change based on speed. As the robot drives faster, the "Obstacle! Stop!" distance increases to account for momentum, preventing skidding or collisions.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Velocity-Dependent Confidence**: Understanding that high-speed movement requires more room to stop.
*   **Proportional Deceleration**: Slowly reducing motor speed as an object gets closer, rather than a harsh "Digital" stop.
*   **Kinematic Safety**: Mapping current speed to a safety-buffer variable.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **L298N or similar Motor Driver**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **PWM Speed** | GP15 | Motor Throttle |
| **Distance** | GP16/17 | Pulse Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `pico_scale`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else_if`**
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `set_motor_speed`**

### 7. Variables
*   **current_speed**: PWM value (0-65535).
*   **dynamic_threshold**: Calculated safety distance.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Throttle Setting**
1.  **Set Speed**: Drive motors at 80% power.

**B. Calculation**
2.  **Safety Buffer**: Calculate `dynamic_threshold = 20 + (current_speed / 2000)`.
    *   Example: At low speed, threshold is 20cm. At high speed, it reaches 50cm.

**C. Active Response**
3.  **Watch**: Sample center distance.
4.  **Branch**:
    *   If `dist` < `dynamic_threshold`: Turn motors OFF and reverse for 500ms.
    *   Else: Continue driving.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Slow Cruise**: Robot drives slowly. It gets within 20cm of a chair before stopping.
2.  **Turbo**: You increase speed. The robot now "sees" the chair and stops at 45cm.
3.  **Result**: Professional-grade braking logic that respects the laws of physics and prevents hardware damage.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m1 = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15)); m1.freq(1000)
trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)

speed = 50000 # 75% Throttle

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

while True:
    d = get_dist()
    # Higher speed = higher stop distance
    # (Speed 50000 / 2000) + 15 = 40cm
    safety_zone = (speed / 2000) + 15
    
    if d < safety_zone:
        print(f"EMERGENCY BRAKE! Dist {d:.1f} < Threshold {safety_zone:.1f}")
        m1.duty_u16(0)
        utime.sleep(2) # Stop
    else:
        m1.duty_u16(speed)
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Fixed Thresholds**: Most beginner robots use a fixed "20cm" limit. This works on carpet but fails on smooth tile where the robot might slide 10cm after the motors stop. Always calibrate your buffer to your surface.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Gentle Stop**: Instead of `duty_u16(0)`, use a loop to decrease speed from `speed` to 0 over 500ms to mimic a real car's brakes.

---

## 1. Project 1274: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"LCD Proximity Alert Matrix". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a 3-segment visual display on an LCD or OLED that shows the "Threat Direction" (Left, Front, Right) in real-time, providing a cockpit-style view of the robot's surroundings.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Spatial UI Design**: Representing 3D objects as 2D symbols on a screen.
*   **Multi-Core Display Updates**: Keeping the screen fresh without slowing down the sensor loop.
*   **ASCII Art Graphics**: Using character blocks `[ ]` to create a visual "Radar" panel.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x IR Obstacle Sensors**
*   **16x2 LCD (I2C)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SDA/SCL**| GP4/5 | I2C UI |
| **L/C/R Sensors**| GP10/11/12 | Input Array |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Smart Display, drag `set_lcd_cursor`**
*   **from Smart Display, drag `print_lcd_text`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_else`**

### 7. Variables
*   **status_str**: A text representative of the obstacle map (e.g., "| ! | |").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Layout**
1.  **Initialize**: Clear the LCD and print "[ L ] [ C ] [ R ]" on the top row.

**B. Detection**
2.  **Poll**: Read all three sensors.
3.  **Construct**:
    *   If Left sensor blocked, character 1 = "X", else character 1 = "_".
    *   Repeat for Center and Right.

**C. Visual Update**
4.  **Print**: Show the constructed string "[ X ] [ _ ] [ X ]" on the bottom row.
5.  **Refresh**: Only update the LCD if the sensor state has changed (Delta check).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Empty Room**: LCD shows "[ _ ] [ _ ] [ _ ]".
2.  **Person on Left**: LCD shifts to "[ X ] [ _ ] [ _ ]".
3.  **Box in Front**: LCD shifts to "[ _ ] [ X ] [ _ ]".
4.  **Result**: A professional diagnostic panel that allows you to see what the robot "sees" without using a laptop.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, i2c_lcd

# Mock LCD setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
lcd = i2c_lcd.I2cLcd(i2c, 0x27, 2, 16)

s_l = machine.Pin(10, machine.Pin.IN)
s_c = machine.Pin(11, machine.Pin.IN)
s_r = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.IN)

last_state = ""

while True:
    # 0 = Blocked, 1 = Clear
    l = "X" if s_l.value() == 0 else "_"
    c = "X" if s_c.value() == 0 else "_"
    r = "X" if s_r.value() == 0 else "_"
    
    current = f"[{l}] [{c}] [{r}]"
    
    if current != last_state:
        lcd.clear()
        lcd.putstr("SENSORS: L C R\n")
        lcd.putstr(current)
        last_state = current
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **LCD Latency**: Writing to an I2C LCD takes about 50-100ms. If you update the screen *every* loop, your robot's reaction time will drop from 100Hz to 10Hz. Use the "Delta Check" (only update if state changes) as shown in the code.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Distance Text**: Instead of "X", show the actual distance in centimeters (e.g., "[12] [45] [08]") for each sector.

---

## 1. Project 1275: Automated Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Autonomous Maze Solver - Right Hand Rule". At the end of this project, you will be able to program any wheeled robot to navigate a complex maze without human intervention by following the "Wall Follower" algorithm, using a side-mounted sensor to maintain a fixed distance from the right-hand wall.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Wall Hugging Logic**: Steering slightly left if too close to the wall, and slightly right if too far away.
*   **Dead-Ends and Juncions**: Detecting when the path ahead is blocked and executing a pivot turn.
*   **Proportional Control (P-Loop)**: Using the error (Distance - 15cm) to calculate the steering correction magnitude.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x HC-SR04 (Front + Right Side)**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Front Sonar** | GP16/17 | Collision Stop |
| **Right Sonar** | GP18/19 | Guidance Source |
| **L/R Motors** | GP10-13 | Propulsion |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `drive_tank`**
*   **from Math, drag `pico_abs_diff`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_elseif_else`**

### 7. Variables
*   **wall_dist**: Current reading from the Right sensor.
*   **target_gap**: 15cm (The ideal distance from the wall).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Forward Motion**
1.  **Check Front**: If Front Distance < 10cm, Spin Left 90 degrees (Corner logic).

**B. P-Loop Steering**
2.  **Measure**: Read Right sensor.
3.  **Correct**:
    *   If `wall_dist` > 20cm: Turn Right slightly (Path is opening).
    *   If `wall_dist` < 10cm: Turn Left slightly (Too close to wall).
    *   Else: Drive straight.

**C. Corner Handling**
4.  **Loop**: Continuously run this check every 50ms to ensure a smooth "S-curve" path along the wall.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Robot enters the maze.
2.  **Tracking**: It finds the right-hand wall and follows it like a rail.
3.  **Obstacle**: It hits a corner. Front sensor triggers. Robot pivots left and continues following the new wall.
4.  **Result**: An infinite loop that will eventually find the exit of any non-circular maze.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m_left = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(10)); m_left.freq(1000)
m_right = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(12)); m_right.freq(1000)

trig_f = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT); echo_f = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
trig_r = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT); echo_r = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.IN)

def get_dist(t, e):
    t.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    t.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); t.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(e, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

while True:
    df = get_dist(trig_f, echo_f)
    dr = get_dist(trig_r, echo_r)
    
    if df < 15: # Front Wall - Turn Left
        m_left.duty_u16(0); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
        utime.sleep(0.5)
    else:
        # P-Control: Target 15cm from right wall
        if dr > 20: # Drifting away - Turn Right
            m_left.duty_u16(40000); m_right.duty_u16(20000)
        elif dr < 10: # Too close - Turn Left
            m_left.duty_u16(20000); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
        else: # On Track
            m_left.duty_u16(40000); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **90-Degree Bias**: If your robot turns 90 degrees too quickly, it might lose the wall entirely. Use "Differential Steering" (one motor faster than the other) instead of a full "Spin Turn" for wall tracking.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **PID Loop**: Implement full PID (Proportional-Integral-Derivative) for incredibly smooth wall tracking that doesn't wobble.

---

## 1. Project 1276: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Cliff Detection Interlock". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a "Floor Watcher" sensor that points downward. If the robot moves over an edge (stairs or table lip), the sensor detects "Infinite Distance," triggering a high-priority motor disconnect to prevent a catastrophic fall.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Inverted Logic**: Interpreting a *surge* in distance as a danger signal.
*   **Fail-Safe Latency**: Ensuring the cliff check is the very first thing the CPU does in every loop cycle.
*   **Latch Recovery**: Requiring a manual reset after a cliff trigger to ensure the robot is physically moved to a safe location.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 (Down-Facing)**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Floor Sonar** | GP14/15 | Cliff Watcher |
| **Motors** | GP10-13 | Drive System |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `set_digital_pin`**
*   **from Wait, drag `wait_until`**

### 7. Variables
*   **floor_dist**: Distance to the ground (usually 5-10cm).
*   **is_safe**: Boolean system lock.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Calibration**
1.  **Measure Deck**: Check the distance between the sensor and the floor. Set `CLIFF_POINT` = `floor_dist + 10cm`.

**B. The Watchdog**
2.  **Poll**: Continuously read `floor_dist`.
3.  **Detect Drop**:
    *   If `floor_dist` > `CLIFF_POINT`:
        *   **Action**: Set Motor PWM to 0.
        *   **Action**: Set `is_safe` = False.
        *   **Alarm**: Sound buzzer continuously.

**C. Safety Lock**
4.  **Wait**: Do not drive again until the robot is picked up and placed on solid ground (where `floor_dist` returns to normal).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Drive**: Robot navigates toward the edge of a table.
2.  **Edge**: The front wheel goes over. The sensor sees 2 meters (the floor below) instead of 5cm (the table).
3.  **Save**: The motors reverse instantly. The robot survives the "Cliff."
4.  **Result**: Industrial-grade safety interlock for vacuums and exploration crawlers.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m_pwr = machine.Pin(10, machine.Pin.OUT) # Global motor enable
cliff_trig = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
cliff_echo = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def check_cliff():
    cliff_trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    cliff_trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); cliff_trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(cliff_echo, 1, 30000) * 0.017
    # If distance > 15cm (on a table 5cm high), we are falling
    if d > 15 or d < 0: return True
    return False

while True:
    if check_cliff():
        print("!!! CLIFF DETECTED - EMERGENCY STOP !!!")
        m_pwr.off()
        while check_cliff(): # Wait until placed back on ground
            utime.sleep(1)
        print("Back on solid ground. Resetting.")
        m_pwr.on()
        
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **ADC Noise**: Cheap sensors might report a "0" or "infinite" reading randomly for 1ms. If you stop the robot every time this happens, it will move very choppily. Use a "Counter" (must see cliff 3 times in a row) to confirm the danger.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Self-Correction**: Instead of just stopping, have the robot reverse and rotate 180 degrees to search for a safe path.

---

## 1. Project 1277: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Obstacle Density Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an automated logger that records the number of "Near-Misses" (obstructions < 10cm) per hour, allowing you to generate a "Safe-to-Drive Map" of a room to see which areas are too cluttered for the robot.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Interval Binning**: Storing data into hourly or minute-based buckets for statistical review.
*   **Flash Memory Logging**: Writing sensor counts to a text file on the Pico's internal file system.
*   **Frequency Analysis**: Identifying "High-Clutter Zones."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x IR Obstacle Sensors**
*   **Pico Serial Monitor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensors** | GP10-12 | Obstacle Sources |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Lists, drag `append_to_list`**
*   **from Smart IO, drag `ticks_ms`**
*   **from Comm, drag `write_to_file`**

### 7. Variables
*   **clutter_count**: Number of obstructions seen in the current 10-minute window.
*   **density_map**: A list representing hourly clutter levels.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Monitoring**
1.  **Poll**: Read all IR sensors. If any are blocked, increment `clutter_count`.

**B. Temporal Save**
2.  **Timer**: Check if 10 minutes have passed.
3.  **Log**:
    *   Write `clutter_count` to the internal `clutter_log.txt` file.
    *   Reset `clutter_count` to 0.

**C. Audit Report**
4.  **Dump**: When requested, print the entire file to the terminal to see which part of the day was most "Obstacle Rich."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Setup**: Robot drives around the living room.
2.  **Clutter**: It bumps into shoes, chairs, and pets.
3.  **Result**: The log shows "12:00PM: 45 hits", "2:00PM: 120 hits".
4.  **Conclusion**: You realize the room is too messy for the robot to work efficiently between 2 and 3 PM when people are home.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

sensors = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.IN) for i in [10, 11, 12]]
hits = 0
last_save = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    for s in sensors:
        if s.value() == 0:
            hits += 1
            utime.sleep(0.5) # Debounce hit
            
    # Every 60 seconds (scaled down for demo)
    if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_save) > 60000:
        with open("clutter.txt", "a") as f:
            f.write(f"Interval Hits: {hits}\n")
        print(f"Data Logged: {hits} obstructions.")
        hits = 0
        last_save = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **File Wear**: Writing to the Pico's flash memory too often (e.g., every 10ms) can eventually "wear out" the memory. Only write data every 10-60 minutes, or the end of a session.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Spatial Logging**: If using encoders, log the exact (X,Y) coordinates whereobstacles are found.

## 1. Project 1278: Smart Advanced Obstacle Avoidance System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Networked Obstacle Map Streaming". At the end of this project, you will be able to connect the Pico W to a central "Control Tower" (Your PC) via WiFi and stream a real-time byte-array of obstacle data, allowing you to visualize the robot's world from a remote laptop.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Remote Procedure Calls (RPC)**: Sending sensor data as a standardized "Packet" over TCP/IP.
*   **Binary Data Encoding**: Converting a list of 18 distances into a compact binary format for fast transmission.
*   **Dashboard Integration**: Viewing a live "Radar" ring on your PC screen powered by Pico W data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W**
*   **HC-SR04 on a Servo (Radar head)**
*   **Laptop with Python/Networking**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W WiFi**| Internal | Telemery Link |
| **All Sensors** | GP10-18 | Data Feed |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Internet, drag `connect_wifi`**
*   **from Internet, drag `tcp_stream_send`**
*   **from Lists, drag `to_byte_array`**

### 7. Variables
*   **packet_id**: Serial number for each sweep.
*   **telem_string**: Formatted data string "12,45,0,0,10...".

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Wireless Link**
1.  **Connect**: Join the local WiFi network.
2.  **Handshake**: Open a TCP socket to the server's IP.

**B. Data Serializing**
3.  **Scan**: Perform a 180-degree sweep (Project 1272 logic).
4.  **Flatten**: Convert the list of distances into a comma-separated string.

**C. Streaming**
5.  **Broadcast**: Send the string via the WiFi socket.
6.  **Loop**: Refresh every 3 seconds for a continuous remote update.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Robot enters the dark basement.
2.  **Stream**: It starts spinning its sensor and sending data.
3.  **Result**: Your laptop upstairs shows a green "Radar" sweep, highlighting exactly where the basement walls and pillars are.
4.  **Advantage**: You can monitor the robot's exploration safely from another room.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network, socket, utime, machine

WIFI = "YourSSID"
PASS = "YourPass"
PC_IP = "192.168.1.100"

def get_scan():
    # Placeholder for Project 1272 logic
    return [utime.ticks_ms() % 100] * 18

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(WIFI, PASS)
while not wlan.isconnected(): utime.sleep(1)

sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
sock.connect((PC_IP, 8080))

while True:
    data = get_scan()
    msg = ",".join(map(str, data))
    try:
        sock.send(msg.encode() + b"\n")
    except:
        break
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **WLAN Timeout**: If the robot drives too far from the router, the WiFi will drop. Add a `try/except` block to your code to handle reconnections automatically so the robot doesn't crash while waiting for a network it can't find.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Command Control**: Let the PC send a command BACK to the robot: "STOP" or "TURN LEFT" based on the map.

## 1. Project 1279: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Sensor Parallax Wizard". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a calibration routine that calculates the "Alignment Offset" of two sensors. Since sensors are never perfectly parallel, you will use math to "Zero" the sensors so they agree on what "Straight Ahead" looks like.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Angular Offset Correction**: Identifying if Sensor A is angled 2 degrees further left than Sensor B.
*   **Software Zeroing**: Adding/Subtracting a constant `offset` to every sensor reading to normalize the array.
*   **Interactive Tuning**: Using serial commands to increase/decrease the offset until both sensors read 100cm when pointed at a wall 100cm away.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x HC-SR04 (L/R Pair)**
*   **Measuring Tape (for reference)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor L** | GP16/17 | Offset A |
| **Sensor R** | GP18/19 | Offset B |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `add_offset`**
*   **from Comm, drag `read_serial_input`**
*   **from Logic, drag `if_key_pressed`**

### 7. Variables
*   **offset_l**, **offset_r**: Calibration constants.
*   **wall_baseline**: 100.0cm.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Reference Setup**
1.  **Position**: Place both sensors exactly 50cm from a flat wall.

**B. Gap Audit**
2.  **Poll**: Read both sensors.
    *   Sensor L reads 48cm.
    *   Sensor R reads 52cm.

**C. Correction**
3.  **Zeroing**:
    *   Set `offset_l = +2.0`.
    *   Set `offset_r = -2.0`.
4.  **Confirm**: Rerun the check. Both sensors should now output exactly 50.0cm.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Launch**: Tool starts. "Comparing sensor alignment..."
2.  **Adjust**: You see a 4cm delta.
3.  **Command**: You type "R-2" in the terminal.
4.  **Result**: Both readings "Merge" into one stable value.
5.  **Engineering**: Your robot will now drive perfectly straight toward a wall without drifting to one side.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

off_l, off_r = 0.0, 0.0

while True:
    # d_l = read_sonar_left()
    # d_r = read_sonar_right()
    d_l, d_r = 48.5, 51.2 # Mock raw data
    
    cal_l = d_l + off_l
    cal_r = d_r + off_r
    
    print(f"L: {cal_l:.1f} | R: {cal_r:.1f} | Delta: {cal_l-cal_r:.1f}")
    
    # Simple manual tuner
    cmd = input("Adjust (L+, L-, R+, R-) or 'ok': ")
    if cmd == "L+": off_l += 0.5
    if cmd == "L-": off_l -= 0.5
    if cmd == "R+": off_r += 0.5
    if cmd == "R-": off_r -= 0.5
    if cmd == "ok": break
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ignoring Temperature**: If you calibrate in a 10-degree garage and move to a 30-degree sunlit room, your offset might change! Re-run this wizard whenever the environment changes significantly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Calibrate**: Program the Pico to automatically calculate the offsets by taking 100 samples and finding the mean difference.

## 1. Project 1280: Advanced Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Vector Field Histogram Simulation". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement the industry-standard "VFH" algorithm for navigation. Instead of just turning away from obstacles, the robot creates a "Directional Probability Map" and chooses the vector that is most "Open," allowing for smooth, fluid motion through clutter.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Vector Analysis**: Combining Distance and Angle into a single "Force" that pushes the robot away.
*   **Obstacle Repulsion**: Treating obstacles as invisible "Magnets" that push the robot toward open space.
*   **Path Costing**: Evaluating 8 different headings and choosing the "Lowest Cost" path that gets closer to the goal while staying away from objects.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x HC-SR04 Array (L, C, R)**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **All Sensors** | Array | 3-Directional FOV |
| **Motors** | L/R | Differential Steering |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `calculate_vector`**
*   **from Logic, drag `weight_path`**
*   **from Motion & Motors, drag `smooth_turn`**

### 7. Variables
*   **vector_map**: A list of "Danger Scores" for Left, Center-Left, Center, Center-Right, and Right.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Scoring**
1.  **Poll Sensors**: Get distances for L, C, and R.
2.  **Weighting**:
    *   Danger score = `1.0 / dist`. (Closer = Higher Danger).

**B. Path Selection**
3.  **Evaluate**:
    *   Path 1 (Left 30 deg): Check `Danger_L` and `Danger_C`.
    *   Path 2 (Straight): Check `Danger_C`.
    *   Path 3 (Right 30 deg): Check `Danger_R` and `Danger_C`.

**C. Execution**
4.  **Drive**: Select the path with the lowest cumulative Danger Score and apply differential steering.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Navigate**: Robot enters a cluttered room (chairs, tables).
2.  **Analyze**: It doesn't "Stop-Turn-Stop." It continuously adjusts its arc.
3.  **Flow**: It fluidly snakes between two chair legs.
4.  **Result**: High-end autonomous behavior that mimics how a human walks through a crowd.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

# Mock motor and sensor setup
def get_dists():
    return [30.0, 50.0, 25.0] # [L, C, R]

def move(l_speed, r_speed):
    # Set Motor PWMs
    pass

while True:
    d = get_dists()
    
    # Calculate "Cost" for 3 paths
    cost_left = 100/d[0] + 50/d[1]
    cost_straight = 100/d[1]
    cost_right = 100/d[2] + 50/d[1]
    
    print(f"Costs: L:{cost_left:.1f} S:{cost_straight:.1f} R:{cost_right:.1f}")
    
    # Select best
    if cost_straight <= cost_left and cost_straight <= cost_right:
        move(1.0, 1.0) # Straight
    elif cost_left < cost_right:
        move(0.4, 1.0) # Arcing Left
    else:
        move(1.0, 0.4) # Arcing Right
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Over-Steering**: If your danger-weights are too high, the robot will "Panic-Turn" 180 degrees the moment it sees a wall. Use "Smoothing Factors" to ensure the steering changes are gradual.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Goal Tracking**: Add a "Goal Vector" (e.g., North) so the robot actively tries to work its way through the clutter toward a specific destination.


## 1. Project 1266: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
## 1. Project 1266: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Zonal Collision Avoidance System". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial-style "Traffic Light" safety system that uses distance thresholds to provide multi-stage visual warnings for parking or obstacle avoidance.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Safety Zones**: Defining "Safe," "Alert," and "Danger" physical regions using logic.
*   **Visual Cues**: Using color states (Green/Yellow/Red) and flash patterns (Pulse) to communicate urgency.
*   **Hysteresis in Safety**: Preventing "Blinking" between colors when an object is exactly on the boundary.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Common Cathode RGB LED**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LED RED** | GP16 | Danger State |
| **LED GREEN** | GP17 | Safe State |
| **LED BLUE** | GP18 | (For Yellow: Mix R+G) |
| **Sonar Trig** | GP14 | Trigger |
| **Sonar Echo** | GP15 | Echo |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_else_if`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`utime_sleep_ms`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   `dist_cm`: The current measured distance.
*   `warning_level`: A string describing the state ("None", "Caution", "STOP").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Threshold Definition**:
    *   **Level 1 (Green)**: Distance > 100cm. All is clear.
    *   **Level 2 (Yellow)**: Distance 50cm to 100cm. Approach slowly.
    *   **Level 3 (Red)**: Distance 10cm to 50cm. Stop immediately.
    *   **Level 4 (Blink Red)**: Distance < 10cm. Collision imminent!
2.  **Logic Execution**:
    *   Use an `if/elif/else` chain to determine which LED color to activate.
3.  **Alarm Logic**:
    *   In the "Blink" state, toggle the Red LED on and off every 100ms for high urgency.
4.  **Loop Frequency**:
    *   Update every 50ms to ensure the driver has time to react.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Approach**: You drive a toy car toward the sensor.
2.  **Feedback**: 150cm (Green) -> 80cm (Yellow) -> 30cm (Solid Red) -> 5cm (Flash Red).
3.  **Result**: You stop perfectly before hitting the wall.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# LED Pins
r_led = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
g_led = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

def set_color(r, g):
    r_led.value(r)
    g_led.value(g)

while True:
    # Read sonar (Mocked)
    d = 25.5 
    
    if d > 100:
        set_color(0, 1) # Green
    elif d > 50:
        set_color(1, 1) # Yellow (Red + Green)
    elif d > 15:
        set_color(1, 0) # Solid Red
    else:
        # Emergency Flash
        for _ in range(5):
            set_color(1, 0); utime.sleep(0.1)
            set_color(0, 0); utime.sleep(0.1)
            
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Grounding**: Forgetting a common ground between the sensor and the Pico can cause "Z-axis" floating errors where the distance jumps between 0 and 400 randomly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Beeper Sync**: Add a buzzer that beeps faster as you get closer, like a modern car's parking sensor.

---

## 1. Project 1267: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Passage Filtering". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Smart Doorway Counter" that counts people passing through a hallway while ignoring small objects (dogs, swinging arms) and stationary obstacles using time-of-flight gating.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Event Duration Gating**: Identifying that a human takes ~500ms to pass a door, whereas a hand swipe takes ~50ms.
*   **State Machine (Edge Triggering)**: Only counting when the beam is broken AND then cleared.
*   **Minimum Inactive Time**: Preventing double-counts by ignoring subsequent triggers for a split second.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **Wall/Door Frame for mounting**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **HC-SR04** | GP16/17 | Side-mounted beam |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag **`ticks_ms`**, **`ticks_diff`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`while_not`**

### 7. Variables
*   `total_count`: The number of confirmed people.
*   `break_start`: The timestamp when the beam was first interrupted.
*   `HALLWAY_WIDTH`: The distance to the opposite wall (used to detect breaks).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Establish Baseline**:
    *   Measure the empty doorway. If the wall is 100cm away, set your `THRESHOLD = 80cm`.
2.  **Detection Loop**:
    *   Wait for `distance < THRESHOLD`.
3.  **Timestamping**:
    *   Record the exact `start_time` in milliseconds.
4.  **Clearance Loop**:
    *   Wait for `distance > THRESHOLD` (The person has finished passing).
5.  **Validation**:
    *   `Duration = CurrentTime - start_time`.
    *   If `300ms < Duration < 2000ms`, then increment `total_count`.
    *   Ignore anything faster (Noise/Hands) or slower (Stationary object).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Walking**: You walk through at normal speed. The timer records 600ms. Count = 1.
2.  **Waving**: You wave your hand. The timer records 40ms. Count stays at 1.
3.  **Leaning**: You lean against the wall in the door. The timer hits 3000ms. Ignored.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Configure Sensor
def read_dist():
    # trig.high()... pulse_time... 
    return 45 # Mock person blocking beam (Wall is 100)

THRESHOLD = 80
people_count = 0

print("Smart Counter: Monitoring Doorway...")

while True:
    d = read_dist()
    
    if d < THRESHOLD:
        # Beam Broken
        start_ms = utime.ticks_ms()
        
        # Wait for beam to clear
        while read_dist() < THRESHOLD:
            utime.sleep(0.01)
            
        # Calculate duration
        duration = utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), start_ms)
        
        # Filter: Humans take 200ms - 2000ms typically
        if 200 < duration < 2000:
            people_count += 1
            print(f"PASSAGE DETECTED! Total: {people_count}")
        else:
            print(f"FILTERED: Object too fast/slow ({duration}ms)")
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking While Loop**: If someone stands in the doorway forever, the `while read_dist() < THRESHOLD` loop will never exit. In a real-world app, you should add a timeout to this loop.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Directional Pair**: Use two sensors side-by-side to determine if people are ENTERING or EXITING.

---

## 1. Project 1268: Smart Advanced Ultrasonic Distance System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Sensor Grid Abstraction Layer". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a "Firmware Wrapper" that manages four ultrasonic sensors (N, S, E, W) and generates simplified text reports over UART, hiding the complex timing and pin management from a master computer.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hardware Abstraction (HAL)**: Creating a library-like structure so a programmer can just call `get_obstacle_location()` instead of managing 8 pins manually.
*   **UART Messaging**: Sending structured data ("NORTH: 12cm") to another microcontroller or PC.
*   **Round-Robin Polling**: Efficiently scanning multiple sensors in a sequence to avoid cross-talk.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x HC-SR04 Sensors**
*   **USB-to-Serial TTL Adapter** (to view output on PC)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **UART TX** | GP0 | Telemetry Out |
| **Grid 1 (N)**| GP2/3 | Front |
| **Grid 2 (S)**| GP4/5 | Back |
| **Grid 3 (E)**| GP6/7 | Right |
| **Grid 4 (W)**| GP8/9 | Left |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag **`for_each_item`**
*   From **Comm**, drag **`uart_write_text`**

### 7. Variables
*   `sensor_map`: A list containing the pin pairs for North, South, East, and West.
*   `report`: A string being built for serial transmission.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define the Grid**:
    *   Map the pins: `N=[2,3], S=[4,5], E=[6,7], W=[8,9]`.
2.  **Sequential Scan**:
    *   Iterate through the map. Trigger the sensor, measure, and store the result.
3.  **Data Filtering**:
    *   Ignore readings > 200cm (Empty space).
4.  **Message Formatting**:
    *   Construct a string like `[OBJ] NORTH: 45cm | EAST: 12cm`.
5.  **Transmission**:
    *   Send the string over UART0 at **9600 baud**.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Operation**: The Pico scans the 4-way grid every 200ms.
2.  **Interaction**: You place your hand to the Right of the Pico.
3.  **UART Console**: Shows `[SCAN] OK | E: 12 | N: INF | S: INF | W: INF`.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Configure UART
uart = machine.UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=machine.Pin(0), rx=machine.Pin(1))

# Sensor Abstraction
grid = [
    {"dir": "NORTH", "trig": 2, "echo": 3},
    {"dir": "SOUTH", "trig": 4, "echo": 5},
    {"dir": "EAST",  "trig": 6, "echo": 7},
    {"dir": "WEST",  "trig": 8, "echo": 9}
]

def scan_grid():
    msg = "[SENSORS]"
    for s in grid:
        # Mocking the physical read process
        dist = 150 # dummy read
        if dist < 50: # Only report close obstacles
            msg += f" {s['dir']}:{dist}cm"
    return msg

while True:
    report = scan_grid()
    uart.write(report + "\n")
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Interference**: Firing all 4 sensors at once will cause the "South" echo to be caught by the "North" sensor. You must wait at least 30ms between grid points.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **JSON Packaging**: Change the UART format to JSON (e.g., `{"N": 45, "S": null}`) so a Python script on your PC can parse it easily.

## 1. Project 1269: Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Raw Echo Timing Investigation". At the end of this project, you will be able to perform a "Timing Trace Analysis" to understand how the HC-SR04 translates microsecond pulses into centimeters, and how to verify the calibration of your specific sensor module using a known physical reference.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pulse Width Modulation (Input)**: Understanding that the "Echo" pin is a square wave whose length directly equals the travel time of sound.
*   **Microsecond Resolution**: Learning why `utime.sleep_us` is critical for accurate rangefinding.
*   **Linear Regression**: Verifying that `Distance = Time * C` where C is your unique sensor's calibration constant.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04**
*   **Tape Measure (Metric)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **USB Serial** | Internal | For Data Collection |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`divide`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`time_pulse_us`**

### 7. Variables
*   `raw_us`: The total round-trip time in microseconds.
*   `calculated_cm`: The resulting distance.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Collect Raw Data**:
    *   Trigger the sensor and use `machine.time_pulse_us()` to get the raw time.
2.  **Verify Against Reality**:
    *   Place a box at exactly 10.0cm. Record the `us` value (e.g., 580us).
    *   Place it at 20.0cm. Record the `us` value (e.g., 1160us).
3.  **Find the Constant**:
    *   Dividing `us / cm` should give you ~58.
4.  **Scientific Audit**:
    *   Print to the console: `REAL: 10cm | RAW: 582us | ERROR: 0.03%`.
5.  **Zero-Point Offset**:
    *   Identify if your sensor has a "Starting Offset" (e.g., it always adds 1cm due to the housing shape).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Analysis**: You perform the 10cm and 50cm checks.
2.  **Discovery**: You find your particular sensor is 2% faster than the standard formula suggests.
3.  **Correction**: You update your formula from `/58` to `/59.2` for perfect matches.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

print("Ultrasonic Calibration Analyzer")
print("Place object at 100cm exactly.")

while True:
    # Manual Trigger
    trig.value(1); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.value(0)
    
    # Measure the width of the HIGH pulse
    raw_time = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    
    if raw_time > 0:
        # Standard physics: us / 58 = cm
        std_cm = raw_time / 58
        
        # Calculate your custom multiplier
        # (100cm / raw_time)
        print(f"RAW_US: {raw_time} | STD_CALC: {std_cm:.2f} cm")
        
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Clock Drift**: This logic assumes the Pico's internal clock is perfectly accurate. While good, it can drift with temperature.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Temperature Correction**: Combine this with Project 1261 to see how the "Raw US" changes even if the box doesn't move, just because the air got warmer.

## 1. Project 1270: Advanced Advanced Ultrasonic Distance Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Autonomous 'Bat-Navigation' Sweep". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a mobile robot that navigates autonomously by performing 180-degree "Radar Sweeps" with a servo-mounted sensor to find the largest opening in a room and navigate toward it.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Spatial Sampling**: Moving a sensor to build an internal "Map" of the environment.
*   **Max-Value Search**: Iterating through a list of distances to find the "Clear Path."
*   **Behavioral Robotics**: Moving away from simple "Bump and Turn" to "Scan and Plan."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04**
*   **SG90 Servo**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP15 | Scanner Control |
| **Sonar Trig/Echo**| GP16/17 | Eyes |
| **Motors** | GP10-13 | Drive Wheels |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag **`find_index_of_max`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`for_loop`**

### 7. Variables
*   `scan_results`: A list containing 5 distances (Left, L-Center, Center, R-Center, Right).
*   `best_dir`: The angle associated with the maximum distance.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Stop and Scan**:
    *   When an object is detected 20cm ahead, stop the robot.
2.  **Radar Sweep**:
    *   Move the servo from 0 to 180 degrees in 45-degree steps.
    *   At each step, record the distance into `scan_results`.
3.  **Decision Making**:
    *   Use `max(scan_results)` to find where the most space is.
    *   If index 0 is max, turn 90 Left. If index 4 is max, turn 90 Right.
4.  **Execute**:
    *   Drive forward into the clear space.
5.  **Bat Logic**:
    *   If all scans are < 10cm, the robot is trapped—execute a u-turn.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Encounter**: Robot drives toward a chair leg.
2.  **Scan**: Servo looks Left (15cm), Center (12cm), Right (150cm).
3.  **Result**: Robot recognizes the right side is open, turns, and continues exploring.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Configure Scanner Servo
# scan_srv = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15))...

def perform_180_scan():
    results = []
    angles = [0, 45, 90, 135, 180]
    for a in angles:
        # move_servo(a)
        utime.sleep(0.3)
        # d = read_sonar()
        d = 45 # Mock value
        results.append(d)
    return results

print("Bat-Nav System: Exploration Starting...")

while True:
    # 1. Check Path
    # if read_sonar() < 20: 
    if True: # Simulating encounter
        print("OBSTACLE! Scanning room...")
        map = perform_180_scan()
        
        # 2. Find best path
        max_dist = max(map)
        target_idx = map.index(max_dist)
        
        # 3. Choose behavior
        if target_idx < 2:
            print("Action: HARD LEFT")
        elif target_idx > 2:
            print("Action: HARD RIGHT")
        else:
            print("Action: STRAIGHT")
        
        utime.sleep(2) # Demo pause
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Servo Speed**: If you trigger the sonar while the servo is still moving, the "Echo" will come back at a diagonal, resulting in a false "Long" reading. Always wait 200ms after a servo move before pinging.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Lidar Map**: Draw the 180-degree sweep results on an OLED screen as an "Ardent Scan" radar map.

## 1. Project 1271: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Sensor Fusion (Peripheral Vision)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a multi-sensor array using both Ultrasonic and IR sensors to create a wider 180-degree safety field for robot navigation.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Fusion**: Combining data from different types of sensors (Time-of-Flight vs. Infrared Refraction) for a more reliable result.
*   **Peripheral Awareness**: Understanding why a single "Front" sensor leaves "Blind Spots" when turning.
*   **Logical OR Triggering**: Managing multiple inputs to trigger a single "Stop" behavior.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **IR Obstacle Sensor**
*   **Active Buzzer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Ultrasonic Trig** | GP16 | Center Vision |
| **Ultrasonic Echo** | GP17 | Center Vision |
| **IR Side Sensor** | GP14 | Side vision |
| **Buzzer** | GP15 | Alarm |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_or`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_ultrasonic`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_output`**

### 7. Variables
*   `dist`: Distance reading from the sonar.
*   `side_detect`: Boolean from the IR sensor.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Polling Loop**:
    *   Continuous scan of GPIO 14 (IR) and GP 16/17 (Sonar).
2.  **Logic Gate**:
    *   `if distance < 25 OR side_sensor == 0`: Trigger the alarm.
3.  **Visual Debugging**:
    *   Print a message indicating *which* sensor saw the obstacle first.
4.  **Action**:
    *   Sound the buzzer for 100ms when a breach is detected.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Clear Path**: Robot sees nothing in front or to its side. Buzzer is silent.
2.  **Side Swipe**: A wall appears to the side of the robot (IR detects).
3.  **Front Block**: A chair is directly in front (Ultrasonic detects).
4.  **Result**: The buzzer sounds if *either* condition is met, providing 180-degree protection.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
ir_side = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN)
buzz = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    duration = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return (duration * 0.0343) / 2

while True:
    d = get_dist()
    side = ir_side.value() == 0 # IR pulled low on detect
    
    if d < 25 or side:
        print(f"!!! OBSTACLE !!! Center: {d:.1f}cm, Side: {side}")
        buzz.on()
    else:
        buzz.off()
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **IR Sun Glare**: IR sensors will trigger falsely in direct sunlight. Always test indoors or in shaded areas.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual-Sided**: Add a second IR sensor on the other side for a full left-center-right view.

## 1. Project 1272: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Panoramic Environmental Scanning". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a "Radar Scanner" by mounting a distance sensor on a servo and recording distance data into a list at 10-degree intervals.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Spatial Data Buffering**: Storing multiple distance points in an array/list.
*   **Servo-Sonar Sync**: Synchronizing physical movement with measurement timing.
*   **Search Algorithms**: Finding the "Max" value in a list of readings to find the safest path.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Ultrasonic Sensor**
*   **SG90 Servo**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo PWM** | GP16 | Scanner sweep |
| **Ultrasonic Trig** | GP17 | Distance sensor |
| **Ultrasonic Echo** | GP18 | Distance sensor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Loops**, drag **`for_loop`**
*   From **Variables**, drag **`set_list_item`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`set_servo_angle`**

### 7. Variables
*   `scan_results`: A list of 19 distance values (0 to 180 degrees).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Sweep Logic**:
    *   Rotate the servo from 0 to 180 in steps of 10.
2.  **Measure and Store**:
    *   At each angle, wait 200ms for vibration to stop, then take a reading.
3.  **Data Processing**:
    *   Print the entire list to the console after the sweep.
4.  **Pathfinding**:
    *   Use the `index of max()` to determine which angle has the most clearing.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Action**: The servo sweeps its head "looking" across the room.
2.  **Observation**: The terminal prints a list of numbers (e.g., `[40, 45, 12, 10...]`).
3.  **Decision**: The Pico identifies that "Angle 50" has the largest distance.
4.  **Result**: You have successfully mapped the surroundings.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); servo.freq(50)
trig = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.IN)

scan_results = [0] * 19 # 0, 10, 20... 180

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

for i in range(19):
    angle = i * 10
    # Map 0-180 to servo duty
    duty = int(1638 + (angle/180)*8000)
    servo.duty_u16(duty)
    utime.sleep(0.2)
    
    dist = get_dist()
    scan_results[i] = dist
    print(f"Angle {angle}: {dist:.1f}cm")

# Simple logic: Find index of largest distance
best_dir = scan_results.index(max(scan_results)) * 10
print(f"CLEAREST PATH IS AT: {best_dir} DEGREES")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sweep Speed**: Moving the servo too fast will cause momentum to "smear" the sonar pulse, giving incorrect data.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **OLED Radar**: Plot these points as dots on an OLED display to see a real-time radar map.

## 1. Project 1273: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dynamic Braking Zones". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Variable Speed Thresholds," where the robot's stopping distance increases dynamically as its motor speed increases to account for momentum.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic Safety Margins**: Understanding that stopping from 100% speed requires more space than stopping from 20%.
*   **Momentum Compensation**: Using math to adjust sensor trip-points based on actuator state.
*   **Safety Interlocks**: Overriding user speed commands when a danger zone is entered.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Sonar**
*   **L9110 Motor Driver + DC Motor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Motor PWM** | GP15 | Speed control |
| **Sonar Trig** | GP16 | Trigger |
| **Sonar Echo** | GP17 | Echo |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`add`**, **`divide`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_greater`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`set_motor_speed`**

### 7. Variables
*   `speed_pwm`: Current speed of the robot.
*   `safety_threshold`: Calculated distance required to stop.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Calculate Margin**:
    *   Set `threshold = 15 + (speed / 2000)`. (Higher speed = higher threshold).
2.  **Sense**:
    *   Measure distance to the nearest object.
3.  **Override**:
    *   If `distance < threshold`, instantly set motor speed to 0.
4.  **Feedback**:
    *   Print "Emergency Stop Active" and wait 2 seconds before allowing movement.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Slow Mode**: Robot moves at 20% speed. Threshold is ~18cm. It gets close before stopping.
2.  **Fast Mode**: Robot moves at 90% speed. Threshold is ~45cm. It stops much earlier to prevent a crash.
3.  **Result**: Adaptive safety that mimics real automotive collision avoidance.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m1 = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15)); m1.freq(1000)
trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)

speed = 50000 # 75% Throttle

def get_dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

while True:
    d = get_dist()
    # Higher speed = higher stop distance
    # (Speed 50000 / 2000) + 15 = 40cm
    safety_zone = (speed / 2000) + 15
    
    if d < safety_zone:
        print(f"EMERGENCY BRAKE! Dist {d:.1f} < Threshold {safety_zone:.1f}")
        m1.duty_u16(0)
        utime.sleep(2) # Stop
    else:
        m1.duty_u16(speed)
        
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Fixed Thresholds**: Using a single 10cm threshold at high speeds will lead to the robot crashing into walls because it cannot stop instantly.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Reverse Gear**: When a stop occurs, have the robot back up 5cm automatically.

## 1. Project 1274: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Environmental Cockpit Display". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a real-time "Danger Dashboard" on an LCD that shows the occupancy of 3 separate sectors (Left, Front, Right) simultaneously using ASCII art.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **User Interface Design**: Summarizing complex sensor data into a 16x2 character grid.
*   **Symbolic Feedback**: Using characters like `[X]` for blocked and `[_]` for clear.
*   **UI Refresh Logic**: Only updating the screen when data changes (Diff-ing).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x IR Obstacle Sensors**
*   **I2C 16x2 LCD**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LCD SCL/SDA** | GP4/5 | I2C |
| **S_Left, S_Mid, S_Right**| GP10, 11, 12 | Digital Inputs |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Text**, drag **`create_text_with`**
*   From **Smart Display**, drag **`lcd_putstr`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_not_equal`**

### 7. Variables
*   `last_dashboard`: A string storing the previous screen state.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Bitmasking**:
    *   Read all three sensors. Convert to `X` (Blocked) or `_` (Clear).
2.  **String Build**:
    *   Create a string: `[L:X] [C:_] [R:X]`.
3.  **LCD Sync**:
    *   Compare to `last_dashboard`. If different, clear the LCD and write the new string.
4.  **Header**:
    *   Write "SENSORS ACTIVE" on the top row of the LCD.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: The screen shows `[_] [_] [_]`.
2.  **Action**: You put your hand on the left side.
3.  **Update**: The LCD instantly flickers to `[X] [_] [_]`.
4.  **Result**: A professional monitoring system that gives the operator an "Eye-in-the-Sky" view.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, i2c_lcd

# Mock LCD setup
i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
lcd = i2c_lcd.I2cLcd(i2c, 0x27, 2, 16)

s_l = machine.Pin(10, machine.Pin.IN)
s_c = machine.Pin(11, machine.Pin.IN)
s_r = machine.Pin(12, machine.Pin.IN)

last_state = ""

while True:
    # 0 = Blocked, 1 = Clear
    l = "X" if s_l.value() == 0 else "_"
    c = "X" if s_c.value() == 0 else "_"
    r = "X" if s_r.value() == 0 else "_"
    
    current = f"[{l}] [{c}] [{r}]"
    
    if current != last_state:
        lcd.clear()
        lcd.putstr("SENSORS: L C R\n")
        lcd.putstr(current)
        last_state = current
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **LCD Spam**: Clearing the LCD every loop (1000x/sec) will cause it to dim and show nothing. Only update when the data changes!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Distance Overlay**: If using Sonar, show the actual "cm" number on the second line.

## 1. Project 1275: Automated Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Autonomous Maze Solving (Right-Hand Rule)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a robot that navigates a corridor or maze by using "Wall Following" logic—maintaining a fixed distance from a wall using Proportional Speed control.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Proportional Feedback**: Adjusting motor speed proportional to the error (e.g., if too far from wall, turn right harder).
*   **Setpoints**: Defining the "Ideal Distance" (e.g., 15cm from wall) and trying to maintain it.
*   **Differential Steering Dynamics**: How to turn the robot by adjusting the L/R balance of motor power.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x HC-SR04 Sonars (Front and Right)**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sonar Front** | GP16/17 | Collision Stop |
| **Sonar Right** | GP18/19 | Wall Tracker |
| **Motor L/R** | GP10, 12 | PWM Speed |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`subtraction`** (Error calculation)
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`set_motor_pwm`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_greater`**

### 7. Variables
*   `dist_front, dist_right`: Current distance readings.
*   `target_dist`: 15cm (The goal wall distance).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Logic Chain**:
    *   First check `Front`. If < 15cm, STOP and spin LEFT (Search for open space).
2.  **Tracking Logic**:
    *   If `Right` > 20cm: The robot is drifting away from the wall. Turn RIGHT.
    *   If `Right` < 10cm: The robot is too close to the wall. Turn LEFT.
3.  **Equilibrium**:
    *   If `Right` is between 10-20cm, drive both motors at 50% power.
4.  **Loop Frequency**:
    *   Update every 50ms to prevent "Wrestling" with the wall.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Interaction**: You place the robot in a hallway.
2.  **Action**: It drives forward, but constantly wiggles its wheels to stay indexed off the right-side wall.
3.  **Result**: The robot completes the hallway and turns around a corner autonomously.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m_left = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(10)); m_left.freq(1000)
m_right = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(12)); m_right.freq(1000)

trig_f = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT); echo_f = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
trig_r = machine.Pin(18, machine.Pin.OUT); echo_r = machine.Pin(19, machine.Pin.IN)

def get_dist(t, e):
    t.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    t.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); t.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(e, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

while True:
    df = get_dist(trig_f, echo_f)
    dr = get_dist(trig_r, echo_r)
    
    if df < 15: # Front Wall - Turn Left
        m_left.duty_u16(0); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
        utime.sleep(0.5)
    else:
        # P-Control: Target 15cm from right wall
        if dr > 20: # Drifting away - Turn Right
            m_left.duty_u16(40000); m_right.duty_u16(20000)
        elif dr < 10: # Too close - Turn Left
            m_left.duty_u16(20000); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
        else: # On Track
            m_left.duty_u16(40000); m_right.duty_u16(40000)
            
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Cross-talk**: If the sonars fire too close together, the Front ping might be heard by the Right sensor. Ensure they trigger sequentially in the code.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Left Hand Rule**: Switch the sensor and logic to follow the left wall instead.

## 1. Project 1276: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Anti-Cliff Collision Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a downward-facing safety trigger that detects "Negative Obstacles" (cliffs, stairs, or table edges) and disables all motor power before a fall occurs.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Negative Obstacles**: Understanding obstacles that are "Empty Space" rather than physical objects.
*   **Calibration of Ground Plane**: Setting a baseline for the floor distance (e.g., 5cm) and triggering on increases (e.g., >15cm).
*   **Critical Fault Latches**: Once a cliff is detected, the robot stays off until physically moved back to solid ground.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Downward Facing HC-SR04**
*   **Main Battery Disconnect (Relay or Pin)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Cliff Sonar** | GP14/15 | Mounted facing floor |
| **Motor Power** | GP10 | Global Enable |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_greater`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_output`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`while_loop`**

### 7. Variables
*   `floor_dist`: Distance recorded to the ground.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Baseline**:
    *   Measure the distance to the floor when sitting normally (e.g. 5cm).
2.  **Safety Loop**:
    *   Every 20ms, ping the ground.
3.  **Cliff Detection**:
    *   If `distance > 15cm` (The floor has "vanished"):
        *   Kill motor power.
        *   Flash Red LED.
4.  **Recovery**:
    *   Stay in a `while cliff_detected: pass` loop until the sensor sees a valid ground again.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Operation**: Robot is driving forward on a white table.
2.  **Incident**: The robot reaches the edge. The ultrasonic pulse doesn't bounce back for 30cm.
3.  **Result**: The Pico detects the "Void" and locks the wheels instantly.
4.  **Success**: The robot hangs 1cm off the edge but doesn't fall.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

m_pwr = machine.Pin(10, machine.Pin.OUT) # Global motor enable
cliff_trig = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
cliff_echo = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def check_cliff():
    cliff_trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    cliff_trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(10); cliff_trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(cliff_echo, 1, 30000) * 0.017
    # If distance > 15cm (on a table 5cm high), we are falling
    if d > 15 or d < 0: return True
    return False

while True:
    if check_cliff():
        print("!!! CLIFF DETECTED - EMERGENCY STOP !!!")
        m_pwr.off()
        while check_cliff(): # Wait until placed back on ground
            utime.sleep(1)
        print("Back on solid ground. Resetting.")
        m_pwr.on()
        
    utime.sleep(0.02)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Angle of Incidence**: If the sonar is tilted too much, the pulse might skip off the floor and never return. Keep it perfectly perpendicular to the ground.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Reverse**: On cliff detection, slowly back up for 1 second to move away from the danger zone.

## 1. Project 1277: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Obstacle Density Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a traffic analyzer that logs the number and intensity of "Near-Misses" encounter by a robot over a 24-hour period, identifying cluttered "Problem Zones" in a home or office.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Incident Logging**: Using state memory to record an "Event" only once per encounter.
*   **Bucketing**: Accumulating hits over a specific time window.
*   **Statistical Analysis**: Determining the "Health" of a path based on collision frequency.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **IR Sensor Array (L/C/R)**
*   **Internal Storage (Flash)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **S_Array** | GP10, 11, 12 | Input signals |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Variables**, drag **`change_by_1`**
*   From **Time**, drag **`ticks_diff`**
*   From **Text**, drag **`append_to_file`**

### 7. Variables
*   `hit_count`: Current number of incidents in this interval.
*   `log_timer`: The 1nd minute ticker.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Monitor**:
    *   Continuously check for a LOW signal on any of the 3 IR sensors.
2.  **Debounce**:
    *   If triggered, increment `hit_count` and wait 500ms (to avoid double-counting the same chair leg).
3.  **Periodic Save**:
    *   Every 60,000ms (1 minute), write the `hit_count` to a text file `clutter.txt`.
4.  **Reset**:
    *   Clear `hit_count` to 0 and start the next minute.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Interaction**: The robot roams for an hour.
2.  **Observation**: Every minute, the terminal shows `Log Saved: 4 hits`.
3.  **Discovery**: You open the file and see that between minutes 10 and 15, there were 40 hits.
4.  **Result**: You have identified that the "Kitchen" is the most cluttered area for the robot.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

sensors = [machine.Pin(i, machine.Pin.IN) for i in [10, 11, 12]]
hits = 0
last_save = utime.ticks_ms()

while True:
    for s in sensors:
        if s.value() == 0:
            hits += 1
            utime.sleep(0.5) # Debounce hit
            
    # Every 60 seconds (scaled down for demo)
    if utime.ticks_diff(utime.ticks_ms(), last_save) > 60000:
        with open("clutter.txt", "a") as f:
            f.write(f"Interval Hits: {hits}\n")
        print(f"Data Logged: {hits} obstructions.")
        hits = 0
        last_save = utime.ticks_ms()
        
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Disk Wear**: Don't save to file every single time a sensor is triggered (which could be 100 times a second). Always buffer into a variable and save at intervals.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Time of Day**: Integration Project 1267 (RTC) to log *exactly* when the obstacles happened.

## 1. Project 1278: Smart Advanced Obstacle Avoidance System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Wireless Radar Telemetry Bridge". (Pico W). At the end of this project, you will be able to stream high-resolution panoramic scan data over WiFi to a remote computer, enabling "Off-Board" navigation processing and visual radar rendering on a PC dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **TCP/IP Socket Streaming**: Sending raw byte-arrays over a wireless connection.
*   **Data Serialization**: Converting a list of numbers into a comma-separated string for network transmission.
*   **Remote Rendering**: Understanding that the Pico can be a "Sense Node" while a PC acts as the "Brain."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico W** (Wireless)
*   **HC-SR04 Scanner Array**
*   **Host PC running Python**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **All Sensors** | GP Array | Data source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Internet**, drag **`connect_wifi`**
*   From **Internet**, drag **`tcp_client_send`**
*   From **Text**, drag **`join_list_with_commas`**

### 7. Variables
*   `server_ip`: The address of your PC.
*   `scan_data`: The array of 18 distance points.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Network Handshake**:
    *   Join the local WiFi network. Open a socket to the PC's IP and Port (e.g. 5000).
2.  **Scan Sequence**:
    *   Perform a 180-degree sweep (as in Project 1272).
3.  **Transmit**:
    *   Join the list into a string: `"45, 50, 12, ... \n"`.
    *   Send via WiFi socket.
4.  **Loop**:
    *   Perform one scan every 1 second.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Operation**: The Pico starts roaming.
2.  **Visual**: On your PC screen, a Python script draws a green radar map based on the Pico's incoming WiFi data.
3.  **Result**: You are remotely monitoring the robot's environment from across the house.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network, socket, utime, machine

WIFI = "YourSSID"
PASS = "YourPass"
PC_IP = "192.168.1.100"

def get_scan():
    # Placeholder for Project 1272 logic
    return [utime.ticks_ms() % 100] * 18

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(WIFI, PASS)
while not wlan.isconnected(): utime.sleep(1)

sock = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
sock.connect((PC_IP, 8080))

while True:
    data = get_scan()
    msg = ",".join(map(str, data))
    try:
        sock.send(msg.encode() + b"\n")
    except:
        break
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Latency**: WiFi signals can drop. Always wrap your `socket.send` in a `try/except` block to prevent the Pico from crashing if it loses the connection.

## 1. Project 1279: Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Material Echo-Absorption Audit". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a diagnostic tool that identifies "Stealth Obstacles" (like curtains or fur) that absorb ultrasonic pulses, and calibrates the sensor's gain or threshold to compensate for different surface textures.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Acoustic Absorption**: Understanding that soft materials don't reflect sound as well as hard walls.
*   **Secondary Verification**: Using an IR sensor to cross-reference an Ultrasonic distance when the sonar "misses" an object.
*   **Confidence Scores**: Calculating a percentage of how "Sure" the robot is that the path is clear.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **HC-SR04 Sonar**
*   **Short-Range IR Sensor**
*   **Samples**: Cardboard, Foam, Carpet, Wood.

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **All Sensors** | GP Array | Comparison setup |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`difference`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_and`**

### 7. Variables
*   `sonar_dist, ir_detect`: Readings from both technologies.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Dual Sensing**:
    *   Read both Sonar (Analog Distance) and IR (Digital Presence).
2.  **Conflict Check**:
    *   If `IR == Detected` AND `Sonar > 100cm`: You have found a "Stealth Object" that has absorbed the sound wave.
3.  **Warning**:
    *   Print "STEALTH OBJECT DETECTED - SONAR UNRELIABLE".
4.  **Baseline Test**:
    *   Test different materials at a fixed 10cm distance. Note which ones cause the Sonar to fail.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Test**: A wooden block is seen by both. Confidence 100%.
2.  **Test**: A thick wool sweater is seen by IR, but the Sonar says "400cm" (Empty).
3.  **Outcome**: The tool warns you that your robot will hit "Soft" obstacles unless you trust the IR sensor more.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime

trig = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT); echo = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.IN)
ir = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN)

def dist():
    trig.low(); utime.sleep_us(2)
    trig.high(); utime.sleep_us(2); trig.low()
    d = machine.time_pulse_us(echo, 1, 30000)
    return d * 0.017 if d > 0 else 400

while True:
    d = dist()
    p = ir.value() == 0 # Presence
    
    if p and d > 50:
        print("AUDIT: Absorption Detected! (Object present but Sonar blind)")
    elif p and d < 50:
        print("AUDIT: Optimal reflection (Hard surface)")
    
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Range Limitations**: IR sensors only work up to ~10cm. Don't expect this test to work at 2 meters!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Correction**: If absorption is detected, have the robot slow its speed by 50% for safety.

## 1. Project 1280: Advanced Advanced Obstacle Avoidance Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dynamic Potential Field Navigation (PFN)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement "Vector-Based Navigation," where the robot treats obstacles as "Repelling Magnets" and its goal as an "Attracting Magnet," calculating the steering result by summing these forces together.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Vector Math for Robotics**: Summing multiple forces (Goal + Obstacle₁ + Obstacle₂) into a single steering angle.
*   **Repulsion Curves**: Understanding that the "Repelling Force" should get exponentially stronger as the obstacle gets closer.
*   **Smooth Pathing**: Eliminating jerky logic by using continuous math instead of simple `if` statements.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3-Directional Sonar Array**
*   **Digital Compass (HMC5883L)**
*   **2WD Robot Chassis**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Compass I2C** | GP0, 1 | Goal Orientation |
| **All Sensors** | Array | Repulsion Map |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`exponential_curve`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`sum_vectors`**

### 7. Variables
*   `repel_vector`: The combined force pushing the robot away from walls.
*   `attract_vector`: The force pulling the robot toward North (the goal).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Calculate Repulsion**:
    *   For L, C, and R sensors: `Force = 1.0 / (dist^2)`.
2.  **Calculate Steering**:
    *   `SteerAngle = (GoalAngle) - (R_Force - L_Force)`.
3.  **Speed Control**:
    *   If `Center_Force` is high, slow down.
4.  **Execute**:
    *   Apply the resulting SteerAngle to the motors.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Operation**: The robot aims North.
2.  **Dynamics**: As it approaches a chair on its left, the "L_Force" increases, pushing the robot's steering toward the right automatically.
3.  **Result**: The robot fluidly glides around obstacles without ever stopping or making sharp turns. It looks "Alive."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, math

# Mock sensors
def get_forces():
    # Force = 100 / distance
    # Return [L, C, R]
    return [2.0, 1.0, 10.0] # Right wall is closer

target_heading = 0 # North

while True:
    f = get_forces()
    
    # Calculate deflection
    # If Right is 10 and Left is 2, deflect left by 8 units
    deflection = (f[2] - f[0]) * 5
    
    steer = target_heading - deflection
    
    print(f"Goal: {target_heading} | Repel: {deflection} | Final Steer: {steer}")
    # drive_robot(steer)
    
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Local Minima**: Sometimes a robot will get stuck in a "Corner" where the Repulsion forces from both walls perfectly cancel its Attract force. Add a "Kick" behavior (random turn) if the robot hasn't moved in 3 seconds.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Goal**: Give the robot a list of 5 GPS waypoints (coords) to visit in order.

---


# Workspace Limits (mm)
SAFE_MIN_X = -50
SAFE_MAX_X = 50

def request_movement(x, y):
    # 1. Enforce Boundary
    safe_x = max(SAFE_MIN_X, min(x, SAFE_MAX_X))
    safe_y = max(0, min(y, 100)) # Floor is 0
    
    # 2. Alert on violation
    if safe_x != x or safe_y != y:
        print("WARNING: Target out of workspace! Clamping coordinates.")
        # beep_buzzer()
        
    # 3. Proceed with valid data
    print(f"Pathfinding to: ({safe_x}, {safe_y})")
    # move_servos_to(safe_x, safe_y)

request_movement(75, 50) # Tries to go too far right
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hard vs Soft Stops**: Never rely solely on software clamping. Ensure you have mechanical stops (or current sensing) to prevent motor damage if the sensor loses calibration.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Limits**: Change the box size based on the current height (Y). The higher the arm is, the wider it can safely reach.
