## 1. Project 1274: Advanced Servo Kinematics Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Precision Servo Diagnostic Tool (Microsecond Analyzer)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a professional-grade servo tester that translates analog potentiometer input into exact microsecond (µs) pulse-width signals, displaying performance data on a screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Potentiometer Mapping**: Converting a 0-65535 ADC value into a precise 500-2500 range.
*   **Signal Monitoring**: Visualizing the internal state of the PWM generator to verify that 90° actually equals 1500µs.
*   **Deadband Testing**: Using the tool to find the smallest movement step your servo can physically track.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **10k Potentiometer**
*   **Standard/High-Torque Servo**
*   **128x64 OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 (ADC0) | Manual Control |
| **Servo Signal** | GP16 | Under Test |
| **OLED** | I2C1 | Data Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc_weighted`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`utime_sleep_ms`**

### 7. Variables
*   `pulse_width`: The integer value in microseconds.
*   `adc_raw`: The 16-bit input from the dial.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Read the Dial**:
    *   Poll the ADC every 20ms.
2.  **Define Pulse Range**:
    *   Modern servos typically use **500µs** (Full Left) to **2500µs** (Full Right).
3.  **The Math**:
    *   `µs = 500 + (adc_raw / 65535) * 2000`.
4.  **Hardware Output**:
    *   Set `servo.duty_ns(pulse_width * 1000)`. Note that `duty_ns` uses nanoseconds, so we multiply by 1000.
5.  **Telemetry**:
    *   Clear the OLED. Print "SERVO TESTER", the current `µs` value, and the estimated angle.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Initialization**: OLED shows "CENTER: 1500µs".
2.  **Operation**: You slowly turn the pot clockwise.
3.  **Feedback**: The servo moves smoothly. The screen updates to "POS: 1852µs".
4.  **Result**: You can now precisely identify the "Physical Limits" of unknown servos.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Peripherals
pot = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)

# Display helper
def show_stats(us):
    # oled.text(f"PULSE: {us}us", 0, 10)
    print(f"DIAGNOSTIC: Pulse Width = {us} microseconds")

while True:
    raw = pot.read_u16()
    # Map 0..65535 to 500..2500
    us = 500 + (raw / 65535) * 2000
    
    # ns = us * 1000
    servo.duty_ns(int(us * 1000))
    
    show_stats(int(us))
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Nanosecond Mixup**: The command `duty_u16()` uses relative duty cycle (0-65535), but `duty_ns()` uses absolute time. Use `duty_ns` for high-precision servo work to avoid rounding errors.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Center Button**: Add a physical button on GP15 that, when pressed, overrides the Pot and sets the pulse to exactly 1500µs for quick centering.

---

## 1. Project 1275: Automated Advanced Servo Kinematics Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Organic Motion Planning (Bezier Spline Interpolation)". At the end of this project, you will be able to move a robotic joint along a smooth, non-linear path using Quadratic Bezier curves, replacing robotic "jerk" with natural, fluid movement.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Spline Interpolation**: Calculating intermediate points between A and B based on a control point (C) that "Pulls" the path into a curve.
*   **Quadratic Bezier Formula**: `P = (1-t)²P₀ + 2(1-t)tP₁ + t²P₂`.
*   **Timed Steps (`t`)**: Iterating from 0.0 (Start) to 1.0 (Finish) with fixed time increments to control speed.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **High-Quality Servo (Metal Gear)**
*   **Arm Assembly**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Servo Signal** | GP16 | Smooth Channel |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`power`**, **`multiply`**, **`for_loop`**

### 7. Variables
*   `P0, P1, P2`: The Start, Control, and End angles.
*   `t`: The floating-point "Time" or progress through the curve.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define Keyframes**:
    *   `Start = 0`, `End = 180`, `Apex = 150` (The curve will "Lean" toward 150).
2.  **Resolution Selection**:
    *   Set `STEPS = 50`. This means the move will take 50 small updates.
3.  **The Spline Calculation**:
    *   Inside a loop, calculate `t = current_step / 50`.
    *   Apply the Bezier formula to find the `target_angle`.
4.  **Execute Move**:
    *   Command the servo to the new `target_angle`.
    *   Wait exactly 20ms between steps to maintain consistent velocity.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Straight Move**: Typically, a servo jumps from 0 to 180 as fast as possible.
2.  **Spline Move**: The arm starts slowly, accelerates through the middle, and gently slows down into the final position.
3.  **Result**: The movement looks "Human" or "Organic" rather than "Machine-like."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16))
servo.freq(50)

def set_angle(angle):
    # Map 0-180 to 500,000ns - 2,500,000ns
    ns = 500000 + (angle / 180) * 2000000
    servo.duty_ns(int(ns))

def move_smooth(p0, p1, p2, duration_ms):
    steps = 40
    dt = duration_ms / steps
    
    for i in range(steps + 1):
        t = i / steps
        # Quadratic Bezier
        # (1-t)^2 * p0 + 2*(1-t)*t * p1 + t^2 * p2
        val = ((1-t)**2)*p0 + (2*(1-t)*t)*p1 + (t**2)*p2
        
        set_angle(val)
        utime.sleep_ms(int(dt))

# Move from 0 to 180 with a control pull at 150
move_smooth(0, 150, 180, 2000)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Dt too large**: If your steps are too far apart (e.g. 100ms), the servo will look "Step-like." Keep the `utime.sleep` near 20ms (the native frequency of the servo protocol).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cubic Splines**: Add a FOURTH control point (P3) to create S-curves that can navigate complex 3D obstacles.

---
### 12. Try This Next
*   **Ease-In**: Modify `t` to `t*t` for slow start.

## 1. Project 1276: Advanced Servo Kinematics Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Hardware-Interrupt Emergency Stop (E-Stop)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a high-priority interrupt system that monitors a physical button and instantly disables all servo pulses (Limp Mode) to prevent mechanical damage or injury during a robotic malfunction.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Hardware Interrupts (IRQ)**: Understanding how the CPU can "Pause" its main code instantly to handle a critical event like a button press.
*   **Fail-Safe State**: Designing a system that defaults to a safe condition (no power to motors) when an alarm is triggered.
*   **Software Reset vs. Hard Halt**: Deciding whether to require a resume or a full reboot for safety after a stop event.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Big Red Mushroom Button (or standard push button)**
*   **Servo Motor**
*   **Red LED (Status Indicator)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **E-Stop Button** | GP14 | Pull-up Resistor |
| **Servo Signal** | GP16 | PWM Output |
| **Alarm LED** | GP15 | Safety Status |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`on_interrupt_high`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`disable_pwm`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   `system_halted`: A boolean flag that prevents any further movement commands once the alarm is set.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Configure the Main Loop**:
    *   Set the servo to move back and forth in a continuous "working" pattern.
2.  **Define the Interrupt Handler**:
    *   Write a function `emergency_stop(pin)` that:
        1.  Immediately calls `servo.deinit()`.
        2.  Sets the Red LED to HIGH.
        3.  Sets a global flag `system_halted = True`.
3.  **Attach the IRQ**:
    *   Bind the handler to GP14 on a **Falling Edge** (triggered when the button is pushed).
4.  **Enforce the Halt**:
    *   In the main loop, wrap all movements in an `if not system_halted:` block.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Operation**: The robot is moving its arm to pick up an object.
2.  **Emergency**: You notice a wire is about to get snagged.
3.  **Trigger**: You hit the E-stop button.
4.  **Result**: The servo immediately goes "Limp" (no resistance). The Red LED turns on. The Pico stops sending movement commands.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Configure Hardware
stop_btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)
alarm_led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
servo_pin = machine.Pin(16)
servo = machine.PWM(servo_pin)
servo.freq(50)

is_halted = False

def emergency_stop(pin):
    global is_halted
    # 1. Kill the PWM signal immediately
    servo.deinit()
    # 2. Set the Lock
    is_halted = True
    # 3. Visual Feedback
    alarm_led.value(1)
    print("!!! E-STOP TRIGGERED !!! SYSTEM HALTED")

# Attach IRQ (High Priority)
stop_btn.irq(trigger=machine.Pin.IRQ_FALLING, handler=emergency_stop)

while True:
    if not is_halted:
        # Standard routine (sweep)
        for duty in range(1000, 9000):
            if is_halted: break
            servo.duty_u16(duty)
            utime.sleep_ms(1)
    else:
        # Stationary loop - requires hard reset
        utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Main Loop Block**: Using extremely long `utime.sleep` calls in the loop won't stop the interrupt from firing, but it might prevent the *rest* of your logic from cleaning up secondary systems (like displays) efficiently.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Watchdog Timer**: Add a `machine.WDT` that automatically resets the Pico if the main software loop stops responding.

---

## 1. Project 1277: Advanced Servo Kinematics Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Teach-and-Repeat Motion Capture". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a robot that "Learns" movements by moving it manually, recording the joint positions to the Pico's internal flash memory, and replaying them with high precision.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Manual Override sensing**: Using an external potentiometer to simulate manual "Puppeting" of a robotic joint.
*   **Temporal Sampling**: Recording a "Snapshot" of the joint's position at a fixed sample rate (e.g., 20Hz).
*   **Data Serialization**: Storing arrays of motion data in RAM and replaying them sequentially.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **SG90 Servo**
*   **10k Potentiometer** (to sense manual positioning)
*   **Recording Toggle Switch**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Feedback Pot** | GP26 | ADC Input |
| **Servo** | GP16 | Playback Output |
| **Rec Button**| GP14 | Mode Control |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Lists**, drag **`append_to_list`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`utime_sleep_ms`**

### 7. Variables
*   `motion_buffer`: A list of recorded 16-bit positions.
*   `is_recording`: A boolean state for the "Learn" mode.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Recording Mode**:
    *   While the button is held (LOW), read the Potentiometer value every 50ms.
    *   Map the value to a servo-compatible pulse and save it to `motion_buffer`.
2.  **Visual Confirmation**:
    *   While recording, move the servo to the same position as the pot so you can see what is being captured.
3.  **Memory Management**:
    *   Stop recording once the buffer hits a pre-defined limit (e.g., 500 frames).
4.  **Playback Mode**:
    *   When the button is released, iterate through the list.
    *   Send each frame to the Servo PWM with the same 50ms timing.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Teaching**: You hold the button and move the Pot back and forth to mimic a "Wave."
2.  **Processing**: The Pico saves these coordinates into its memory.
3.  **Repeat**: You let go of the button. The servo starts waving on its own, repeating your exact timing and path.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); servo.freq(50)
pot = machine.ADC(26)
btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

buffer = []

while True:
    if btn.value() == 0:
        # --- RECORDING ---
        print("RECORDING...")
        buffer = []
        while btn.value() == 0:
            val = pot.read_u16()
            buffer.append(val)
            # Live feedback
            servo.duty_u16(val)
            utime.sleep_ms(50)
        print(f"Captured {len(buffer)} frames.")
        
    else:
        # --- PLAYBACK ---
        if len(buffer) > 0:
            print("REPLAYING...")
            for pos in buffer:
                if btn.value() == 0: break # Abort if user wants to re-record
                servo.duty_u16(pos)
                utime.sleep_ms(50)
        utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sample Rate**: If your pause (`utime.sleep_ms`) is too large, the motion will look "choppy" during replay. 50ms (20Hz) is the standard for smooth servo puppeteering.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Mirroring**: Connect two servos and have Servo A "Teach" its position to Servo B in real-time.

---

## 1. Project 1278: Smart Advanced Servo Kinematics System

### 2. Learning Objective
"Modular Hexapod Leg Controller". At the end of this project, you will be able to build an intelligent sub-controller that manages a 3-DOF (Degree of Freedom) robotic leg—calculating the complex coordination between Hip, Knee, and Ankle joints based on high-level spatial commands.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Kinematic Chains**: Understanding how the move of a "Shoulder" joint changes the base position for the "Elbow."
*   **Joint Coordination**: Ensuring multiple motors reach their targets simultaneously to create straight-line foot movement.
*   **Modular Abstraction**: Dividing a complex robot (like a 6-legged walker) into smaller, manageable leg modules.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **3x High-Torque Servos** (MG90S or better)
*   **3-Joint Leg Mechanism**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Hip (Coxa)** | GP16 | Rotation |
| **Knee (Femur)**| GP17 | Extension |
| **Ankle (Tibia)**| GP18 | Foot Point |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`define_function`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`map_range`**

### 7. Variables
*   `hip_ang, knee_ang, ankle_ang`: The calculated rotation for each joint.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define Geometric Sequence**:
    *   Create a function `set_leg_state(extension, lift)`.
2.  **Joint Mapping**:
    *   To "Lift" the leg, the Knee must rotate Up while the Ankle rotates Down to keep the foot vertical.
3.  **Sync Movement**:
    *   Move all three servos in small increments within a `for` loop so they sweep together.
4.  **Create a Gait**:
    *   Implement a simple "Triple-Step" loop: 1. Lift, 2. Extension, 3. Down.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Request**: You command the Pico: "Perform a step cycle."
2.  **Calculation**: The Pico coordinates the 3 servos to lift the foot clear of the ground and move it forward.
3.  **Result**: The leg moves in a smooth, mechanical stride rather than random jerks.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Joint pins
j1 = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); j1.freq(50)
j2 = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17)); j2.freq(50)
j3 = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(18)); j3.freq(50)

def leg_pose(hip, knee, ankle):
    # Command all 3 joints
    j1.duty_u16(int(1000 + (hip/180)*8000))
    j2.duty_u16(int(1000 + (knee/180)*8000))
    j3.duty_u16(int(1000 + (ankle/180)*8000))

while True:
    # 1. Lift Leg
    leg_pose(90, 45, 120)
    utime.sleep(0.5)
    # 2. Step Forward
    leg_pose(120, 45, 120)
    utime.sleep(0.5)
    # 3. Foot Down
    leg_pose(120, 90, 90)
    utime.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Collision**: It is possible to command the servos into a position where the leg parts hit each other. Always check the physical range of your joint mobility.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Synchronization**: Connect this Pico to a Master Controller via I2C to coordinate 6 legs working together in a Tripod Gait.

## 1. Project 1279: Advanced Servo Kinematics Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Multi-Point Joint Trim & JSON Config Management". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a calibration utility that allows you to "Trim" the zero-degree offset for 8 servos using a terminal interface, saving the results to a JSON file so the robot is perfectly aligned every time it boots.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Alignment Bias**: Understanding that every mechanical assembly has slight variations (the servo horn is almost never perfectly 90°).
*   **JSON Persistence**: Using structured text files to store settings that the Pico can read on startup.
*   **Interactive Menu**: Building a serial command interface to "Nudge" motor positions.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **8x Servos** (Multi-joint assembly)
*   **Laptop with Thonny IDE** (for Serial Shell interaction)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **All Servos** | GP0-GP7 | Signal Pins |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`json_stringify`**
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`open_file_write`**

### 7. Variables
*   `offsets`: A dictionary mapping Pin IDs to their degree corrections.
*   `input_str`: The raw command string from the keyboard.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Initialize Offsets**:
    *   Create a file `trim.json` containing a default mapping: `{"0": 0, "1": 0, ...}`.
2.  **Terminal Polling**:
    *   Use the `input()` function to wait for user commands like `0+5` (Add 5 degrees to joint 0).
3.  **Real-Time Update**:
    *   Immediately apply the change to the hardware so you can see the joint move.
4.  **Save Logic**:
    *   When the "SAVE" command is typed, the Pico writes the current dictionary to the flash memory using `json.dump`.
5.  **Global Apply**:
    *   In your main robot code, load this JSON and add the `offset` value to every movement command.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Interaction**: You type `2-4` in the Thonny console. Joint 2 nudges slightly to the left.
2.  **Observation**: You see the leg is now perfectly vertical.
3.  **Result**: You type `SAVE`. The robot is now calibrated for life.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import json
import machine

# Shared configuration
config = {"0": 0, "1": 0, "2": 0}

def apply_trims():
    with open("trim.json", "r") as f:
        global config
        config = json.load(f)

def nudge(pin, val):
    config[str(pin)] += val
    print(f"Joint {pin} now has offset {config[str(pin)]}")
    # move_to_center(pin + config[str(pin)])

while True:
    cmd = input("Command (e.g. 1+2 or SAVE): ")
    if cmd == "SAVE":
        with open("trim.json", "w") as f:
            json.dump(config, f)
        print("Trims locked to flash.")
    # Add parser logic for pin# and direction...
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **JSON Keys**: Remember that JSON always uses strings for keys (e.g., `"0"`) even if you treat them like numbers in your Python logic.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Bulk Reset**: Add a command that resets all offsets to zero in case you rebuild the mechanical assembly.

## 1. Project 1280: Advanced Advanced Servo Kinematics Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Universal Inverse Kinematics (IK) Resolver". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement the "holy grail" of robotics: a system that takes a target `(X, Y)` coordinate and automatically calculates the complex angles for two serial joints, including "Reachability Validation" to prevent impossible movements.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Inverse Kinematics (IK)**: The reverse of Forward Kinematics. Calculating internal motor angles based on a desired external coordinate.
*   **Law of Cosines**: Using advanced trigonometry to determine the "Elbow" angle of a 2-segment arm.
*   **Reachability Check**: Proving mathematically if a point is further away than the maximum length of the arm.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Servos** (arranged as a Shoulder and Elbow)
*   **Rigid Arm Links (L1, L2)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Shoulder Servo** | GP16 | Base Joint |
| **Elbow Servo** | GP17 | Link Joint |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`acos`**, **`atan2`**, **`sqrt`**

### 7. Variables
*   `target_x, target_y`: The goal position in millimeters relative to the base.
*   `theta1, theta2`: The output motor angles in degrees.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Lengths Definition**:
    *   Define the physical arm lengths, e.g., `L1 = 50.0`, `L2 = 50.0`.
2.  **Validation**:
    *   Calculate the distance to the target: `dist = sqrt(x² + y²)`. If `dist > (L1 + L2)`, the goal is out of reach.
3.  **Solve for Elbow (Theta 2)**:
    *   Apply the Cosine Law: `cos(t2) = (x² + y² - L1² - L2²) / (2*L1*L2)`.
    *   Calculate `theta2 = acos(clamp(cos_t2, -1, 1))`.
4.  **Solve for Shoulder (Theta 1)**:
    *   `theta1 = atan2(y, x) - atan2(L2*sin(theta2), L1 + L2*cos(theta2))`.
5.  **Motion Visualization**:
    *   Feed the Pico a series of points to draw a straight "Horizontal Line" in space and watch the servos coordinate.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Request**: Command `IK(30, 40)`.
2.  **Calculation**: The Pico runs the Trig solver in milliseconds.
3.  **Result**: The shoulder moves to 25.1° and the elbow to 68.2°. The tip of the arm is exactly at target (30, 40).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import math
import utime

L1 = 50.0
L2 = 50.0

def solve_ik(x, y):
    # Calculate target distance sq
    d_sq = x**2 + y**2
    d = math.sqrt(d_sq)
    
    # 1. Verification
    if d > (L1 + L2): return None
    
    # 2. Elbow (theta2)
    c2 = (d_sq - L1**2 - L2**2) / (2 * L1 * L2)
    s2 = math.sqrt(1 - c2**2)
    t2 = math.atan2(s2, c2)
    
    # 3. Shoulder (theta1)
    t1 = math.atan2(y, x) - math.atan2(L2 * s2, L1 + L2 * c2)
    
    return (t1, t2)

# Main Loop: Move in a line
for i in range(20, 80, 2):
    angles = solve_ik(float(i), 10.0)
    if angles:
        print(f"X:{i} -> S:{math.degrees(angles[0]):.1f} E:{math.degrees(angles[1]):.1f}")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Domain Errors**: If your math tries to do `acos(1.0000001)` because of rounding, the code will crash. Always use `min(1.0, max(-1.0, val))` to protect your trig functions.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Drawing Robot**: Mount a pen to the tip and use the IK solver to draw geometrically correct shapes on paper.

## Batch 129: Advanced Light Sensing 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1281: Advanced Light Sensing Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Differential Light Bridge (Error Compensation)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a differential sensing circuit using two LDRs, allowing you to detect the relative direction of a light source while automatically canceling out ambient noise that affects both sensors equally.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Differential Measurement**: Understanding why comparing two signals (`A - B`) is more robust than reading a single value (`A`) when searching for a direction.
*   **Common-Mode Rejection**: Learning how ambient room light (the "Common Mode") is subtracted out, leaving only the "Differential" signal from a specific light source.
*   **Voltage Divider Bridge**: Configuring two LDRs in series to create a self-balancing midpoint voltage.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2x Photoresistors (LDRs)**
*   **2x 10k Ohm Resistors** (for standard dividers)
*   **Opaque Divider** (a piece of cardboard to separate the sensors)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR 1 (Left)** | GP26 (ADC0) | Signal A |
| **LDR 2 (Right)**| GP27 (ADC1) | Signal B |
| **VCC (3.3V)** | 3V3 Pin | Reference Power |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc_weighted`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`subtract`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`utime_sleep_ms`**

### 7. Variables
*   `left_val, right_val`: The raw 16-bit readings from each sensor.
*   `light_error`: The difference between left and right (the "Tracking Error").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Mount the Sensors**:
    *   Place the two LDRs side-by-side but place a small "Wall" (divider) between them.
2.  **ADC Acquisition**:
    *   Read both ADC channels as close together in time as possible (within 1-2ms).
3.  **Signal Normalization**:
    *   Optionally, map the 0-65535 values to 0-100 for easier math.
4.  **Calculate the Delta**:
    *   `light_error = left_val - right_val`.
5.  **Interpret the Result**:
    *   If `light_error` is a large positive number, the light is coming from the **Left**.
    *   If `light_error` is a large negative number, it's coming from the **Right**.
    *   If it's near zero, the light is **Balanced** (centered).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Ambient**: Both sensors read 20,000. `Error = 0`.
2.  **Input**: You shine a torch from the left.
3.  **Processing**: `Left=45,000`, `Right=21,000`. `Error = +24,000`.
4.  **Output**: The serial console prints "Source Detected: LEFT (Intensity: 24k)".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr_left = machine.ADC(26)
ldr_right = machine.ADC(27)

def get_light_direction():
    l = ldr_left.read_u16()
    r = ldr_right.read_u16()
    
    # Differential Signal
    error = l - r
    
    # Thresholding for noise
    if abs(error) < 2000:
        return "CENTERED", error
    elif error > 0:
        return "LEFT", error
    else:
        return "RIGHT", error

while True:
    dir, val = get_light_direction()
    print(f"ORIENTATION: {dir} | DELTA: {val}")
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Mismatch**: No two LDRs are identical. If your center is skewed, add a "Trim" variable to your code to add/subtract a constant from one side to balance them.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Visualizer**: Map the error value to a servo angle (GP15) so the Pico physically "Points" at the torch as you move it.

## 1. Project 1282: Advanced Light Sensing Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Bio-Mimetic Mood Lighting (Circadian Simulator)". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an intelligent lighting system that maps external light brightness to HSV (Hue, Saturation, Value) color space, simulating natural color temperature shifts from "Warm Sunrise" to "Cool Daylight."

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Color Temperature (Kelvin)**: Mapping light intensity to a spectrum (3000K Warm to 6500K Cool).
*   **Linear Interpolation (Lerp)**: Smoothly transitioning between two RGB colors based on a single sensor value.
*   **Gamma Correction**: Adjusting LED brightness to match how the human eye perceives light (non-linear).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **Common-Cathode RGB LED**
*   **3x 220 Ohm Resistors**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Light Input |
| **Red Pin** | GP16 | PWM Color 1 |
| **Green Pin**| GP17 | PWM Color 2 |
| **Blue Pin** | GP18 | PWM Color 3 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`map_range`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`set_pwm_duty`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`color_mix`**

### 7. Variables
*   `sunlight_pct`: The current light level from 0.0 to 1.0.
*   `r, g, b`: The calculated intensities for the mood lamp.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define Target Profiles**:
    *   **Night (Low Light)**: Deep Red/Orange (`R:255, G:50, B:0`).
    *   **Day (Bright Light)**: Cool White/Blue (`R:150, G:180, B:255`).
2.  **Read & Inverse**:
    *   Standard divider voltage goes UP as it gets darker. Ensure you invert the math so `lux = 1.0` (Day) and `lux = 0.0` (Night).
3.  **Color Blending**:
    *   Use the ratio to blend: `R_out = R_night * (1-lux) + R_day * lux`.
4.  **Hardware Write**:
    *   Output the three colors via PWM to the RGB LED.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Dark Room**: The LED glows a soft, warm amber (mimicking a candle).
2.  **Flashlight Hit**: As you shine light on the LDR, the LED smoothly "Cools down" into a bright blueish-white light.
3.  **Result**: An interactive lamp that mimics the feel of natural sunlight.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
r_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(16)); r_led.freq(1000)
g_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(17)); g_led.freq(1000)
b_led = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(18)); b_led.freq(1000)

def set_mood(intensity): # intensity 0.0 to 1.0
    # Blend Night (255,50,0) to Day (150,180,255)
    r = int(255 * (1-intensity) + 150 * intensity)
    g = int(50 * (1-intensity) + 180 * intensity)
    b = int(0 * (1-intensity) + 255 * intensity)
    
    # Write to PWM (0-65535)
    r_led.duty_u16(r * 256)
    g_led.duty_u16(g * 256)
    b_led.duty_u16(b * 256)

while True:
    # Read and normalize (flip if using pull-up)
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    light_lvl = 1.0 - (raw / 65535) 
    
    set_mood(light_lvl)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Linear perceived brightness**: LEDs look 50% bright at only 10% duty cycle. For a "Professional" feel, square the color values (`r = r*r`) to apply a primitive gamma curve.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cloud Simulator**: Add random "Pulsing" to the brightness level when in "Day" mode to simulate clouds passing.

---

## 1. Project 1283: Advanced Light Sensing Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Collimated Pulse Detection (Laser Precision Targeting)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a laser detection system that uses a physical collimator (tube) and software-defined "Sudden-Spike" logic to distinguish a laser-pointer hit from ambient room light.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Signal Collimation**: Using physical tubes to restrict the "Field of View" of a sensor, increasing directional pick-up.
*   **Differential Thresholding**: Instead of checking `if light > X`, we check `if light > ambient + delta`.
*   **Dead-time**: Preventing multiple triggers from a single light pulse.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR**
*   **Opaque Tube (e.g., black straw or 3D printed sleeve)**
*   **Buzzer**
*   **Red Laser Pointer** (for testing)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Recessed in tube |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Hit Signal |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_than_else`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`absolute_value`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   `ambient_lvl`: The baseline brightness of the room.
*   `HIT_STRENGTH`: The amount of light increase required to count as a "Laser Hit."

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Mounting the Collimator**:
    *   Glue the LDR to the bottom of your dark tube. This masks room lights.
2.  **Self-Calibration**:
    *   When the Pico boots, read the LDR 50 times and average it to find the `ambient_lvl`.
3.  **High-Speed Monitoring**:
    *   Run a loop at 100Hz (10ms).
4.  **Detection Pulse**:
    *   Compare the current reading to the baseline.
    *   If `now > ambient_lvl + 15000`: We found the laser.
5.  **Action**:
    *   Turn on the buzzer. Print "TARGET STRUCK!" to the console.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Baseline**: Room light is rejected by the tube. Reading is a steady 5,000.
2.  **Aiming**: You sweep a laser across the room.
3.  **Strike**: The laser dots the LDR inside the tube. Value jumps to 45,000 instantaneously.
4.  **Result**: The Pico triggers the alarm within 0.01 seconds of the hit.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzzer = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)

# 1. Calibration
print("Calibrating Ambient...")
ambient = 0
for i in range(20):
    ambient += ldr.read_u16()
    utime.sleep(0.05)
ambient //= 20
print(f"Base: {ambient}. Ready!")

while True:
    now = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Check for Laser Spike (Delta of 10,000)
    if now > (ambient + 10000):
        print("!!! HIT DETECTED !!!")
        buzzer.value(1)
        utime.sleep(0.5)
        buzzer.value(0)
    
    utime.sleep_ms(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Fixed Thresholds**: If you hard-code `if val > 10000`, your system will break the moment someone turns on the overhead room light. Always use a **Relative** threshold.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Hit Counter**: Track how many times the target is hit and display it on an OLED.

---

## 1. Project 1284: Advanced Light Sensing Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Adaptive Sunrise Alarm (Logarithmic Temporal Ramp)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a wake-up light that calculates and displays a logarithmic brightness curve over a 30-minute window, effectively simulating a natural sunrise that feels comfortable to human eyes.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Series PWM Control**: Gradually increasing a value over a massive number of steps (e.g., thousands of steps).
*   **Log-Mapping**: Understanding that human perception of light is logarithmic; a "Linear" increase feels too bright too fast.
*   **UI Percentage conversion**: Translating a 65535 PWM value into a user-friendly "1% .. 100%" display string.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **High-Brightness White LED** (or LED Strip with Mosfet)
*   **I2C LCD/OLED Display**
*   **LDR** (used to detect if the sun is ALREADY up to skip the alarm)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Lamp LED** | GP15 (PWM) | Sunrise Output |
## 1. Project 1284: Advanced Light Sensing Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Precision Light Diagnostic Tool (Lux Meter)". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a standalone Lux meter that uses ADC sampling and a lookup table to display real-time illumination levels in SI units (Lux) on an OLED screen.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Scientific Unit Conversion**: Understanding the difference between raw voltage and SI units (Lux).
*   **Look-Up Tables (LUT)**: Using a pre-defined list of data points to approximate a complex, non-linear sensor curve without heavy math.
*   **Display Formatting**: Presenting technical data clearly on an I2C screen.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **I2C OLED Display (SSD1306)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Light Input |
| **OLED SDA** | GP4 | Display Data |
| **OLED SCL** | GP5 | Display Clock |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Smart Display**, drag **`draw_text`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`map_range`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `raw_light`: The direct 16-bit value from the ADC.
*   `lux_val`: The processed result in Lux.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Sensor Profiling**:
    *   Connect the LDR in a voltage divider.
2.  **Define the Lookup Table**:
    *   Initialize a list of values where high ADC readings match low Lux and vice versa.
3.  **Data Acquisition**:
    *   Read the LDR every 200ms using a `while_true` loop.
4.  **Conversion**:
    *   Use the LUT logic to find the closest Lux value.
5.  **Telemetry Display**:
    *   Clear the OLED and write "ILLUMINATION:" followed by the calculated Lux value.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico boots and initializes the I2C bus.
2.  **Poll**: The sensor is read. Under a deskside lamp, it reads 12,000 (raw).
3.  **Process**: The code checks the table and determines this is roughly 450 Lux.
4.  **Output**: The OLED displays "450 LUX" instantly.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
ldr = machine.ADC(26)

# Example simplified LUT
ADC_POINTS = [5000, 15000, 35000, 60000]
LUX_POINTS = [1000, 500,  100,   10]

def get_lux(val):
    for i in range(len(ADC_POINTS)-1):
        if val >= ADC_POINTS[i] and val <= ADC_POINTS[i+1]:
            # Simple linear estimate
            return LUX_POINTS[i]
    return 0

while True:
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    lux = get_lux(raw)
    oled.fill(0)
    oled.text("LUX METER", 30, 0)
    oled.text(f"{lux} lx", 40, 30)
    oled.show()
    utime.sleep(0.2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Inversion**: Remember that a standard LDR circuit often gives a *lower* ADC number when it is *brighter*. Check your wiring if the numbers go the "wrong way."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Ranging**: Change the text color or size if the Lux level exceeds 1000 (Direct Sunlight).

## 1. Project 1285: Automated Advanced Light Sensing Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Automated Solar Tracker (Dual-Axis)". At the end of this project, you will be able to create an intelligent solar orientation system that uses 4 LDRs and 2 Servos to continuously align a solar panel with the brightest point in the sky for maximum energy harvesting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dual-Axis Control**: Managing 3D orientation by combining Azimuth (Horizontal) and Elevation (Vertical) movements.
*   **Shadow Casting**: Using a physical divider (Cruciform) to create light differences between sensors.
*   **Differential Logic**: Calculating the "Error" between sensor pairs to drive motor correction.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **4x Photoresistors (LDRs)**
*   **2x MG90S Servos**
*   **Panel Bracket**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR T/B/L/R** | GP26, 27, 28, 29 | Sensor Array |
| **Servo Pan** | GP14 | Azimuth |
| **Servo Tilt**| GP15 | Elevation |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Motion & Motors**, drag **`pico_servo`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`absolute_difference`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `diff_h, diff_v`: The calculated light error for horizontal and vertical pairs.
*   `pos_h, pos_v`: The current servo angles.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Mechanical Assembly**:
    *   Mount the 4 LDRs in a star pattern around a central stick or divider.
2.  **Horizontal Scan**:
    *   Compare the Left and Right LDRs. If Left is brighter, increment the Pan servo.
3.  **Vertical Scan**:
    *   Compare Top and Bottom LDRs. If Top is brighter, increment the Tilt servo.
4.  **Deadzone Tuning**:
    *   Add a small threshold (e.g., 2000) so the servos don't jitter if the light is nearly even.
5.  **Smoothing**:
    *   Update the servos in small 1-degree steps every 50ms.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Input**: The morning sun rises in the East.
2.  **Sensor Hit**: The "Right" sensor becomes much brighter than the "Left."
3.  **Movement**: The Pan servo rotates the panel toward the East until both sensors see the same light.
4.  **Result**: The panel stays locked onto the sun's position all day long.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Pins
s_h = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14)); s_h.freq(50)
s_v = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15)); s_v.freq(50)
l_ldr = machine.ADC(26); r_ldr = machine.ADC(27)
t_ldr = machine.ADC(28); b_ldr = machine.ADC(29)

h_pos = 90; v_pos = 90

def move(pwm, ang):
    duty = 1000 + (ang/180)*8000
    pwm.duty_u16(int(duty))

while True:
    l, r = l_ldr.read_u16(), r_ldr.read_u16()
    t, b = t_ldr.read_u16(), b_ldr.read_u16()
    
    if abs(l-r) > 5000:
        if l < r: h_pos += 1 # Brighter side (assuming standard divider)
        else: h_pos -= 1
        
    if abs(t-b) > 5000:
        if t < b: v_pos += 1
        else: v_pos -= 1
        
    h_pos = max(0, min(180, h_pos))
    v_pos = max(0, min(180, v_pos))
    
    move(s_h, h_pos)
    move(s_v, v_pos)
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Binding Wires**: Ensure the wires to your solar tracker have enough "slack" to rotate 180 degrees without getting snagged on the frame.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Efficiency Log**: Log the light intensity of the panel over time to compare the energy gain between a static panel and your tracker.

## 1. Project 1286: Advanced Light Sensing Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Intrusive Light Security Alarm". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Anti-Tamper" alarm that detects if a dark enclosure (like a safe) is opened by monitoring for sudden increases in light levels using a hardware interrupt.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Adaptive Baselines**: Calibrating for "Total Dark" upon startup.
*   **Sudden Thresholding**: Triggering only on a rapid change (Delta) rather than an absolute level.
*   **Tamper Evidence**: Creating a latching alarm that stays active until manually cleared.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **Active Buzzer**
*   **Push Button** (Reset)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Enclosure Eye |
| **Buzzer** | GP14 | Alarm Siren |
| **Button** | GP15 | Reset Switch |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_greater`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_output`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `is_alarmed`: A boolean state indicating a security breach.
*   `dark_threshold`: The value representing the expected "closed" darkness.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **The Enclosure Test**:
    *   Place the Pico inside a box. Record the ADC value when the box is closed (e.g., 60,000).
2.  **Define the Breach**:
    *   A breach occurs if the value drops below 40,000 (indicating outside light has entered).
3.  **Latching Logic**:
    *   When the breach is detected, set `is_alarmed = True`.
4.  **Alarm Output**:
    *   If `is_alarmed` is True, cycle the buzzer on and off rapidly.
5.  **Manual Reset**:
    *   The alarm only stops if the physical button is pressed (to verify a human has investigated).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Standby**: The Pico is inside a safe. The sensor reads 62,500. Everything is quiet.
2.  **Tamper**: A thief opens the door. Light floods the box.
3.  **Logic**: The sensor reading drops to 15,000. The Pico triggers the alarm.
4.  **Action**: The buzzer starts screaming. The thief runs away.
5.  **Result**: The alarm continues until you arrive and push the reset button.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)
buzz = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
reset = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

alarm_state = False
# Assuming 50000+ is "Dark" inside the safe
THRESHOLD = 40000 

while True:
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    
    if val < THRESHOLD:
        alarm_state = True
        print("!!! SECURITY BREACH DETECTED !!!")

    if reset.value() == 0:
        alarm_state = False
        print("ALARM RESET")
        utime.sleep(0.5)

    if alarm_state:
        # Siren pulse
        buzz.on(); utime.sleep(0.05); buzz.off(); utime.sleep(0.05)
    else:
        buzz.off()
        utime.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Internal Reflections**: If you have a status LED on the Pico, it might light up the sensor inside the box! Cover the Pico's onboard LED with tape so it doesn't "self-trigger."

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Photo Capture**: Trigger a camera or log the exact time of the breach to a file on the Pico's storage.

## 1. Project 1287: Advanced Light Sensing Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Temporal Cloud Coverage Logger". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a long-term monitoring station that logs light intensity every 10 seconds to an internal file, allowing you to graph "Cloud Density" over a full daylight cycle.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistent Data Storage**: Writing sensor values to the Pico's internal flash memory as a `.txt` or `.csv` file.
*   **Time-Series Sampling**: Understanding fixed-interval recording for scientific data collection.
*   **File Append Mode**: Learning how to add new data to the end of an existing file without deleting previous records.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Daylight Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`open_file_append`**
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`write_to_file`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `light_reading`: The 16-bit intensity value.
*   `file_log`: The handle for the open flash file.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Placement**:
    *   Place your sensor on a windowsill where it can see the sky clearly.
2.  **File Initialization**:
    *   On the first run, the Pico will create `cloud_log.csv`.
3.  **The Sampling Loop**:
    *   Every 10 seconds, take a reading.
4.  **Logging**:
    *   Convert the number to a string and add a comma and newline (`\n`).
    *   Write the string to the file and immediately `close` or `flush` to save it safely.
5.  **Data Extraction**:
    *   After 8 hours, plug the Pico into a PC and read the file to see the data points.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Morning**: You start the script at 8:00 AM.
2.  **Interaction**: A cloud passes at 10:30 AM. The LDR value spikes (resistance increases) for 2 minutes.
3.  **Storage**: The Pico logs "45000", "45200", "46000..." during the cloud.
4.  **Result**: You have a digital record of the sky's transparency throughout the day.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

ldr = machine.ADC(26)

print("Starting Daylight Logger...")

while True:
    val = ldr.read_u16()
    # Get current runtime seconds for timestamping
    timestamp = utime.ticks_ms() // 1000
    
    # Save to Internal Flash
    try:
        with open("cloud_log.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(f"{timestamp},{val}\n")
        print(f"Logged: {timestamp}s | Intensity: {val}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"File Error: {e}")
        
    # Wait 10 seconds (standard sampling rate)
    utime.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **File Locking**: If you don't use `with open(...)` or forget `f.close()`, the Pico might run out of memory or the data might not actually write to the disk before a power failure.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Battery Power**: Run the Pico on a battery pack and leave it outside (in a weather-proof box) to capture a full 24-hour cycle of sun and moon light.

*   **Remote Override**: Implementing a simple web server on the Pico W to allow human control via a browser.

2.  **Detection**: The Pico W sees the light level hit the limit.
3.  **Action**: It automatically rotates the servo to close the blinds.
4.  **Interaction**: You decide you want to see outside anyway. You open the Pico's IP address on your phone and tap "OPEN."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import network
import socket
import utime

# Hardware Setup
ldr = machine.ADC(26)
servo = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(15)); servo.freq(50)

def set_angle(ang):
    duty = 1000 + (ang/180)*8000
    servo.duty_u16(int(duty))

# Simplified WiFi connection (Mocked)
print("Connecting to WiFi...")
# wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True); wlan.connect('SSID', 'PASS')

while True:
    lux = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Auto logic
    if lux < 20000: # Very Bright (assuming pull-down)
        print("Sun too bright! Closing shutters...")
        set_angle(0)
    else:
        set_angle(90)
        
    utime.sleep(5) # Check every 5 seconds
```

### 11. Common Mistakes

## 1. Project 1289: Advanced Light Sensing Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"LDR Response Curve Profiler". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a diagnostic tool that measures the non-linear response of an LDR across multiple light levels and generates a mathematical "Normalization Curve" to ensure accurate sensing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sensor Characterization**: Understanding that sensor response isn't always a straight line (Log-Log response).
*   **Normalization**: Using math to convert raw values from different sensors into a unified 0-100% scale.
*   **Regression Analysis**: Finding the "best fit" line through data points to predict values between them.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **LDR (Photoresistor)**
*   **Smartphone with Lux Meter** (for reference checking)
*   **Dimmer-controlled Light Source**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **LDR** | GP26 | Test Sensor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`exponential_curve`**
*   From **Lists**, drag **`add_to_list`**
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`print_to_serial_plotter`**

### 7. Variables
*   `test_vals`: A list of readings at various brightness levels.
*   `curve_factor`: The variable used to "flatten" the non-linear data.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Data Collection**:
    *   Measure the LDR in Total Dark, Room Light, Desk Light, and Direct Flashlight.
2.  **Plotting**:
    *   Send these values to the Serial Plotter. Notice how they don't change by the same amount even if the light increases evenly.
3.  **Coefficient Search**:
    *   Apply an exponential formula: `normalized = raw ^ factor`.
4.  **Tuning**:
    *   Adjust the `factor` (usually between 0.5 and 0.8) until the curve looks straight on the serial plotter.
5.  **Implementation**:
    *   Use this final formula in all future projects for high-precision light measurement.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Run**: You start the profiling script.
2.  **Sweep**: You slowly move a lamp closer to the LDR.
3.  **Visualization**: The raw values follow a steep curve. The "Normalized" values follow a smooth, predictable line.
4.  **Result**: You have found the unique "Mathematical Fingerprint" of your specific LDR.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import math

ldr = machine.ADC(26)

# The Gamma factor for this specific LDR type
# (Determined by calibration testing)
GAMMA = 0.7 

print("RAW, NORMALIZED")

while True:
    raw = ldr.read_u16()
    
    # Normalize to 0.0 - 1.0 range
    p = raw / 65535.0
    
    # Apply Response Curve correction
    # Power function "flattens" the LDR's natural logarithmic curve
    corrected = math.pow(p, GAMMA)
    
    # Print in CSV format for Thonny's Plotter
    print(f"{p:.3f}, {corrected:.3f}")
    
    utime.sleep(0.05)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Fixed Factors**: Using the same calibration factor for different brands of LDRs. Every manufacturer uses different chemical coatings—always re-profile if you switch components!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Mult-Point Correction**: Use a lookup table (LUT) for the parts of the curve that are too complex for a single formula.

## 1. Project 1290: Advanced Advanced Light Sensing Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Optical Gesture Music Controller". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a shadow-based musical instrument that triggers different midi-style notes or drum samples based on when (and how fast) a shadow passes over multiple LDR zones.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Optical Gesture Detection**: Interpreting the *speed* and *order* of shadows to identify user intent.
*   **MIDI Emulation**: Translating light triggers into musical parameters (Pitch/Duration).
*   **Sensor Cross-talk**: Managing the interference between two sensors placed close together.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **2-4x LDRs**
*   **Piezo Buzzer**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Zone 1 (LDR)** | GP26 | Note A |
| **Zone 2 (LDR)** | GP27 | Note B |
| **Speaker** | GP14 | Sound Output |
| **OLED** | GP4, 5 | UI Feedback |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_adc`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_pwm_freq`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `threshold`: The darkness level required to trigger a note.
*   `active_note`: The frequency currently playing.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Keyboard Layout**:
    *   Place 4 LDRs in a row. These are your "Virtual Keys."
2.  **Continuous Polling**:
    *   Scan all 4 ADCs as fast as possible in a loop.
3.  **Trigger Mapping**:
    *   If LDR 1 is shadowed: `play(440Hz)`.
    *   If LDR 2 is shadowed: `play(880Hz)`.
4.  **Velocity Sensing**:
    *   Calculate the time difference between LDR 1 and LDR 2. If it's fast, play a LOUDER note.
5.  **Interface**:
    *   Show the name of the note (e.g., "C4") on the OLED when triggered.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Ready**: You hover your hand over the "Optical Keyboard."
2.  **Performance**: You "tap" the air above the sensors.
3.  **Output**: The buzzer plays distinct musical notes as your shadow hits each sensor.
4.  **Result**: A fully non-contact musical instrument.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime

# Sensors & Audio
zones = [machine.ADC(26), machine.ADC(27)]
buzz = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(14))
NOTES = [262, 330] # C, E

# Base Light Check (Calibration)
BASE = 30000 

def play_note(freq):
    buzz.freq(freq)
    buzz.duty_u16(10000)
    utime.sleep(0.1)
    buzz.duty_u16(0)

while True:
    for i in range(len(zones)):
        val = zones[i].read_u16()
        # If darker than 50% of room light
        if val < BASE // 2:
            print(f"Note {i} Triggered!")
            play_note(NOTES[i])
            # Wait for clear
            while zones[i].read_u16() < BASE // 2: utime.sleep(0.01)
    
    utime.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Shadow Ghosting**: In a room with multiple lights, you might cast multiple shadows. Use a single-point light source (like a desk lamp) for the best "piano" feel.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Air Theremin**: Use one LDR to control the PITCH and a second to control the VOLUME for a continuous, sliding sound.

## Batch 130: Advanced Temp/Humidity 3 (6-8 (Middle))

## 1. Project 1291: Advanced Temp/Humidity Sensor Setup

### 2. Learning Objective
"Low-Level Data Integrity & Checksum Validation". At the end of this project, you will be able to verify the accuracy of 40-bit digital data packets received from a DHT sensor by manually calculating and comparing a checksum to prevent ghost readings or corrupted environmental data.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Packet Anatomy**: Understanding the 40-bit structure of a DHT transmission (8 Humidity High, 8 Humidity Low, 8 Temp High, 8 Temp Low, 8 Checksum).
*   **Checksum Algorithm**: Learning the arithmetic sum logic used to validate 1-Wire communication.
*   **Bitwise Masking**: Using the `& 0xFF` operation to isolate the lower byte of an integer for comparison.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 or DHT22 Sensor**
*   **4.7k Ohm Pull-up Resistor** (if not on a breakout board)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT VCC** | 3.3V Pin | Power |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Bidirectional 1-Wire |
| **DHT GND** | GND | Common Ground |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`get_dht_raw_bits`**
*   From **Math**, drag **`bitwise_and`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_than_else`**

### 7. Variables
*   `raw_bytes`: An array/list containing the 5 bytes received from the sensor.
*   `calculated_sum`: The result of adding the first 4 bytes together.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Request Data**:
    *   Trigger the DHT sensor to send its 40-bit payload.
2.  **Calculate the Sum**:
    *   Add the four data bytes: `Sum = Byte0 + Byte1 + Byte2 + Byte3`.
3.  **Apply Byte Mask**:
    *   Since the checksum is only 8 bits, mask the total: `FinalSum = Sum & 255`.
4.  **Validation Check**:
    *   Compare `FinalSum` against `Byte4` (the sensor's own checksum).
5.  **Data Processing**:
    *   If they match, allow the temperature to be displayed. If they don't, discard the reading and retry.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Transmission**: The DHT sensor sends: `12, 0, 24, 5, 41`.
2.  **Logic**: The Pico adds `12+0+24+5 = 41`.
3.  **Comparison**: `41 == 41`. Result is **VALID**.
4.  **Result**: The screen shows "Temp: 24.5C". No corruption detected.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

sensor = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

def get_verified_reading():
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        # In MicroPython, measure() handles the checksum internally.
        # To demonstrate the logic manually:
        temp = sensor.temperature()
        hum = sensor.humidity()
        
        # Hypothetical manual verification logic:
        # data = sensor.raw_data  # Some libraries expose this
        # calculated = (data[0] + data[1] + data[2] + data[3]) & 0xFF
        # if calculated != data[4]: raise ValueError("Checksum Fail")
        
        print(f"VERIFIED DATA: {temp}C, {hum}%")
        return temp, hum
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"DATA CORRUPTED: {e}")
        return None

while True:
    get_verified_reading()
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Signal Timing**: Moving the sensor to a very long wire (3m+) increases electrical noise, which will cause frequent Checksum Failures. Use a stronger pull-up resistor to compensate.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Error Logger**: Count how many checksum errors occur per hour to determine the "Health" of your connection.

## 1. Project 1292: Advanced Temp/Humidity Data Reader

### 2. Learning Objective
"Dual-Unit HMI Dashboard". At the end of this project, you will be able to build an interactive dashboard on an OLED screen that allows the user to toggle between Celsius and Fahrenheit units using a hardware button, featuring a smooth-scrolling 10-point temperature history graph.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HMI (Human Machine Interface)**: Designing displays that allow users to interact with and change the presentation of data.
*   **State Machines**: Using a variable (`unit_mode`) to track which unit system the display should use.
*   **Data Visualization**: Drawing a basic "Sparkline" graph on a pixel display to show trends over time.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **I2C OLED Display (SSD1306)**
*   **Push Button**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Climate Input |
| **Unit Toggle** | GP14 | Button (Pull-up) |
| **OLED SDA/SCL**| GP4, 5 | UI Display |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`equation_cf`**
*   From **Smart Display**, drag **`draw_line`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `history`: A list of the last 10 temperature readings.
*   `is_fahrenheit`: Boolean flag for unit state.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Dashboard Layout**:
    *   Row 1: Title and current Unit.
    *   Row 2: Large temperature number.
    *   Row 3: Mini-graph (History).
2.  **Toggle Logic**:
    *   Wait for the button at GP14. When pressed, flip `is_fahrenheit` from True to False.
3.  **Measurement Loop**:
    *   Every 1 second, read the DHT. Store the value in a list (maximum 10 items).
4.  **Graphing**:
    *   Iterate through the `history` list and draw small vertical lines on the OLED to create a trend chart.
5.  **Rendering**:
    *   Clear and update the screen. If `is_fahrenheit` is True, apply the conversion before printing.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: OLED shows "22.5 C" and a flat line graph.
2.  **Interaction**: You press the button.
3.  **Change**: The text instantly updates to "72.5 F".
4.  **Trend**: You hold the sensor; the line graph begins to curve upwards as it gets warmer.
5.  **Result**: A professional-grade handheld environmental dashboard.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, dht
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

history = []
f_mode = False

while True:
    try:
        sens.measure()
        t = sens.temperature()
        history.append(t)
        if len(history) > 10: history.pop(0)
        
        if btn.value() == 0:
            f_mode = not f_mode
            utime.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
            
        oled.fill(0)
        disp_t = (t * 1.8) + 32 if f_mode else t
        unit = "F" if f_mode else "C"
        
        oled.text("ENV DASHBOARD", 10, 0)
        oled.text(f"{disp_t:.1f} {unit}", 40, 25)
        
        # Draw History Graph
        for i, val in enumerate(history):
            h = int((val / 40) * 20) # Scale to 20 pixels
            oled.fill_rect(i*12, 60-h, 8, h, 1)
            
        oled.show()
    except: pass
    utime.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Graph Overlap**: If your history list gets too long, the graph will draw off the edge of the screen. Always use `pop(0)` or a slice to keep the list size fixed.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual View**: Use the top half for Temperature and the bottom half for a Humidity graph.

## 1. Project 1293: Advanced Temp/Humidity Threshold Logic

### 2. Learning Objective
"Data Center Thermal Interlock". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement a multi-stage thermal safety system that activates an intake fan at 28°C, sounds a local alarm at 35°C, and triggers a simulated "Remote Shutdown" if the rate of temperature rise exceeds 1°C per minute.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multi-Stage Fault Logic**: Using tiered thresholds to trigger different levels of response.
*   **Rate-of-Change Detection**: Monitoring how *fast* a problem is getting worse (d/dt).
*   **Emergency Interlocks**: Safeguarding expensive hardware through automated logic.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11/22 Sensor**
*   **5V Fan** (or Motor)
*   **Passive Buzzer**
*   **Transistor/N-MOSFET** (for Fan control)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Sensor** | GP15 | Thermal Node |
| **Cooling Fan** | GP14 | Stage 1 (Cooling) |
| **Warning Buzz**| GP13 | Stage 2 (Alarm) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_else_multi`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_output`**

### 7. Variables
*   `temp_now`: The current reading.
*   `fan_active`: State of the cooling system.
*   `rate_trigger`: Alert if temp rises > 1 degree in a minute.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define the Tiers**:
    *   Stage 1 (28C): Turn on Fan. Keep it on until it hits 26C (Hysteresis).
    *   Stage 2 (35C): Sound intermittent alarm beep.
    *   Stage 3 (High Rate): If `Temp - LastTemp > 1.0`, trigger "Emergency Shutdown" message.
2.  **Safety First**:
    *   The Alarm should be louder and faster than the Fan's vibration noise.
3.  **Poll Rate**:
    *   Read the sensor every 5 seconds to detect rapid thermal runaway.
4.  **Logging**:
    *   Print the "Reason" for any action (e.g., "FAN START: OVER TEMP").

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Cool**: Server is at 24C. Fan is OFF.
2.  **Load**: High traffic raises temp to 29C. Fan spins up.
3.  **Failure**: Fan fails or air-con breaks. Temp hits 35C.
4.  **Result**: The buzzer starts beeping urgently. The Pico prints "CRITICAL ERROR: MANUAL INTERVENTION REQUIRED."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, dht

sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
fan = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.OUT)
buzz = machine.PWM(machine.Pin(13))

last_t = 0

while True:
    try:
        sens.measure()
        t = sens.temperature()
        
        # Stage 1: Active Cooling
        if t > 28: fan.on()
        elif t < 26: fan.off()
        
        # Stage 2: Audible Alert
        if t > 35:
            buzz.freq(2000); buzz.duty_u16(30000)
            utime.sleep(0.1); buzz.duty_u16(0)
            
        # Stage 3: Rate check
        if last_t != 0 and (t - last_t) >= 1.0:
            print("!!! EMERGENCY SHUTDOWN: RAPID HEAT RISE !!!")
            
        last_t = t
        print(f"Server Node Temp: {t}C")
        
    except: pass
    utime.sleep(5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Sensor Noise**: A 1.0 degree jump might sometimes be a sensor error. In a real system, you'd confirm the high rate over several readings before shutting down.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Web Console**: Use a Pico W to send a "Slack" or "Discord" notification when Stage 2 is reached.

## 1. Project 1294: Advanced Temp/Humidity Status Monitor

### 2. Learning Objective
"Statistical Climate Monitor". At the end of this project, you will be able to create a professional monitoring station that calculates and displays the 24-hour "Min/Max/Mean" for both temperature and humidity, using persistent "Peak-Hold" indicators on an OLED display.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Aggregate Data**: Calculating an "Averaging Window" to smooth out noise.
*   **Peak-Hold Logic**: Saving extreme values (Record Highs/Lows) in memory.
*   **Data Integrity**: Handling "Nan" or "Error" readings without ruining the average.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11/22 Sensor**
*   **OLED Display**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Sensor |
| **OLED** | GP4, 5 | Dashboard |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`set_max`**, **`set_min`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_than`**

### 7. Variables
*   `mx_t, mn_t`: Maximum and Minimum recorded Temperates.
*   `sum_t, count`: Used to find the Mean over time.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Initialization**:
    *   Set `mx_t = -100` and `mn_t = 100` initially.
2.  **Poll Cycle**:
    *   Read the sensor every 30 seconds.
3.  **Logic Logic**:
    *   If `current > mx_t`, then `mx_t = current`.
    *   If `current < mn_t`, then `mn_t = current`.
4.  **Math**:
    *   Add `current` to `sum_t`. Increment `count`. `Mean = sum_t / count`.
5.  **Output**:
    *   Show "NOW / HI / LO" on the screen for a complete summary.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Boot**: The Pico sees 22C. All values (Hi/Lo/Mean) show 22.
2.  **Noon**: Temp hits 28C. Hi = 28.
3.  **Night**: Temp drops to 15C. Lo = 15.
4.  **Result**: By looking at the screen, you know exactly what the "Range" of your environment was.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, utime, dht
from ssd1306 import SSD1306_I2C

i2c = machine.I2C(0, sda=machine.Pin(4), scl=machine.Pin(5))
oled = SSD1306_I2C(128, 64, i2c)
sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

hi_t = -100; lo_t = 100

while True:
    try:
        sens.measure()
        t = sens.temperature()
        
        if t > hi_t: hi_t = t
        if t < lo_t: lo_t = t
        
        oled.fill(0)
        oled.text(f"NOW: {t}C", 10, 0)
        oled.text(f"HIGH: {hi_t}C", 10, 20)
        oled.text(f"LOW: {lo_t}C", 10, 40)
        oled.show()
    except: pass
    utime.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Startup Bias**: If you don't take your first reading quickly, the `lo_t = 100` might stay 100 if the actual room temp is lower than the initialization value. Ensure the first valid reading sets both variables.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Reset**: Reset the statistics every 24 hours (or 100 readings) to prevent old data from skewing the Mean.

## 1. Project 1295: Automated Advanced Temp/Humidity Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"Precision Food-Curing Automation". At the end of this project, you will be able to program an automated curing environment that executes a 20-day "Dehydration Profile," decreasing the target temperature by 1°C/day while maintaining a constant 80% humidity using a closed-loop relay system.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time-Based Profiling**: Changing system setpoints over long durations (days/weeks).
*   **Closed-Loop Climate Control**: Using real-time feedback to maintain a strict environmental window.
*   **Relay Logic**: Controlling high-power loads (Heaters/Humidifiers) safely.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT22 Sensor**
*   **2x Relay Modules**
*   **Heater & Humidifier**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Heater Relay** | GP16 | Warmth Control |
| **Mister Relay** | GP17 | Humidity Control |
| **DHT Data** | GP15 | Sensor |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait_days`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_else`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_output`**

### 7. Variables
*   `days_elapsed`: Tracking the duration of the curing cycle.
*   `target_temp`: A value that decreases every 24 hours.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Define the Start**:
    *   Begin with a target of 18°C and 80% Humidity.
2.  **The Cooling Ramp**:
    *   Every 24 hours (86,400 seconds), decrease `target_temp` by 1.
3.  **Climate Regulation**:
    *   If `Temp > target_temp`: Turn Heater OFF.
    *   If `Temp < target_temp`: Turn Heater ON.
4.  **Moisture Lock**:
    *   If `Humidity < 80%`: Turn Humidifier ON.
    *   If `Humidity > 82%`: Turn Humidifier OFF.
5.  **Logging**:
    *   Log the current Day and Temperature to the console for tracking.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Day 1**: Chamber is at 18°C.
2.  **Day 5**: The Pico has automatically lowered the target to 14°C.
3.  **Action**: The heater relay clicks only when needed to keep the new, lower temperature.
4.  **Result**: The product cures perfectly according to the scientific profile.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht

sensor = dht.DHT22(machine.Pin(15))
heater = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)
mister = machine.Pin(17, machine.Pin.OUT)

target_t = 18.0
target_h = 80.0
start_time = utime.time()

while True:
    elapsed_days = (utime.time() - start_time) // 86400
    current_target = target_t - elapsed_days
    
    try:
        sensor.measure()
        t = sensor.temperature()
        h = sensor.humidity()
        
        # Temp Control
        if t < current_target - 0.5: heater.on()
        elif t > current_target + 0.5: heater.off()
        
        # Humidity Control
        if h < target_h: mister.on()
        elif h > target_h + 2: mister.off()
        
        print(f"Day {elapsed_days+1} | Target: {current_target}C | Now: {t}C")
        
    except: pass
    utime.sleep(60)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Time Overflow**: On some systems, `utime.time()` might reset after a few weeks. For a 20-day project, ensure your timing logic can handle the duration.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Data Recovery**: Save the `elapsed_days` to a file every hour so the curing cycle can resume if there is a power cut.

## 1. Project 1296: Advanced Temp/Humidity Safety Trigger

### 2. Learning Objective
"Redundant Thermal Safety Logic". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a fail-safe sensing system that monitors for "Sensor Death." If the digital DHT11 fails to report data or fails its checksum, the system automatically hot-swaps to a secondary analog Thermistor to maintain core safety operations.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Fault Tolerance**: Using two different sensor types to ensure the system never goes "blind."
*   **Hot-Swapping**: Automatically redirecting logic from one data source to another based on reliability.
*   **Validation Checking**: Detecting if a sensor is disconnected or sending garbage data.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor (Primary)**
*   **10k NTC Thermistor (Backup)**
*   **10k Ohm Resistor**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT11** | GP15 | Digital Input |
| **Thermistor** | GP26 | Analog Backup |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_try_except`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_ntc_temp`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_dht_temp`**

### 7. Variables
*   `safe_temp`: The current trusted temperature.
*   `is_faulty`: Boolean flag if the DHT fails.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Dual Reading**:
    *   Attempt to read the DHT11 inside a `try` block.
2.  **The Failover**:
    *   If the `try` fails (Error), read the Analog Thermistor instead.
3.  **Conversion**:
    *   Convert the Thermistor's ADC value into Celsius using the Steinhart-Hart equation.
4.  **UI Feedback**:
    *   If using the backup, print "WARNING: SENSOR 1 FAULT - USING BACKUP."
5.  **Steady State**:
    *   Always output the temperature from the most reliable source available.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Healthy**: Both sensors are connected. The Pico displays DHT11 data.
2.  **Failure**: You pull the wire out of the DHT11.
3.  **Logic**: The `sensor.measure()` call throws an error. The Pico catches it.
4.  **Result**: The system immediately switches to the Thermistor. The temperature update never stops.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import utime
import dht
import math

dht_sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
ntc = machine.ADC(26)

def get_ntc_temp():
    val = ntc.read_u16()
    # Simplified NTC conversion
    res = 10000 / (65535 / val - 1)
    temp = 1 / (1/298.15 + (1/3950) * math.log(res/10000)) - 273.15
    return temp

while True:
    try:
        dht_sens.measure()
        temp = dht_sens.temperature()
        print(f"PRIMARY: {temp}C")
    except:
        temp = get_ntc_temp()
        print(f"BACKUP ENGAGED: {temp:.1f}C")
        
    utime.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Calibration Mismatch**: Ensure your NTC conversion is calibrated to match the DHT11's readings, otherwise the temperature will "jump" by several degrees during a failover.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Self-Healing**: Add an LED that stays red as long as the primary sensor is dead, prompting a repair.

## 1. Project 1297: Advanced Temp/Humidity Data Logger

### 2. Learning Objective
"FAT32 Climate Forensic Logger". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an industrial-style data logger that records timestamped climate data directly to an SD Card in CSV format, creating a new "Daily Log" file automatically every midnight for easy Excel analysis.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **SD Card SPI Communication**: Interfacing with removable storage.
*   **Log Management**: Creating separate files for each day to prevent massive, unmanageable datasets.
*   **File Buffering**: Ensuring data is saved even if power is lost.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **MicroSD Card Module**
*   **DS3231 RTC Module** (for dates)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **SD CS/MOSI/MISO** | GP13, 11, 12 | SPI Storage |
| **RTC SDA/SCL**| GP4, 5 | I2C Timer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`pico_sd_mount`**
*   From **System & Storage**, drag **`write_log`**
*   From **Smart Sensors**, drag **`read_dht_all`**

### 7. Variables
*   `filename`: Formatted as "LOG_YYYY_MM_DD.csv".
*   `log_data`: The string containing time, temp, and hum.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Mount Storage**:
    *   Initialize SPI and mount the SD card to the Pico's file system.
2.  **Date Check**:
    *   Get the current date from the RTC. Use it to name the file.
3.  **Data Pulse**:
    *   Read the DHT every 10 minutes.
4.  **The Write**:
    *   Open the file in "Append" mode. Write one line of data. Close the file.
5.  **Midnight Flip**:
    *   If the clock hits 00:00:00, stop writing to the old file and create a new one for the next day.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico mounts the SD card.
2.  **Logging**: It writes "12:00, 22C, 40%" to `LOG_OCT_12.csv`.
3.  **Transition**: At midnight, it seamlessly begins writing to `LOG_OCT_13.csv`.
4.  **Result**: You have organized, uncorrupted daily climate reports.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import os
import utime
import dht

# Mock SD Setup (Requires sdcard.py library)
# spi = machine.SPI(1, sck=Pin(10), mosi=Pin(11), miso=Pin(12))
# sd = sdcard.SDCard(spi, Pin(13))
# os.mount(sd, "/sd")

sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

def log_to_sd(data):
    try:
        with open("/sd/climate.csv", "a") as f:
            f.write(data + "\n")
        print("Data Saved to SD")
    except:
        print("SD Error")

while True:
    sens.measure()
    entry = f"{utime.time()},{sens.temperature()},{sens.humidity()}"
    log_to_sd(entry)
    utime.sleep(600)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Power Pulling**: Never pull the SD card while a write is in progress! Always add a "Safe to Eject" button or LED.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Binary Logging**: Save data as binary instead of text to save space for multi-year logging projects.

## 1. Project 1298: Smart Advanced Temp/Humidity System

"MQTT Smart Home Bridge". (Pico W). At the end of this project, you will be able to connect the Pico W to a private MQTT Broker and publish temperature/humidity data using the "Home Assistant Discovery" protocol, allowing the sensor to appear automatically in a mobile dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced

*   **JSON Serialization**: Sending complex data packets (sensor name, unit, and value) in a single string.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

2.  **Configuration Topic**:

3.  **State Updates**:

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import utime
import dht
from machine import Pin
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient

client = MQTTClient("pico_bridge", "192.168.1.50")
client.connect()

sens = dht.DHT11(Pin(15))

while True:
    sens.measure()
    t, h = sens.temperature(), sens.humidity()
    # Simple publish
    client.publish("home/pico/climate", f"{t},{h}")
    utime.sleep(30)
```

*   **LWT (Last Will and Testament)**: Configure MQTT to notify you if the Pico loses power or goes offline.
