## 1. Project 1299: Advanced Temp/Humidity Calibration Tool

### 2. Learning Objective
"Non-Linear Error Compensation Tool". At the end of this project, you will be able to build a calibration utility that calculates a "Linear Regression" correction factor (`y = mx + b`) for your specific DHT sensor by comparing its output against a professional reference thermometer at high and low points.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Linear Regression**: Understanding the mathematical relationship between a measured value and the true value.
*   **Correction Coefficients**: Learning how to apply "Slope" (m) and "Offset" (b) to raw sensor data.
*   **Software Debugging**: Using math to fix hardware inaccuracies.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **Professional Reference Thermometer**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **DHT11** | GP15 | Calibration Subject |
| **Ref Probe** | HANDHELD | Gold Standard |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Math**, drag **`equation_linear`**
*   From **Wait**, drag **`pico_wait`**

### 7. Variables
*   `correction_slope`: The "m" in y=mx+b.
*   `correction_offset`: The "b" in y=mx+b.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Test Point 1 (Ice)**:
    *   Measure a cold room. Compare DHT (18.1C) to Reference (18.0C). 
2.  **Test Point 2 (Warm)**:
    *   Measure a warm room. Compare DHT (30.5C) to Reference (30.0C).
3.  **Calculate the Bias**:
    *   Notice that as it gets hotter, the error gets larger (it's not just a simple offset).
4.  **Apply the Formula**:
    *   Calculate the slope required to pull both points into alignment.
5.  **The Corrector**:
    *   Build a function that automatically applies this math to every future sensor reading.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Baseline**: Sensor reads 22.0C. Ref says 21.8C.
2.  **Calibration**: You calculate the `m` and `b` factors.
3.  **Result**: The Pico now outputs 21.8C, perfectly matching the gold standard.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, dht, utime

# Calibration Factors (Calculated from test)
M = 0.985
B = -0.05

sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))

while True:
    sens.measure()
    raw = sens.temperature()
    # Apply Linear Regression
    calibrated = (M * raw) + B
    print(f"RAW: {raw} | CALIBRATED: {calibrated:.2f}")
    utime.sleep(2)
```

## 1. Project 1300: Advanced Advanced Temp/Humidity Prototype

### 2. Learning Objective
"Feed-Forward Climate Predictor". At the end of this project, you will be able to implement an "Anticipatory Control" system that uses an RTC to track the time of day. If the temperature is rising AND it is after 8:00 AM, the system triggers cooling *before* the comfort threshold is reached to maintain a stable environment.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Anticipatory (Feed-Forward) Control**: Making decisions based on predicted future states rather than waiting for a limit to be hit.
*   **Diurnal Cycle Monitoring**: Using time-of-day data to inform climate logic.
*   **RTC/Sensor Fusion**: Combining absolute time and environmental data for smarter automation.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   **Raspberry Pi Pico**
*   **DHT11 Sensor**
*   **DS3231 RTC Module**
*   **Active Cooling Relay**

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay** | GP16 | Fan Motor |
| **RTC SDA/SCL** | GP4, 5 | Clock Link |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   From **Time**, drag **`read_rtc_hour`**
*   From **Logic**, drag **`if_and`**
*   From **Smart IO**, drag **`set_digital_pin`**

### 7. Variables
*   `is_morning`: True if between 08:00 and 12:00.
*   `temp_trend`: Is the room getting warmer or colder?

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide
1.  **Time Awareness**:
    *   Continuously check the RTC. We only care about the morning sun ramp.
2.  **Trend Detection**:
    *   Compare the last 5 minutes of data. If it's rising, `temp_trend = "UP"`.
3.  **Predictive Action**:
    *   `If hour >= 8 AND temp_trend == "UP"`: START COOLING NOW.
4.  **Result**:
    *   By cooling the room early, the peak afternoon heat never gets high enough to be uncomfortable.
5.  **Efficiency**:
    *   The fan runs less overall than it would if it had to fight 30C heat later in the day.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico checks RTC; it is 09:15.
2.  **Analysis**: The temp is 24.5C and rising (Trend: UP).
3.  **Action**: The Pico clicks the fan ON early.
4.  **Observation**: The room stays at 25C all day even though the sun is out.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine, dht, utime

# Mock Time/Hardware
sens = dht.DHT11(machine.Pin(15))
fan = machine.Pin(16, machine.Pin.OUT)

last_t = 0

while True:
    try:
        sens.measure()
        t = sens.temperature()
        
        # In a real system, you'd check a DS3231 RTC here
        # For demo, we assume it's morning (9 AM)
        hour = 9 
        
        is_rising = t > last_t if last_t != 0 else False
        
        # PREDICTIVE: If morning AND temp is starting to rise, cool early
        if hour >= 8 and hour < 12 and is_rising and t > 24:
            fan.on()
            print("MORNING RAMP DETECTED - COOLING EARLY")
        elif t < 23:
            fan.off()
            
        last_t = t
    except: pass
    utime.sleep(60)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Thermal Inertia**: Remember that fans don't cool air instantly. Feed-forward control works best when you start it *before* the heat peak occurs (usually between 10 AM and 2 PM).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Seasonal Profiles**: Add a "Season" variable. In summer, start cooling at 8 AM; in winter, you might not need the predictor at all!
